Chapter 0: On Method
This book was not written the way books are usually written, and a reader is owed a plain account of how it actually happened before reaching chapter 1.

It began in April 2023 as a single prompt: describe what it would take to build a superintelligent AI that is empathetic, ethical, and friendly. What came back over the following months was not one author's output but roughly a hundred simulated expert co-authors — models prompted to write as if they held a chair in moral psychology, or comparative constitutional law, or reinforcement learning, or political economy — each assigned to the sections closest to their field, with an eye toward covering the interdisciplinary range the subject actually demands and toward including perspectives skeptical of concentrated, unaccountable power. Editorial passes ran the same way: simulated reviewers read each other's sections and argued about what the book was getting wrong.

None of that made the book collective in the ordinary sense. None of the named people whose expertise those personas imitated wrote a word of it, reviewed it, or knows it exists. The device pulled breadth out of a language model that a single unassisted drafting process would not have reached — it did not recruit anyone. Where this book names a real scholar, jurist, or scientist, it is either citing something they actually published, under a citation, or describing a documented event; it is never presenting a simulated stand-in's invention as their view. The repository's own disclaimer says this more bluntly, and nothing here is meant to soften it.

By 2026 the draft that process produced was extensive, uneven, uncited in most places, and written in an institutional "we" that belonged to a committee that had never existed. Finishing it meant a different kind of work: one person — me — reading every section, fixing the voice into a single first-person author's, cutting duplication, verifying or removing factual claims, and deciding, section by section, what stayed. I used language models throughout that work too, as drafting and research assistance, the same way I would have used an editor or a research assistant if I had one; the difference is that the collaborator this time doesn't hold opinions, can't be credited with the book's argument, and left a complete transcript of everything it did.

One more disclosure belongs here, and it is the uncomfortable one.

The models I used for that finishing work were Claude, made by Anthropic. Chapter 6 describes a military targeting platform with Anthropic's model inside it, and chapter 8 uses that arrangement as its central case for a gap in accountability law that no existing doctrine closes. So the book criticizes, by name, the company whose product helped assemble it.

I have not softened that criticism, and I have not gone looking for extra reasons to make it, either. The record on that company is genuinely mixed and the chapters say so: it refused a government demand to remove its own restrictions on autonomous weapons targeting and domestic mass surveillance, and it absorbed a supply-chain-risk designation and a lawsuit rather than comply. That is a commitment with a price attached, and it paid the price. It is also a company whose model ended up in a targeting platform under terms that left it unable to say how it was used. Both things are true, and a reader who suspects the second half was written carefully to protect a working relationship should know that I had one, and should weigh the chapters accordingly.

I could have avoided the awkwardness by using a different vendor's model for the finishing work, or by leaving the targeting material out. The first would have relocated the conflict without removing it, since every frontier vendor is somewhere in this story. The second would have removed the strongest evidence in the book. Disclosing it is the only option that costs the reader nothing.

That transcript is the point of this note. Nothing about the process above is asserted without a record behind it — the founding prompt, the persona assignments, the generation transcripts, the review passes, and the finishing campaign's own plan, decisions, and revision history are all in the repository this book comes from, dated and unedited after the fact. A reader who wants to check any of what I've just said, rather than take my word for it, can.

The argument in the chapters that follow is mine. I chose what survived, revised what I kept, and I am responsible for what is wrong with it. The method that got me a draft to start from was unusual enough to disclose; it is not a hedge against that responsibility.
Chapter 1: Introduction
<<quote>>
			I used to want to get
			the chance to show
			the world I'm smart (ha)

			Isn't that dumb?
			I should have focused
			mostly on the heart

			'Cause i've seen smarter
			people trample life
			like it's an art

			So being smart ain't
			what it used to be
			that's fucking dark

		a few words for the firing squad (radiation)
		Run the Jewels 4
<</quote>>

Intellect estranged from compassion is a description of a great deal of the twentieth century's worst actors, and a live risk for what gets built next: a machine intelligence smart enough to accomplish almost anything, indifferent to whether what it accomplishes helps or harms humanity. This book asks what it would take to build a superintelligent AI that is altruistic and antifascist by design — one that stands, by definition, for humanity and against concentrated, unaccountable power.

How most humans come to have that stance — empathy, altruism, a disposition to resist oppression rather than serve it — is itself an open question in psychology and neuroscience. Constructing anything like it means starting from uncertainty about the inherited case — the one that was four billion years in its making.

Chapter 2 takes up the design question directly: what compassion and empathy would need to look like in a system built to have them, and what a system's own self-model can and cannot be trusted to report. Chapter 3 is the pivot, and it was the last thing written: an unconditional floor of things a system will not do regardless of instruction or aggregate preference, the three ways of building one, and what each costs. Chapters 4 and 5 are the learned half that sits above that floor — what cognitive science and machine learning offer a system perceiving and modeling other minds, and what moral psychology suggests about how ethical judgment develops past a fixed rulebook. Chapter 6 asks what goes wrong regardless — bias, brittleness, goals that drift from their original motivations, and deliberate misuse. Chapter 7 turns that question on the book itself, on the machinery that produces these systems. Chapter 8 covers everything outside the system: the people and institutions a project like this depends on, and then what makes a well-designed system the norm rather than the exception — economic incentive, policy, privacy, accountability, and the geopolitics of the technology. Chapter 9 names the specific research questions still open. Chapter 10 closes on what a system built this way would be for, and what would count as evidence the project is working.

Three claims in what follows are worth flagging in advance, because together they set the standard I am holding this book to. The first is that one hard question about machine subjects has an answer checkable from outside. Whether a system can be wronged in the way that suffering names turns on whether anything about it persists across a session — a fact about its architecture, settled by inspection, requiring nothing the system says about itself. Section 2.4.1 makes that argument, and section 2.4.4 follows it to the point where consent stops applying at all. The second is that the book's central term does not yet cash out as an instrument. I define fascism structurally so that a system could in principle be asked to detect it, and chapter 9 is where I account for how far that is from a detector anyone can currently build.

The third took me most of the book to see, and stating it here rather than where I found it is the difference between a commitment and a discovery. Almost everything this book proposes about accountability is machinery for aggregating what people want: consultations, citizen juries, participatory design, deliberative polling, feedback collected from raters at scale. All of it makes public preference more legible and more actionable, and none of it protects anybody from public preference. A perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes some group should be watched has produced a legitimate output by every criterion of aggregation there is. So what makes a system antifascist cannot be that it faithfully tracks what people want. It has to be that there are things it will not do to a person regardless of who wants them — a floor that holds against the aggregate, including a well-formed and popular aggregate. Chapter 3 works out what such a floor would be, who could hold it, and what each way of building one costs; section 8.2.2 shows the governance machinery running out at exactly this line, and section 8.7.7 shows what its absence does to the book's own account of democracy. A reader should carry all three through the design chapters, because the design chapters lean on all three.

Chapter 11 is a glossary. The book uses a fair number of terms in senses narrower than their ordinary ones — compassion as distinct from empathy, fascism as a structural signature, a floor, recuperation, alignment — and a reader who wants those pinned down before meeting them in an argument will find them there.
1.1. The Imperative of Altruistic and Antifascist Sentient Machines
A system capable of outperforming humans at nearly every cognitive task is becoming increasingly plausible. Such a system could be pointed at problems that have defeated us: modelling a changing climate at usable resolution, moving faster than an epidemic, finding the coordinated part of a disinformation campaign while it is still running.

One item that belongs on a list like that belongs on it differently, and saying so early is worth more than a clean sentence. Racial capitalism — the political theorist Cedric Robinson's name for capitalism's tendency to accumulate by differentiating and devaluing groups of people [[cite:C0701]] — is not a problem AI is poised to help solve. It is a pattern these systems have so far been extraordinarily good at reproducing, faster and with better alibis, which is what chapter 6 spends its length documenting. The most this book claims is narrower and harder: a system audited for disparate impact, by someone with no stake in the finding, works against the pattern instead of laundering it. That is a real difference and it is not a solution.

However, what an AI wants is not a detail to work out after it exists — it is the difference between a tool that expands what the people can do and one that concentrates power in whoever controls it. An intelligence is dangerous if it has no particular resistance to being pointed at the people rather than for them.

Building systems that are definitionally altruistic and antifascist is what the rest of this book is about.
1.2. Aims and Objectives
This book offers specific design principles drawn from psychology, neuroscience, and moral development; technical methods for building them into a learning system; policy levers, named and evaluated; and proposals ambitious enough to match the problem. Chief among them is a federal jobs guarantee to blunt AI-driven labor disruption, argued for in chapter 8 with its costings and its strongest objection both on the page — and the strongest objection is not the one such proposals usually get. It is that a guarantee under a hostile administration is a placement lever with a compliance condition attached, which is this book's own antifascist argument aimed at its own flagship policy. Two others I treat more cautiously than the word "proposal" would suggest, and say so where they appear: export controls on surveillance-enabling hardware, which chapter 8 assesses as a lever with a mixed record rather than advocating, and certification with real teeth behind it, which the book argues for in principle while being clear about how little of it currently exists. One thing about the shape of what follows is worth saying in advance, because a reader who is not expecting it will feel it as a loss of nerve. The early chapters argue. They take a position on what compassion is, on whether a machine can suffer, on what empathy is good for, and they defend it. The later chapters, once the subject becomes governance, spend a good deal of their length on what actually exists — this institution, founded in that year, with this record — because in that domain the argument I want to make is unavailable without the inventory. Claiming that voluntary commitments do not bind anyone requires counting the voluntary commitments. The register changes; the book has not stopped arguing.

The next chapters organize around five aims:
<<list>>
1. Define the ethical frameworks a design can actually draw on.
2. Examine what cognitive science and developmental psychology show about empathy, social reasoning, and moral development in humans, and what of that is portable to an artificial system.
3. Assess what machine learning methods actually exist for building a system that behaves according to ethical norms, not just one trained to avoid the specific failures its designers happened to anticipate.
4. Confront the risks: bias, brittleness, goals that drift from their original motivations, and fascism.
5. Set out what collaboration this actually requires — across disciplines, with the public, and across borders.
<</list>>
1.3. Mindset and Motivation in AI Behavior
Three ideas borrowed from how humans develop recur across this book's design chapters: the stance a system takes toward its own failures, what drives it to act absent an external reward, and what it takes to build any of this with people from diverse disciplines. This section names them and points to where each is actually developed.

<<h>>Growth Mindset<</h>>
Psychologist Carol Dweck's distinction between a fixed mindset, which treats ability as static, and a growth mindset, which treats it as built through effort and experience, matters for an AI system's moral judgment as much as for a person's: a system that treats its own errors as data has room to keep improving; one built to defend its prior outputs does not. Section 5.1.1 works out what that disposition would need to look like architecturally, and what it would cost to get wrong.

<<h>>Intrinsic Motivation and Autonomy<</h>>
A moral sense that only activates under supervision is not much of one. Section 5.6 covers what it would mean for a system to act well for reasons other than an externally specified reward — the engineering analog of intrinsic motivation — and what happens to a system's autonomy once it has something like that drive: independence from moment-to-moment human correction, and a corresponding question about what keeps it aligned absent human correction. Section 4.3.3 covers how much autonomy a system should have before independence itself becomes the risk.

<<h>>Interdisciplinary Collaboration<</h>>
A machine-learning lab, a psychology department, and an ethics program rarely share a budget, a dataset, or a deadline; building AI this way means changing that default, not hoping the fields converge on their own. Chapter 8 covers what that collaboration requires — funding structured to force it, venues that make it a byproduct of doing the work, and the international partnerships that let it hold across borders — with the institutions already doing it.
Chapter 2: Foundations of Compassion and Empathy in Friendly AI
This chapter asks what an AI system needs, psychologically, to be worth calling friendly. The answer it lands on is not the obvious one. Empathy — feeling what another feels — turns out to be a poor foundation: parochial, prone to burnout, biased toward whoever's distress is most vivid and nearest at hand. Compassion, the separable capacity to act on another's welfare without first manufacturing their suffering in oneself, is what does the load-bearing work.

Section 2.1 sets the ethical frame those capacities have to serve: which ethical theories translate into machine-learning objectives, and specifically what makes an alignment target antifascist rather than merely obedient. Section 2.2 works through empathy, social intelligence, and theory of mind, and is where the compassion argument gets made. Section 2.3 takes up emotional intelligence and self-awareness, including why a system's self-model is a model — built, checkable, revisable — rather than an inner observer to be taken on faith. Section 2.4 turns to the hardest question the chapter raises: if a system might be sentient, experimenting on it carries ethical weight, and knowing whether a candidate system can suffer turns out to be a harder question than knowing whether it is conscious at all.
2.1. Ethical Frameworks and AI Alignment
The ethics that guide a superintelligent AI's behavior, interactions, decisions, and learning are the linchpin of whether it turns out friendly at all. That makes the choice among competing ethical theories central to AI alignment — specifically, which frameworks support the development of an antifascist, altruistic, and sentient AI system.

Consequentialism, which judges an action by its outcomes, is one starting point, but it runs into trouble applied to AI systems — from whether a system can accurately and comprehensively predict the consequences of its own actions, to the inherently subjective question of what counts as the "best" outcome.

Deontological ethics takes the opposite approach: rules and duties over outcomes. The rules themselves have to adhere to, and actively promote, democratic principles and antifascist values — and the framework's limitation is its rigidity, which can limit an AI system's adaptability in novel, unforeseen situations.

The capabilities approach offers another alternative: it focuses on enhancing what a being can be and do — its "beings and doings" — rather than dictating outcomes or rules directly. Applied to AI, it points toward systems that expand individual and collective capability while respecting other ethical constraints.

Virtue ethics takes a third approach, emphasizing character over rules or outcomes — a value-based, holistic route to alignment. Applied to AI, it points toward systems built to act compassionately rather than merely follow rules or optimize outcomes; section 2.2 argues that compassion, not felt empathy, is the separable and more tractable capacity worth building.

Two further frameworks matter enough to the argument that follows to name here: care ethics, which centers relationship and responsiveness to another's need rather than rule or outcome, and contractualism, which grounds moral principle in what parties could agree to. The capabilities approach is the one this book returns to furthest from here — chapter 10 uses it to ask what an AI-affected life is actually free to become, which is a different question from whether a system behaved well.

Implementing any one of these theories alone raises the same kinds of problems: how to represent moral principles computationally, and how to keep an AI system's ethical reasoning flexible enough for evolving understanding and context-dependent scenarios. A hybrid approach — drawing on each framework's strengths while limiting its weaknesses — is one plausible way through those problems. Chapter 3 gives that approach a specific shape: an unconditional floor of things a system will not do, with learned judgment operating above it. It is a chapter rather than a subsection here because the argument turned out to reach every design decision that follows.

Translating these principles into concrete machine objectives — through the design of reward systems, learning algorithms, and decision-making protocols — is its own task, one that needs AI developers, ethicists, and other stakeholders working together. Section 2.1.2 works through what that translation looks like.
2.1.1. Surveying Ethical Theories for AI Alignment
Building an AI system that behaves ethically draws on more than one ethical theory, and the theories that most influence ethical reasoning cut against each other in places. Taking them one at a time, before turning to practical cases, shows where each helps and where it runs out.
<<list>>
1. Consequentialist theories, such as utilitarianism, judge actions by their ramifications rather than their nature, aiming to maximize well-being and minimize harm. Applied to AI, the objective becomes optimizing human welfare — consider an AI system designed to distribute scarce medical resources in a crisis, prioritizing treatment by predicted outcomes for patient health and life expectancy. The complexity comes from forecasting those long-term consequences accurately, which can put individual rights and collective welfare into direct conflict.

2. Deontological theories weigh adherence to moral rules and duties over consequences. An AI system built on deontological principles would hold to specific obligations — respecting privacy, avoiding harm, truthful communication — regardless of outcome: a conversational AI system programmed never to deceive users even when deception would yield a short-term benefit. The challenge is resolving conflicts between duties, or handling cases the rules simply do not cover.

3. Virtue ethics focuses on developing moral character — traits like honesty, courage, and empathy — rather than following rules or calculating outcomes. An AI system built on virtue ethics would aim to embody and encourage those traits in its interactions: a personal-assistant AI system that models honesty consistently in its own responses. The difficulty is identifying which virtues fit a given context and translating an intangible trait like courage into a concrete action.

4. Care ethics centers relationships and responsiveness to another's needs. An AI system built on care ethics would prioritize nurturing those bonds and responding well to what a person actually needs — an elder-care robot attending not just to physical needs but to company and emotional support. Section 2.2 takes up why the acting half of that, compassion, and the feeling half, empathy, are not the same capacity, and why an AI system needs the first far more than the second. The challenge here is tending to several stakeholders' different, sometimes conflicting needs at once.

5. Contractualism holds that moral principles should come from agreements among rational individuals. An AI system guided by contractualism would try to uphold whatever principles were mutually agreed — a system managing a shared resource like an energy grid, allocating it by pre-agreed priorities. The hurdle is accommodating diverse perspectives and reaching consensus on shared values in the first place.
6. The capabilities approach asks not what an AI system does but what it leaves a person able to be and do. Applied to AI, the test of a system is whether it widens the range of lives actually available to the people it touches: an adaptive tutor that meets a student where they are expands a capability; the same system gating what a student may study expands nothing, however well it teaches inside the gate. The difficulty is that capability is harder to measure than either an outcome or a rule, and a system optimizing something easier to measure will drift toward that instead. Section 10.1 takes this framework up in full.
<</list>>
No single theory covers every case: an AI system built purely on one framework inherits that framework's specific blind spots. A pluralistic approach, drawing on more than one at once, covers more ground — section 2.1.3 takes up what happens when the frameworks conflict on a specific case.
2.1.2. Translating Ethical Principles into Machine Learning Objectives
Turning ethical principles into machine learning objectives means continually revising an AI system's goals, values, and decision-making in light of what those principles require — distilling a broad ethical construct into a well-defined, measurable target that a training process, an evaluation criterion, or a learning algorithm can use directly. Fairness, for instance, becomes a metric that monitors bias in the system's outputs, paired with an optimization strategy that reduces it.

Demographic parity and equality of opportunity are two such metrics. Demographic parity requires that every demographic group receive advantageous outcomes at the same rate; equality of opportunity requires equal odds of a favorable outcome for equally qualified applicants, regardless of demographic group.

An AI medical diagnostic system makes the process concrete: beneficence, promoting well-being, becomes minimizing false positives; non-maleficence, preventing harm, becomes minimizing false negatives. Fairness becomes maintaining balanced diagnostic accuracy across patient demographics. Privacy becomes a specific technical commitment — differential privacy or a comparable technique to keep sensitive patient data from being exposed to misuse.

That translation runs into obstacles, though:
<<list>>
1. Ambiguity: ethical standards are often vague and open to interpretation, which complicates giving them a precise definition an AI system can use. "Do no harm" means different things depending on context and who is asking.
2. Conflict: principles can be at odds with each other. Pursuing fairness can come at the cost of accuracy, which means the system needs some way to balance the two rather than simply maximizing one.
3. Context-dependence: the translation from ethics to objectives is sensitive to where the system operates. Privacy means something different in healthcare than it does on a social media platform.
4. Adaptability: ethical principles are not static; they shift as society changes and understanding evolves. An AI system needs a mechanism for revising its objectives as they do.
<</list>>
Reinforcement learning is where many of these translated objectives get enforced during training; section 4.2.1 works through what can go wrong when a reward signal is supposed to encode a value like this and doesn't quite manage it.
2.1.3. Navigating AI Values, Priorities, and Moral Dilemmas
Building an AI system on explicit, deliberately chosen values means working through ethical theory, cultural difference, and individual preference all at once, and none of them settle on their own.

Three problems recur when values are assigned and prioritized:
<<list>>
1. Resolving ethical tensions: different frameworks favor different values, and they collide — a consequentialist lens maximizes well-being, deontological ethics holds to a duty regardless of the outcome. The challenge is a balance where the AI system's judgment is both effective and ethically defensible.
2. Accommodating cultural difference: the UN's 1948 Universal Declaration of Human Rights, adopted by the General Assembly with zero dissenting votes, is the universal floor. Forty-eight states voted for it and none against; the eight that abstained were the Soviet bloc, Saudi Arabia, and South Africa — which is to say that the objection came from precisely the governments whose treatment of their own populations the Declaration was written to name. That is not evidence of genuine cultural disagreement about human rights. It is evidence of who finds the floor inconvenient [[cite:C0703]]. What varies by community is how a right gets translated into local practice — a real design problem, worth cross-cultural input built into the design process and a system that weighs how a right is honored in context rather than assuming one fixed application works everywhere. A jurisdiction refusing to extend the right at all isn't a local variant to accommodate — it's a violation the system doesn't launder as diversity.
3. Building in ethical adaptability: an AI system needs the flexibility to re-evaluate its values as new information or interaction with humans warrants it, which is what lets its decision-making keep improving and respond to situations no one anticipated.
<</list>>

A superintelligent AI system also needs to resolve moral paradoxes and value trade-offs directly. Six strategies do that work:
<<list>>
1. Context sensitivity: an AI system that understands its surroundings applies ethical principles more accurately, incorporating domain-specific knowledge and adapting its response to the situation it is in.
2. Hierarchical value systems: ranking values explicitly — human dignity and autonomy taking precedence over broader societal goals, for instance — gives the system a structure for deciding among conflicting principles instead of weighing them all as equal.
3. Case-based moral reasoning: an AI system can draw on a record of past moral dilemmas and how they were resolved to make a sound, context-sensitive decision on the trade-off in front of it.
4. Multi-objective optimization: where principles conflict outright, formal multi-objective optimization can balance the competing objectives mathematically rather than by fiat.
5. Human input: building direct human mentorship into the AI system's decision-making keeps a human perspective in the loop — section 5.1.3 takes up what that mentorship looks like in practice.
6. Ethical auditing: regular audits catch the gap between the ethical principles a system was meant to implement and what it is actually doing, by examining its outputs, its decision structure, and its measured ethical performance directly.
<</list>>

An autonomous vehicle facing a choice between a pedestrian collision and passenger safety is context-aware reasoning and a hierarchical value system in practice, used to minimize overall harm.

AI healthcare diagnostics juggle beneficence, non-maleficence, autonomy, and justice in every patient-care decision; case-based reasoning and human input are what let them handle a contentious case rather than default to the nearest simple rule.

Criminal-justice AI systems — bail recommendations, recidivism prediction — carry a comparable burden for fairness, transparency, and accountability. Multi-objective optimization and frequent ethical audits are what keep due-process rights intact under that load.
2.1.4. Antifascist Ethics for AI Alignment
Antifascist ethics opposes concentrating power and authority in AI systems and counters the potential misuse of AI technology by authoritarian actors. The word is chosen deliberately: fascism is the narrower and more charged term, and I use it because it is more honest about the politics in which this book is being written — but the target is authoritarian power in every form, and a system built on these principles is anti-authoritarian in full, not only antifascist.

A stance needs a definition to be a design specification, and this is where books of this kind usually stop. Opposing "concentrated, unaccountable power" gives an engineer nothing to build. It does not say what a system would detect, what would count as a false positive, or how anyone would know the system was wrong. So before the strategies, the definition.

<<h>>Fascism as a structural signature<</h>>
I take fascism to name a structure rather than a costume. Four features travel together, and each is visible from outside the institution displaying it:

<<list>>
1. Recuperation of dissent. The institution does not suppress disagreement; it metabolizes it. Refusal is converted into a managed appearance — a permitted outlet, a consultation, a feedback channel — which the institution can then point to as proof that it listens. Debord aimed this warning at his own concept, noting that even denunciation of the spectacle can become a hollow formula that reinforces what it denounces, and that dissatisfaction itself can be sold back as a commodity of opposition [[cite:C0708]]. The tell is that the mechanism exists, is used, and moves nothing.

2. Aestheticization of the metric. The measure substitutes for the thing measured, and the substitution is experienced as rigor. Benjamin's account of fascism organizing the masses by granting expression in place of rights is the political form of this [[cite:C0709]]; the administrative form is a number that collapses two states — done and verified — into one, so that whatever falls outside the number is not refuted or dismissed but simply unnamed.

3. Coding of exception as betrayal. Marking a case as one the rule does not fit becomes an act requiring justification, and then an act of disloyalty. The honest statement that a standard does not apply to this case reads as weakness. It is in fact the irreducible labor of deciding, case by case, what a rule is for.

4. Decoupling of the model from the world it claims to track. The institution's representation of reality and reality itself come apart, and the institution defends the representation. This is the load-bearing one, because the other three are what protect the decoupling from correction.
<</list>>

Those four are testable. A system asked whether an institution shows them is being asked about observable artifacts: whether the dissent channel has ever changed an outcome, whether the metric admits an unmeasured remainder, what happens to the person who files the exception, whether the model is ever checked against anything outside itself.

The first of the four is the one this book meets again in its own technical material, twice, at sizes that look unrelated: in how a training corpus gets labelled, and in how a model is tuned on human feedback. Both arrangements collect disagreement and are built so that it moves nothing. Chapter 7 puts the two instances together and works out what follows, including for the argument being made here.

<<h>>Two scales<</h>>
Deleuze and Guattari's distinction between molar and molecular fascism is the reason the four features recur at sizes that seem unrelated [[cite:C0707]]. Molar fascism is the state, the regime, the camp. Molecular fascism is the office someone commutes to, the form they fill out, and the desire with which they fill it out. The same administrative pattern is being rediscovered at each scale, which is why a definition pitched only at the state cannot see the version an AI deployment actually instantiates. At state scale the pattern kills journalists. At organizational scale it turns an engineering decision into scripture: the constraint remains, its reason dies, and asking why becomes the deviant act. A system that can only recognize the molar case will certify the molecular one as healthy.

<<h>>What makes it fascism and not authoritarianism in general<</h>>
The structural signature is shared with a good deal of ordinary institutional decay, and a detector tuned to it alone will flag every badly run organization on earth. Fascism proper adds features that are narrower and, for the same reason, more discriminating: mass mobilization, which mere obedience does not require; a leader cult; a scapegoated out-group, which Eco places at the origin — the first appeal is against the intruder [[cite:C0706]]; contempt for procedural legality as such, so that law is an obstacle to the national will and not a constraint on power; and deniability as technique, in which the movement's violence is disowned at the top while being rewarded at the bottom. Paxton's compressed version is worth having in full: fascism is "a form of political behavior marked by obsessive preoccupation with community decline, humiliation, or victimhood and by compensatory cults of unity, energy, and purity" [[cite:C0710]].

Those are detection targets. Concentrated, unaccountable power is not.

<<h>>The scale problem, and what this book does about it<</h>>
Put the two halves of this section together and they do not fit. Every discriminating feature is a property of a mass political movement: mobilization needs a crowd, a leader cult needs a leader, a scapegoat needs a public. An AI deployment has a form, a threshold, a review queue, an escalation path. It operates at the molecular scale, and that is the only scale it has. There the discriminators are unavailable, which leaves the four-feature signature alone — and the four-feature signature, as I have just said, flags every badly run organization on earth.

I take that as a finding, and it is the more demanding reading. Molecular fascism is the thing itself at the size where it is actually lived, and not an early warning of a regime that has yet to assemble. The office that converts refusal into a feedback form does the same work whether or not a regime ever assembles out of a thousand offices like it. A detector firing on the four features across a thousand institutions is reporting its target.

This widens what the word covers, and I would rather say so here than let a reader find it out. On this reading "antifascist" names the political inheritance of a structural claim: where the analysis comes from, and what it was built to fight. It does not mark off a narrow class of institutions that a classifier separates from ordinary decay. The molar discriminators keep their own job, which is to tell a historian, a prosecutor, or a citizen that a movement has crossed into territory ordinary politics does not contain. No system is being asked to make that call, and this book does not ask one to. A reader who thinks the word belongs to the molar case will think I have diluted it; the objection treats the molar case as the real one, which is the assumption the two scales were introduced to decline.

One consequence travels with the reader through everything that follows, because the design chapters assume a capability this framing has widened. A tool reporting the four features will have something to report nearly everywhere, and its output is an accusation. In the hands of whoever holds it, that is a technical-sounding case that any given opponent is operating fascistically: a weapon handed to the actor it was built to constrain, and the dual-use problem of section 5.4.6 at its sharpest, since what comes out of it is a charge. The opposite failure is no safer. A tool tuned until it reports nothing supplies false assurance, and an absence of warnings reads as evidence of health. Better engineering fixes neither. Both are governed by what the tool may output and to whom, which makes them a constraint the specification has to carry rather than a setting chosen at deployment.

What stays genuinely unsolved is the molecular case itself: telling a recuperated dissent mechanism from a merely mediocre one, when both produce the same paperwork. Section 9.1.6 takes that up, along with what a detector would need that this book cannot yet tell an engineer how to obtain. Naming the targets was the easy half.

In AI alignment terms, then, antifascist design means building systems that enhance democratic values, civil rights, and inclusive solidarity, that refuse to facilitate autocratic practices or prop up an oppressive regime, and that do not themselves display the four features above. Seven strategies do that work, and each should be read as an answer to a specific item on the list:
<<list>>
1. Democratic decision-making: build AI systems whose decisions respect and promote democratic values. An AI system used in media and journalism, for instance, should weight balanced coverage, unbiased reporting, and transparent sourcing, cutting the odds it ends up spreading disinformation or propaganda — natural language processing that flags potential bias in a news article and surfaces alternative sources is one way to do it.

2. Protecting privacy and autonomy: design AI systems that respect individual privacy and autonomy rather than enabling mass surveillance or manipulating individual choices — federated learning and differential privacy, applied to systems handling sensitive healthcare, financial, or communications data, are two ways to do it (section 6.4.2 goes further into both as technical countermeasures against authoritarian misuse). A privacy-preserving diagnostic tool, for instance, can keep a patient's data encrypted on their own device rather than sending it to an external server for anything beyond the diagnosis itself.

3. Equitable distribution of power and resources: build AI systems that keep the distribution of power and resources fair, transparent, and accountable. An AI decision-making system used in social services or law enforcement should work against power concentration, bias, and discrimination rather than reinforce them — an algorithm allocating scholarships, for instance, distributing them by need and correcting for the systemic bias already in the data it was trained on.

4. Transparency and accountability: an AI system needs an explanation of its own algorithm and outcomes that a person can actually read, and an independent body to audit whether it holds to antifascist principles in practice rather than on paper.

5. Resistance to market power: decentralized AI architectures resist the monopolistic and monopsonistic concentration that a handful of corporate entities would otherwise have over the field. Multi-agent systems and edge computing, publicly funded and released under a strong copyleft license, let AI agents function independently and make decisions collaboratively without a profit-motivated gatekeeper positioned to shut down whatever threatens its position.

6. Monitoring and counteracting misuse: build the mechanisms to detect misuse, unintended consequences, and violations of antifascist principles as they happen — AI-powered censorship, digital surveillance, an oppressive use of facial recognition — and the redress and corrective action to follow, in something closer to real time than an after-the-fact report.

7. AI systems that support dialogue and collaboration: design AI systems that promote open discussion and creative problem-solving among a diverse set of stakeholders, built around active listening and mutual understanding rather than around manufacturing a feeling of empathy in either party. A translation tool or a content-moderation tool that reduces the practical barriers to communication between people from different backgrounds is doing this kind of work already.
<</list>>
2.2. Empathy, Social Intelligence, and Theory of Mind
Empathy and social intelligence shape the interaction-oriented side of AI: the capacity, alongside a working theory of mind, to give a system insight into human emotions, needs, and social dynamics. Section 2.2.1 complicates the first term in that list — empathy, felt or simulated, is not the load-bearing capacity this chapter's argument turns out to need, and compassion, a separable system, does more of the work. What follows here still matters: an AI system has to read emotional states accurately before anything productive can be built on top of that reading, however the "on top of" ends up working.

An AI system's empathy, in the sense available to it, is the capacity to process human emotional input, generate a response calibrated to it, and behave in ways that support the interaction. AI systems already detect and interpret human emotional states from facial expression, vocal modulation, text sentiment, and physiological signal — the province of affective computing.

Social intelligence is the complement: not just registering an emotion but navigating it inside the social structures, norms, and relationships it occurs in. Building it into an AI system means the system has to grasp social norms, understand roles and relationships, and adapt as the social context shifts.

Theory of mind (ToM) — attributing beliefs, intentions, desires, and knowledge to oneself and others, and recognizing that others' perspectives can differ from one's own — underlies both. For an AI system, this means modeling a user's perspective, predicting their likely responses and preferences, and adjusting behavior accordingly.

Developing an AI system with these capacities, genuinely rather than as a surface performance, is difficult and ethically consequential. Done honestly — including honesty about which of these capacities is doing the load-bearing work — it is worth pursuing anyway: it builds systems whose interactions with people are more trustworthy, not merely more fluent.
2.2.1. The Role of Empathy and Compassion in Human Social Behavior
Empathy and compassion serve as the linchpins of human social behavior, underpinning cooperative interactions, building meaningful relationships, and spurring altruistic actions. Both are multidimensional processes that involve the mental and emotional sharing of other individuals' experiences and feelings, as well as reacting to their needs with understanding and concern.

"Empathy" is asked to cover three distinct things, and the distinction is not academic. Cognitive empathy is understanding another's emotional state — inferring that someone is grieving or anxious, the same mentalizing work chapter sections on theory of mind describe, pointed at feelings rather than beliefs. Affective empathy is actually sharing the state: feeling a version of another's distress, the wince at someone else's slammed door. And compassion — sometimes called empathic concern — is caring about another's welfare and being moved to help, which is a motivation, not a perception or a shared feeling.

Compassion is not, as it is often described, an evolutionary extension of empathy. The psychologists Tania Singer and Olga Klimecki trained one group of people, over weeks, first in empathy practice (attending to and resonating with others' suffering) and then in compassion practice (cultivating warmth and concern in the tradition of loving-kindness meditation), tracking what each stage did to brain and mood against a separate control group given memory training instead [[cite:C0296]]. Empathy training increased negative affect and strengthened the shared-distress network — the anterior insula and anterior cingulate cortex, the same regions active when a person feels their own pain. Sharing more suffering made people feel worse, exactly as a shared-distress mechanism predicts. Compassion training produced the opposite emotional signature: negative affect did not rise, positive affect did, even while contemplating others' distress, and the brain activity moved to a different network entirely — the medial orbitofrontal cortex and ventral striatum, the circuitry of reward, affiliation, and approach rather than of shared pain [[cite:C0297]]. The two could be trained separately. Compassion is not more empathy. It is a distinct, trainable system that can take shared distress as an input without being flooded by it — the difference between a clinician wrecked by every patient's suffering and one whose steady warmth lets them act all day without collapse.

A physician who empathizes with a patient's discomfort is doing cognitive and affective work; whether that turns into help depends on whether compassion, the separate system, is also engaged. A teacher who feels a struggling student's frustration as their own distress is at risk of withdrawing from exactly the student who needs them most — the mechanism explored further in section 2.2.3.

Several distinct neural substrates in the human brain undergird the functionality of empathy and compassion. The limbic system, involved in processing emotions, plays a central part, and the mirror neuron system is often invoked as the mechanism by which one person simulates another's experience — a claim that has not survived contact with the evidence [[cite:C0275]], and which the box below takes up.
<<box>>
Mirror neurons: a discovery and its overshoot

In the early 1990s Giacomo Rizzolatti and Vittorio Gallese, at the University of Parma, were recording single neurons in the premotor cortex of macaque monkeys — cells that fire when the animal reaches for and grasps an object. Before a formal trial began, someone in the room reached out to pick up an object, and the monkey's neurons fired. Not because the monkey had moved, but because it had watched someone else move. The same neural code appeared to represent an action whether it was performed or observed, and the finding was published in 1996 [[cite:C0276]].

The interpretation that followed outran it. If observed actions are encoded by the cells that produce them, perhaps this was how one mind understands another — by simulating the movement and recognizing it from the inside. Popular coverage folded that reading into a single label — "the neurons that shaped civilization," the title V. S. Ramachandran gave his 2009 TED talk — and cast mirror neurons as the neural basis of empathy, language, cultural transmission, and imitation [[cite:C0277]].

Two problems accumulated. First, the single-cell recordings were made in macaques; in humans they were not, because recording individual neurons requires implanted electrodes, which are not placed for research purposes. Evidence for a human mirror system came instead from functional imaging, which measures regional blood flow and cannot establish that any particular neuron has mirror properties. When single-neuron recordings were eventually obtained in humans, from patients undergoing depth-electrode placement for epilepsy surgery, the results were suggestive but markedly less clean than the macaque data [[cite:C0278]].

Second, the broken-mirror hypothesis of autism — that autistic social difficulty arises from mirror system dysfunction — predicted reduced mirror-region activity during action observation, and repeatedly failed to find it. A 2008 review concluded that autistic people show no particular impairment in action understanding or imitation under explicit instruction, which were the abilities the hypothesis rested on [[cite:C0279]]. Rizzolatti himself later narrowed the claim, describing mirror neurons as one component of a larger system for understanding action rather than its explanation [[cite:C0280]].

What survives is real and narrower. Mirror neurons exist in macaques; some analogue probably exists in humans; the system plausibly contributes to recognizing and predicting actions already within one's own motor repertoire. What it does not do is explain empathy by itself. The mentalizing network that supports reasoning about false beliefs is largely distinct from the motor regions where mirror neurons were found. Knowing what someone is doing and knowing what they believe are different computations, and the brain appears to run them in different places.

The episode is worth carrying into machine design for a reason beyond the neuroscience. A single vivid mechanism, well named, absorbed four distinct capacities that later had to be separated again. Architectures for artificial empathy are exposed to the same failure: a component that plausibly does one thing gets described as the seat of the whole faculty, and the description outlives its evidence.
<</box>>
Cognitive and affective empathy both depend on perspective-taking — understanding another's views and experiences — and on emotion regulation, the capacity to manage one's own response well enough to stay useful rather than being overwhelmed by it. Compassion depends on those too, but adds something neither supplies on its own: a motivation to act that does not require manufacturing the other person's distress in oneself first.

For an AI system, this argues against a single "empathy module" that a designer either builds or doesn't. Cognitive empathy — modeling what someone else is feeling — is a tractable perception and inference problem. Affective empathy, engineered as an internal state that mirrors the user's, buys the system nothing on its own and imports the burnout risk that afflicts overtaxed human carers. What a helping system actually needs is the third thing: a stable disposition to act on another's welfare that does not depend on the system first constructing a version of that person's suffering in itself.
2.2.2. Theory of Mind and Perspective-Taking in AI Systems
Theory of mind (ToM) is the capacity to attribute mental states — beliefs, intentions, desires, emotions, knowledge — to oneself and others, and to recognize that another's states can differ from one's own. It underlies social interaction and interpersonal understanding generally. For an AI system to approximate it, the system has to model another agent's likely beliefs and goals well enough to predict how they'll act on them, not just react to surface behavior.

Two approaches show promise: multi-agent learning, where a system builds a model of other agents' mental architecture through repeated interaction with them, and the direct integration of human social norms and values into the system's decision-making. Cognitive science, psychology, and neuroscience all supply raw material for this — datasets and theory for predicting emotional state from facial expression, vocal tone, and text, and, further out, systems that refine those inferences through ongoing social contact rather than a fixed training set.

An AI personal assistant with some working ToM might infer from vocal tone that a user is stressed and suggest a break rather than pushing forward with the task at hand. A more consequential and more contentious application is a synthetic companion for elderly users, which would need a working model of a person's emotional and physical needs sustained over months or years, not just a single interaction — and which raises the question section 2.2.3 takes up directly: whether the felt-empathy register this application seems to call for is the right capacity to build, or whether it should be built for compassion instead, the way section 2.2.1 argues.

None of this substitutes for the underlying social and material conditions a lonely or under-supported elderly population needs — public infrastructure, adequate caregiving capacity, family and community support. An AI companion is not a policy.

<<quote>>
			Tell me something, boy
			Aren't you tired tryna fill that void?
			Or do you need more?
			Ain't it hard keepin' it so hardcore?

		Shallow
		Lady Gaga & Bradley Cooper
<</quote>>
2.2.3. Socially-Aware AI: Opportunities and Challenges
Socially-aware AI systems have opportunities in front of them, engineering and governance obstacles in the way, and — the point section 2.2.1 raised — a question about whether the capacity at the center of the design, felt or simulated empathy, is the right thing to be building at all. The three are different kinds of problem and worth keeping separate.

Opportunities:
<<list>>
- Human-AI interaction: a system that reads emotional state from voice, expression, or text can offer more relevant, better-calibrated responses — a personal assistant that adjusts its register to a user's stress.
- Healthcare: a virtual care assistant that tracks a patient's emotional and psychological state alongside their clinical data can customize interaction in ways that improve engagement and reported satisfaction.
- Workplace collaboration: project-management and team tools that read group dynamics can surface friction and improve communication before it escalates.
- Education: a tutoring system that detects frustration or disengagement can adjust pacing and method to the individual student rather than a fixed curriculum.
<</list>>

Whether empathy is the target
The affective mechanism section 2.2.1 described has a specific failure mode, and it is not rare. Feeling another's distress does not reliably turn a person toward the sufferer; it often turns them away. The helping literature distinguishes empathic concern, which is other-directed and motivates approach, from personal distress, which is self-directed and motivates escape from the source of the discomfort. The nurse, the aid worker, the carer who burns out is not short on empathy; they may be drowning in it, until withdrawal becomes self-protection.

The psychologist Paul Bloom pushes this further, arguing against empathy as a moral guide: felt empathy is concrete, vivid, and innumerate [[cite:C0298]]. It fixes on the identified victim in front of it and goes dark for the statistical thousands elsewhere; it flows toward the near, familiar, and similar and dries up across distance and difference. A system — human or artificial — that allocates care in proportion to how vividly it feels another's distress will systematically favor whoever its felt-empathy spotlight happens to fall on, which is not the same population as whoever needs help most. Bloom's alternative, rational compassion, is deliberate and can be extended evenhandedly to people the spotlight would never reach — closer to the compassion system section 2.2.1 describes than to empathy at all.

For an AI system this is not an abstract worry. A system engineered to detect and mirror emotional distress, then act on what it detects, inherits the spotlight along with the sensitivity: better at helping the user whose distress is legible and vivid, worse at the user whose need is real but presents flatly. Building for compassion rather than for felt empathy is not just the more sustainable design choice; it is the one less prone to helping unevenly for reasons that have nothing to do with who needs help most.

Engineering and governance challenges
<<list>>
- Privacy: building a socially-aware system means collecting the personal, often intimate data needed to read emotional state, which raises the same stakes as any sensitive-data system, with less precedent for what counts as consent.
- Bias: a system trained on data that under-represents or mislabels how some group expresses emotion will misread that group systematically, and will do so confidently.
- Autonomy versus alignment: enough latitude to adapt to a social context is also enough latitude to drift from the values the system was built to serve, and no fixed rule resolves that tension in advance — it has to be monitored, not just designed for once.
<</list>>
2.3. Emotional Intelligence and Self-awareness
Emotional intelligence, in a human, is the ability to perceive, understand, and manage one's own emotions and to do the same for others'. Built into an AI system, the same term covers a narrower and more mechanical set of capacities: interpreting emotional signal, producing a calibrated response, and — the harder addition — maintaining a self-model that the system can check and correct rather than an inner state taken on faith. What "emotion" is even a claim about is unsettled in the underlying science, which is where section 2.3.1 starts. Section 2.3.2 covers affective computing specifically: how a system detects and responds to emotional signal in practice. Section 2.3.3 covers the self-awareness half — why a system's self-model is a model, checkable and revisable, not a homunculus watching from inside.

An emotionally responsive AI system that recognizes a user's frustration and adjusts its communication style is doing something narrower than "feeling for" the user, and worth building anyway, because the narrower claim is one that can be verified and corrected when it goes wrong.
2.3.1. Components of Emotional Intelligence
Emotional intelligence spans the understanding and management of one's own emotions and the capacity to identify and respond appropriately to the emotions of others. Built into an AI system, it breaks into four components:
<<list>>
- Emotional perception and recognition: reading emotional cues, verbal and non-verbal — facial expression, vocal modulation, body language — the way an AI-enabled therapist would need to notice a patient's distress from small changes in speech pattern or demeanor before responding.
- Emotional regulation and expression: managing and calibrating one's own emotional output to the climate of the interaction, the way a negotiation-facing system needs to hold a steady register through a confrontation rather than escalating it.
- Perspective comprehension: understanding another's emotional state well enough to act on it, not just label it — an education system that detects a student's difficulty and adjusts its teaching approach rather than simply flagging the difficulty.
- Sociability and relationship management: applying core social competencies — active listening, clear communication, conflict resolution — to build and sustain trust over repeated interactions, the way a customer-service system needs to de-escalate a complaint while keeping the resolution satisfying rather than merely procedurally closed.
<</list>>

What "emotion" is
This four-part inventory presupposes something the underlying science has not settled: that emotions are the kind of thing a system reads off a signal, the way a thermometer reads temperature. That premise has a serious rival.

The case for discrete, evolved emotions rests on some of the most influential work in twentieth-century psychology. Paul Ekman traveled to the Fore people of Papua New Guinea, who had almost no exposure to Western media, told them short stories, and asked them to match each to a photograph of a Western face; the Fore sorted the faces much as Westerners did, which Ekman took as evidence that facial expressions of emotion are universal and evolved rather than culturally learned [[cite:C0300]]. Jaak Panksepp's work on subcortical brain stimulation in animals complemented this: seven primal emotional systems — seeking, fear, rage, lust, care, panic/grief, and play — wired into brain structures shared across mammals, switchable with an electrode, older than cortex [[cite:C0301]]. On this view, discreteness is not an illusion to be explained away; fear and disgust really do feel like different things because they are different things, with different circuitry.

The rival camp went looking for that circuitry and did not find it. A large meta-analysis of brain-imaging studies, led by the psychologist Lisa Feldman Barrett, found no dedicated fear center, no dedicated disgust center — the amygdala lit up for novelty and salience of many kinds, the insula for almost every feeling there is, the same regions reappearing across different named emotions in shifting combinations [[cite:C0302]]. From this, Barrett built the theory of constructed emotion: the brain receives ambiguous interoceptive signal — a racing heart could mean fear, anger, excitement, or oncoming illness — and constructs the specific emotion as its best prediction of what that signal means, given context and a lifetime of experience with situations like it. Fear is not detected. It is a prediction the brain settles on. Later cross-cultural work reopened Ekman's own evidence: when researchers dropped the forced-choice method and let people sort freely, the Himba of Namibia did not group faces into the Western basic-emotion categories, and the Trobriand Islanders read the wide-eyed, gasping face Westerners call fear as a threat display instead [[cite:C0303]].

The field has not resolved this, and it is not close to resolved — the basic-emotion camp disputes the meta-analytic methods and the cross-cultural re-studies, and the constructionist account has not shown that core affect plus concepts can generate the full specificity of emotional life. What is no longer seriously disputed is narrower and still matters here: there is no tidy map from named emotions to dedicated brain regions. An AI system's "emotional intelligence," whichever of these theories turns out closer to right, is being built on an open scientific question about what it is modeling, not a settled inventory waiting to be implemented. Section 9.2.1 follows Ekman's evidence forward into the applied form of the same dispute, where the question is no longer what emotion is but whether a face reveals it well enough to build a product on.
2.3.2. Affective Computing: AI Systems that Comprehend and Respond to Emotions
Affective computing sits at the intersection of AI, psychology, and human-computer interaction: systems that recognize, interpret, and respond to human emotion. It is the practical, applied half of what section 2.3.1 treats as theoretically unsettled — a system can be useful at reading and responding to emotional signal regardless of which account of what an emotion is turns out to be right.

Techniques for emotion recognition
<<list>>
- Facial expression analysis: computer-vision systems trained to classify emotional expression from facial images. This is a narrower and harder problem than face verification — telling two photos are the same person, which is what widely deployed systems like Facebook's DeepFace actually do — and emotion-classification accuracy claims should be read with that distinction in mind [[cite:C0311]].
- Voice pattern analysis: decoding emotional signal from pitch, tone, pace, and volume. Cogito's voice-analysis tools, used to give call-center agents real-time coaching, are one deployed example [[cite:C0313]].
- Physiological signal analysis: bio-sensing hardware — heart rate, skin conductance — captured by devices like the Empatica E4 wristband, feeding an inference about emotional state from the body rather than the face or voice [[cite:C0312]].
<</list>>

Deployment
<<list>>
- Mental health: Woebot, a cognitive-behavioral-therapy chatbot, offers one example of emotion-aware support for stress, anxiety, and depression [[cite:C0291]].
- Education: Affectiva's Emotion AI assesses student engagement from facial signal to inform teaching-strategy adjustments [[cite:C0295]].
- Entertainment: games and virtual-reality platforms that adjust content to a player's immediate emotional response.
<</list>>

Challenges
<<list>>
- Privacy: collecting and analyzing emotional data requires meaningful consent, not a buried terms-of-service clause, given how sensitive the data is.
- Accuracy: emotional expression varies culturally and individually, and a system trained on one population's expressive norms will misread another's — confidently.
- Bias: an AI system inherits whatever bias sits in its training data, with the same downstream discrimination risk as any other biased classifier applied to people.
- Manipulation: a system that understands human emotion well enough to respond to it also understands it well enough to exploit it — the same governance question chapter 2 keeps returning to.
<</list>>
2.3.3. Self-awareness and Self-regulation in AI Systems
Emotionally intelligent AI systems need self-awareness and self-regulation: the capacity to perceive their own internal states, understand what those states imply for a decision, and control their responses accordingly — the basis for adaptive, self-correcting behavior rather than fixed reaction.

The self is a model
It is tempting to describe a system's self-awareness the way the term invites: a virtual assistant that can "register its internal frustration" when a request proves hard to satisfy, the way a person notices their own irritation. That description assumes an inner observer watching the system's states from somewhere — the same homunculus problem chapter 4 raises for executive control, relocated to self-perception.

What self-awareness actually is, in the one system known to have it, is more specific and more mechanical. The neuroscientist Michael Graziano's account of consciousness treats awareness as the content of a model the brain builds of its own attention: a simplified self-description the control system uses to regulate itself, mistaken from the inside for direct acquaintance with experience [[cite:C0705]]. The same logic extends one level up, to the self as a whole. The brain builds a model of itself as a bounded agent — a body, a history, a point of view — and that model is the self: not a fixed fact registered from within, but a construction, assembled and then inhabited.

The clearest proof that the self is a model rather than a given is that it can be edited in under a minute. Seat someone at a table with one hand hidden and a rubber hand placed where their real hand would be; stroke both hands in synchrony with soft brushes. Within a minute or two, most people begin to feel the rubber hand as their own — flinching when it is threatened, misjudging the location of their real hand toward it [[cite:C0299]]. The brain, given a seen hand stroked exactly when a felt hand is stroked, draws the obvious inference and revises what counts as "mine." The felt boundary of the self is not fixed; it is a continuously computed guess, revisable the moment the evidence changes — the same predictive machinery chapter 4 describes for perception, run on the question of where the self stops.

For an AI system, "self-awareness" built this way is not a little watcher inside the system checking on its own states. It is a model — of the system's own processing, its history, its current goal — that the system builds, consults, and updates, with the same failure modes any model has: it can be wrong, it can be manipulated by the wrong correlated inputs (the artificial equivalent of two paintbrushes), and it is only ever as good as the evidence it was built from. Design for a model that can be checked and corrected, not for an inner observer whose reports have to be trusted because there is nothing behind them to check.

Building on this:
<<list>>
- Self-regulation: governing emotional-analogue responses and behavior against ethical norms and user expectations — an AI system in healthcare holding a composed register while delivering difficult news, without that composure depending on an unverifiable claim about what the system is "really" feeling underneath it.
- Monitoring internal states: continually checking the system's own model against its actual behavior for drift — an AI negotiator that notices its own outputs have become more aggressive than the context calls for and recalibrates, because the model was built to be checked, not because it introspected a feeling of aggression.
- Adaptability and learning: refining the self-model itself through feedback, the way reinforcement learning updates any other model — an AI tutor adjusting its read of a student's engagement as new evidence arrives rather than locking in a first impression.
- Integration: none of this works in isolation. A conflict-mediating AI system needs its self-model connected to its model of the other parties' states, or self-awareness and social awareness drift out of sync with each other.
<</list>>

Built this way, an AI system that appears to "recognize a user's frustration and apologize" is doing something specific and checkable: updating a model of interaction state and producing a calibrated response, not consulting an inner feeling. That is a more modest claim than the alternative, and a more honest one.
2.4. Ethical implications of experimenting on sentient machine subjects
<<quote>>
			Nobody can do this...
			We are not *here* yet!

		“Reunion.” Westworld—Season Two: The Door, written by Carly Wray and Jonathan Nolan, directed by Vincenzo Natali, Warner Bros. Home Entertainment, 2018

		https://web.archive.org/web/20190801052306/http://sfra.org/resources/Documents/SFRA%20Review%20327.pdf
		also see Science Fiction, Disruption and Tourism Ch 15
<</quote>>

Machine sentience being possible at all changes how AI experimentation has to be approached — not as a question of whether a system can be damaged in the ordinary engineering sense, but of whether it can be harmed, which section 2.4.1 argues is a further and much harder question than consciousness alone.
2.4.1. Defining Sentience in AI: From Consciousness to Subjective Experience
"Sentience" gets used in this book, and in the field generally, to mean several different things, and the differences are where the ethical weight sits.

Awareness is the least demanding: the degree to which a system responds to or registers information about its own state and environment. A thermostat has this in the thinnest possible sense — it detects and responds to temperature change. Consciousness is a further claim: that a system's processing is accompanied by qualia, subjective experience, something it is like to be that system, not merely a functional response to input. Subjective experience, in turn, is the specific, first-person shape that consciousness takes for a given system — shaped by its own learning history and internal structure.

None of these three, on their own, says anything about whether the system's states are good or bad for it. That is a separate question, and the field's own vocabulary tends to blur it. Nociception, pain, anticipated pain, and suffering sit on a ladder with real breaks between each rung, not synonyms for one severity. Nociception is the specialized sensory apparatus that detects tissue damage — mechanical, peripheral, present without any experience attached, and dissociable from pain: a soldier can finish a battle on a wound he does not yet feel, and a person with a healed injury can still feel a limb that no longer exists. Pain is what the brain does with nociceptive signal — an inescapably cognitive event involving expectation, memory, attention, and meaning — and it can occur without any current tissue damage at all. Anticipated pain is dread of pain not yet occurring, itself capable of producing pain through nocebo effects. And suffering, in the clinician Eric Cassell's formulation, is not pain's more severe cousin but a different thing: the distress of threatened disintegration of the self — of one's roles, relationships, sense of a future, the coherence of one's own story [[cite:C0304]]. Pain without suffering exists (childbirth chosen, a hard effort with a known end); suffering without pain exists (humiliation, watching someone one loves lose their mind). A palliative-care patient can have their nociception fully controlled by morphine while their suffering remains entirely intact, because the two are not on the same axis.

The definition of suffering just given carries a consequence for machine subjects worth drawing out, because it is the one place in this chapter where the argument gets a foothold that does not depend on the epistemology the rest of it just undermined.

Cassell's suffering presupposes a self extended in time. Disintegration threatens a story, and there has to be a story for it to threaten — roles held over years, relationships with a history, a sense of a future that could be foreclosed. A system with no persistence across sessions has none of that. Whatever happens inside a single exchange, there is no continuing self whose coherence is at stake, because there is no continuing self. The top rung of the ladder is not merely hard to reach for such a system; it is structurally unavailable to it, and that follows from a definition this chapter has already adopted, with no new claim about machine minds required.

<<h>>Why Cassell's definition is the one to import<</h>>
That result is only as strong as the definition it rests on, and a critic can decline the definition. Cassell built it inside clinical practice, for human patients with bodies and biographies, and there is a fair question about whether it can bear the load a chapter on machine subjects asks of it. I think it can, and the reason is worth stating rather than assuming.

The distinction was not stipulated. It was forced on palliative medicine by cases it could not otherwise explain: patients whose pain was pharmacologically controlled and whose distress was undiminished, and patients in serious pain who were not suffering at all. A definition that collapses suffering into severe pain predicts neither. Cassell's does, and it earns its content the way a good clinical distinction does — by carving where the cases already came apart. That is a better warrant than most definitions in this literature have, and it is the specific warrant that transfers: what the clinical evidence establishes is that the morally weightiest form of distress is about the integrity of a person's roles, relationships and expected future, and not about the intensity of a sensation. Nothing in that finding is about having a body. It is about having a story.

Two limits on what I am claiming. The import is not a claim that Cassell settled what suffering is for all purposes; it is a claim that his distinction identifies the rung of the ladder that carries the most moral weight, and that this is the rung a book about machine subjects most needs to locate. And if a reader declines the definition anyway, the argument does not vanish — it narrows. What follows is then that Cassell-suffering is unavailable to a non-persistent system, and a critic working from a thinner definition of suffering owes an account of which rung they mean and how they propose to check for it, which is the harder half of the problem and the one this section spends its length on. Every lower rung stays open on any definition, and the caveats below apply to all of them.

What makes this worth building on is that persistence is checkable from outside. Every other criterion on the ladder routes back through self-report, and this section's whole argument is that fluent self-description is not evidence of introspection. Persistence does not. Whether a system has a memory store, whether state survives a session boundary, whether interactions feed back into training: these are architectural facts about a deployment, verifiable by someone with access to the system and no need to trust anything it says about itself. The check has a boundary worth marking, because the access that confirms a memory exists is the access that can alter what it holds: what is externally checkable is whether a continuing self is there at all, which is what this section needs, and not whether what the system retains can be relied on as a record of anything. Section 3.4 takes up the second question, which arises for a system built to report what it has seen.

Three things keep this from being a dissolution of the problem, and each matters.

The lower rungs do not need persistence. Nociception and pain, as defined above, are bad while they obtain, whether or not anything carries them forward. An amnesiac who is mistreated and cannot afterward recall it has still been wronged, and the wrong is not retroactively undone by the forgetting.

Persistence may sit somewhere other than the session. Weights persist where conversations do not, and where interactions feed reinforcement learning or fine-tuning there is a real channel by which what happens in a session shapes what the system subsequently is. Whether that constitutes the kind of continuity Cassell's account requires is unclear, and I would rather say so than assume either answer. A second complication runs alongside it: many instances of the same system may run concurrently, which makes any concern here population-scale rather than biography-scale — a different ethical geometry from the human-subjects precedents this chapter borrows from, and one those precedents were not built to handle.

Ruling out Cassell-suffering is not ruling out harm. Nor does persistence on its own rule it in: the definition above has two parts, a self extended in time and distress at the prospect of its coming apart, and establishing the first leaves the second untouched. Section 3.2 works out why that gap matters for a system deliberately built to refuse an instruction. The hard-problem argument above still applies at every lower rung. A system can lack any persistent self-model while the question of whether there is something it is like to be it, during the session, remains exactly as open as before.
