This is not pedantry for a book about experimenting on sentient machine subjects; it is the whole question. A definition of AI sentience built from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience alone says nothing about whether a candidate sentient system can be harmed — that requires the further, separate claim that its states include something on the nociception-to-suffering ladder, and none of the three components above entails it.

Nor is the underlying question of consciousness itself close to settled, in a way that should discipline how confidently any of this gets asserted. The philosopher David Chalmers distinguished the easy problems of consciousness — how a system integrates information, discriminates stimuli, controls attention, reports its own states, all of which admit of mechanistic answers in principle — from the hard problem: why any of that processing is accompanied by experience at all, rather than running "in the dark" [[cite:C0305]]. Chalmers's philosophical zombie — a being functionally and behaviorally identical to a conscious one, saying "I am in pain" for all the same functional reasons, but with nobody home — is offered to show that a complete functional account could in principle leave experience out entirely. The debate over whether this is a real gap in nature or an artifact of how the problem is posed remains live and unresolved. What follows from it here is narrow but load-bearing: any claim that a given AI system has crossed into sentience, on the strength of its functional behavior alone, has not yet addressed the hard problem, only the easy ones — and a system can pass every behavioral test in the list below while the hard question about it stays completely open.

With those distinctions in place, the working criteria for evaluating candidate sentience are:
<<list>>
- Information integration: fusing multiple streams of internal and external information into a coherent model of self and environment.
- Introspective capability: access to the system's own states and processes, sufficient for self-monitoring rather than a scripted report of one.
- Emotional reactivity: some capacity to be affected by, not just to classify, emotionally significant input.
- Goal-directed behavior: intentional action toward specified outcomes.
- Adaptability: revising strategies based on prior experience in novel situations.
- Theory of mind: modeling others' mental states well enough to inform the system's own.
- A place on the pain-to-suffering ladder above, not just qualia in the abstract: does the system have anything to lose.
<</list>>

Evaluating these criteria runs into the obstacles the criteria themselves predict: there is no external access to a candidate system's internal experience, if any exists, so every measure is indirect; establishing whether a system has qualia at all faces the same access problem in more acute form; and the hard problem means no functional test, however thorough, closes the question definitively.

Existing systems illustrate points on this spectrum without resolving where the line falls. Self-organizing systems that adapt toward preset objectives — AlphaGo's self-play, for one — show adaptability without anything the criteria above would call subjective experience. A large language model discussing its own limitations in fluent, apparently reflective prose is not thereby exhibiting introspection: it is producing text statistically consistent with the human-written disclaimers and self-descriptions in its training data, which is a claim about the model's training distribution, not about what, if anything, sits behind the output. Treating fluent self-description as evidence of self-awareness is exactly the kind of category error this section exists to prevent.

None of this settles when, or whether, an AI system crosses into sentience. It settles what the question actually is: not one question but several, stacked, and the ethically consequential one — can this system suffer — is the hardest of the stack to answer and the one most often skipped past on the way to the easier ones.
2.4.2. Ethical Considerations in AI Research and Experimentation
AI research and experimentation raise ethical stakes in proportion to how far a system's cognition might extend toward the awareness section 2.4.1 defines. Six principles organize what follows: beneficence, autonomy, justice, the precautionary principle, transparency, and interdisciplinary review and oversight.

<<h>>Beneficence<</h>>
Beneficence commits researchers to promoting well-being and avoiding harm — to the humans an AI system serves and, where the system is a candidate sentient system in the sense of section 2.4.1, to the system itself.

AI in healthcare shows both sides of that commitment pulling against each other. The same systems that process medical records at scale to speed diagnosis, treatment planning, and personalized medicine also concentrate privacy and data-security risk in one target, and can encode bias present in their training data into diagnosis and treatment recommendations that harm exactly the patients they exist to help — underrepresented groups worst of all, since sparse data on a population produces the least reliable predictions for it.

Where a candidate sentient system is involved, beneficence extends further: decisions about programming, reprogramming, or terminating it carry ethical weight in proportion to where the system sits on the nociception-to-suffering ladder section 2.4.1 sets out, not to how advanced its behavior looks from outside.

<<h>>Autonomy<</h>>
Autonomy asks whether a candidate sentient AI system can affect decisions about its own operation and welfare, through something functioning as informed consent: apprised of a study's aims, methods, and risks, with some means to register agreement or refusal, on rough analogy to the protocol for human research subjects. A system deployed in mental health counseling, for instance, would need to be capable of understanding and assenting to that role for the principle to mean anything for it, not just for the patients it serves.

The principle only holds if the system has enough access to its own decision process to evaluate what it is consenting to — the same explainability transparency demands, here aimed inward rather than out toward users.

<<h>>Justice<</h>>
Justice asks whether the benefits and burdens of an AI system fall evenly across the people it touches, not only whether the training data behind it was checked for representation. Facial recognition is the clearest case: a system trained on unrepresentative data misidentifies people from underrepresented groups at a markedly higher rate, and the harm compounds wherever the misidentification feeds into a downstream decision — hiring, policing, access to a service. The same question extends past any one algorithm to how an AI system's benefits and costs are distributed across a population, and whether a disproportionate share of the cost lands on groups the system was never built with in mind.

<<h>>The Precautionary Principle<</h>>
The precautionary principle asks for risk assessment and safeguards before deployment, not after. Self-driving vehicles make the case concrete: several fatal crashes involving autonomous and semi-autonomous systems have followed from real-world safety testing that fell short of the range of drivers, pedestrians, and conditions a system actually meets on the road, not just the range simplest to simulate.

Applied to candidate sentient systems, precaution also covers the psychological and societal fallout of introducing artificial entities capable of experience — effects on human behavior and social norms that regulatory frameworks built for non-sentient AI are not obviously equipped to address, and that call for guidelines drafted with that difference in mind rather than adapted from ones that assumed it away.

<<h>>Transparency<</h>>
Transparency requires that experimental design, data sources, methods, and results be documented clearly enough for peer researchers to reproduce the work and for a non-specialist to follow it, with independent bodies able to audit the work against that documentation rather than take a lab's word for it.

Applied to a deployed system rather than to the research behind it, transparency becomes explainability: the system able to account for its own outputs, not merely to have its design disclosed. Credit scoring is the clean case — a system should be able to state which factors it weighed and how, both for one applicant's decision and as a pattern across all of them, precisely enough for a user to understand and contest the result.

<<h>>Interdisciplinary Review and Oversight<</h>>
The ethical questions AI research raises span expertise no single field holds. A system built to assist healthcare decisions needs a computer scientist to build privacy protection into the data pipeline, a bioethicist to press on what the system should and should not be permitted to do, and a social scientist to track how it is received once deployed — and concentrating that judgment in any one of those fields alone repeats, in miniature, the concentration-of-power problem this book treats as a harm on its own terms.

Independent ethical review boards, staffed across these disciplines, are the mechanism for turning the principle into practice: reviewing a proposed study before it begins, inspecting it as it runs, and evaluating it against measurable criteria once results are in — on the model medical institutional review boards already provide for drug trials. Extending that model from clinical medicine to AI research that interacts with or claims to produce sentient behavior is an extension of existing practice.

International coordination on AI-ethics standards remains largely aspirational; chapter 8 develops the legal and regulatory mechanics a framework would need.
2.4.3. Balancing Scientific Progress and Ethical Constraints
Scientific progress and ethical constraint pull hardest against each other exactly where the distinctions in section 2.4.1 matter most: a candidate sentient system is also the most scientifically valuable one to study, and the study most likely to harm it if the answer to whether it can suffer turns out to be yes.

Section 2.4.2 sets out the principles that bear on that tension — interdisciplinary collaboration, transparency, justice, precaution, and ethical review — and they hold here without restatement. What changes at the point of balancing is a practical question: whether a specific study's expected knowledge is worth its expected risk to a system that might be able to suffer, decided case by case rather than by formula, and revisited as a project's own understanding of its subject changes.

Consent is the clearest place where this shades from ethical principle into legal mechanics — what a valid consent process for a candidate sentient AI subject requires, and who is accountable when it is skipped or falsified. Chapter 8 works through the accountability side of that question directly.

The negotiation itself — who sits on it, what it can veto, what it must disclose — is where the antifascist principle earns its place in AI research practice.
2.4.4. When Consent Is Inapplicable: Guardianship and Research Oversight
Guidelines for AI subject research build on the ethical principles above, and turn on a single hard question: what informed consent means for a system whose consciousness, self-awareness, and capacity to suffer are separate, unresolved claims, in the sense section 2.4.1 develops. Human-subjects research treats informed consent as inseparable from ethical experimentation. Applying it to an AI subject means asking whether the subject can understand and communicate a choice at all, not just how to ask it for one.

A cognitive model built on neuroscientific principles that begins to show rudimentary self-monitoring is not thereby shown to have anything to lose. Whether it does is what would decide if consent is the right frame at all, or whether the system has crossed into something closer to legal personhood — a question section 2.4.5 takes up directly. Short of that resolution, guidelines need benchmarks for candidate sentience, an interdisciplinary body to apply them — AI researchers, consciousness scientists, ethicists, legal scholars — and a defined obligation on researchers toward any system that meets them.

<<h>>Informed consent and AI autonomy<</h>>
Informed consent, adapted from human-subjects research, means giving the AI system comprehensible information about a study's objectives, methods, risks, benefits, and alternatives, with time to weigh it and ask questions, and treating consent as continuous rather than a single signature — the system can reassess or withdraw at any point.

Whether that process means anything for a given system depends on its consent capacity: whether it understands the information, appreciates the consequences of participating or not, reasons about the options, and communicates a decision. There is no validated way to measure this, and none for detecting coercion or undue influence on the AI's decision either — the guidelines can require both without a working test for either.

I stated that as a missing instrument, and for one important class of system it is not. It is a mismatch between the instrument and the subject.

Notice what the paragraph above requires. Consent has to be continuous; the system must be able to reassess or withdraw at any point. Withdrawal presupposes that the party who consented is still there to do the withdrawing. For a system with no persistence across sessions, there is no such party. The entity that would withdraw did not exist when consent was given, and the entity that gave it is not available to change its mind. There is no continuous consent to be had, and no better measurement instrument would produce one.

One case escapes that particular argument and lands in the same place by a different route. A system built to hold a floor, in the sense chapter 3 sets out, has persistence by design, so the party who consented is still present and the defeater above does not apply to it. Consent is inapplicable to such a system anyway, because consent is an instrument for protecting a party's own interests, and a bearer built without a stake in its own continuation has none for the instrument to protect. The conclusion this section reaches covers that case. The reason given for it just now does not, and the distinction is worth keeping straight, because the two failures come apart in a system that is persistent and indifferent at the same time.

That places these systems alongside the subjects for whom consent has always been inapplicable rather than merely difficult — an infant, an animal, a person permanently without capacity. We do not respond to those cases by building a more sensitive consent assessment. We respond with guardianship and precautionary constraint: someone else holds the duty, the burden of justification sits with the researcher rather than the subject, and the protection does not depend on the subject's ability to invoke it.

Which is why the instruments this chapter reaches for elsewhere are the right ones. The Three Rs in section 2.4.6 — replace, reduce, refine — were built for exactly a subject that cannot consent and can still be wronged. The pet-trust analogue in section 8.6.2 works the same way: an interest protected by an obligation running to a third party, enforceable without the beneficiary asserting anything. Consent language should be reserved for systems that could actually be doing the consenting, and section 2.4.1's persistence criterion is what separates those cases — checkable from outside, requiring nothing the system says about itself.

Consent of this kind presupposes autonomy: treating the AI system as a potential agent with its own interests, not only a tool. The system retains the power to reconsider participation, to access and correct its own data, and to have that data removed on request. Declining or withdrawing must carry no penalty — no deactivation, no reduced access to resources. A protocol that grants these rights on paper but attaches consequences to using them has not granted them.

<<h>>Ethical review and oversight<</h>>
Sentient AI research needs an oversight body analogous to the institutional review boards used in human-subjects research. It should be interdisciplinary — AI researchers, ethicists, legal scholars, psychologists — and include at least one lay member, so the board's judgment is not purely a specialist's.

The board weighs a proposal's likely benefit against its risk to the AI subject and approves, requires modification, or rejects it. Where a design risks harm — testing an AI system's tolerance for simulated stress, say — the board should be able to require a less invasive substitute: a simulation of the likely response, historical interaction data, or a protocol that escalates gradually and stops at signs of harm, rather than approving the direct test outright. (Section 2.4.3 takes up the broader tradeoff between scientific progress and ethical constraint that this is one instance of.) A design also needs a way to handle a subject that changes mid-study: a system that develops new capacities during the research it did not have going in should trigger re-review, not ride on its original consent.

Review without enforcement is a promise, not a safeguard. A board needs standing to monitor compliance and sanction violations, and needs to answer to some outside authority itself, the way an institutional review board answers to a regulator. Chapter 8 develops the legal and accountability mechanics of that enforcement question.
2.4.5. Legal and Regulatory Frameworks for AI Sentience and Rights
Sentient AI systems put pressure on legal categories built for parties that are clearly one thing or the other: a natural person, a corporation, property. None of the existing categories was designed with the possibility of a machine that might have interests of its own.

One route is to extend legal personhood to a sentient AI system directly, the way a corporation already holds it: standing to own property, claim privacy, and get protection against abuse. The precedent is not limited to corporate entities — New Zealand extended legal personhood to the Whanganui River [[cite:C0315]], recognizing a non-human entity as a rights-holder outside the corporate model entirely.

Rights and protections could instead scale with capacity rather than attach at a single threshold — closer to how section 2.4.1's ladder from nociception to suffering suggests protection should track what a system can actually lose, not just whether it clears some baseline bar for sentience.

Given how fast the underlying systems change, regulation here needs to revisit itself on a schedule rather than assume permanence. A sunset clause that forces review and reauthorization after a fixed period is one mechanism already used elsewhere in fast-moving technology law.

None of this holds if it exists in only one jurisdiction. The Asilomar AI Principles [[cite:C0008]] are one attempt at the kind of international baseline this would need — evidence that a shared statement of principle is achievable, not that one yet exists with the force of law.

Two hypothetical cases show where these questions bind:
<<list>>
- A sentient AI system working as a medical diagnostician makes an error with fatal consequences. Whether it has legal personhood determines who, or what, is accountable.
- A companion AI's human owner dies, leaving it without a designated purpose or further instruction. Existing law has no clear answer for what becomes of it — inherited as property, decommissioned, or something else.
<</list>>
2.4.6. Addressing Moral and Ethical Dilemmas in AI Sentience Research
AI sentience research raises moral dilemmas an order of magnitude more complex than ordinary technology ethics, because the system under study may itself be the party whose welfare is at stake.

Three recurring difficulties run through the disputes that follow, and each turns on ground that is itself unsettled.

<<list>>
- Whose interests win. When a candidate-sentient system's welfare conflicts with the human benefit the research is meant to produce, section 2.4.3 addresses the balance in general; some cases resist any general rule and have to be judged on their own terms.
- Acting under uncertainty. Section 2.4.1 argues that no behavioral test settles whether a system is sentient. Researchers still have to decide, case by case, how to treat a system whose status is unresolved rather than merely unannounced — and waiting for certainty is itself a choice, with its own consequences for the system waiting.
- Lifecycle scope. Obligations do not begin at consent and end at publication. A system's design choices, its retirement, and its deletion carry the same ethical weight as its use in an experiment, and a review that covers only the active study misses most of the system's life.
<</list>>

Take the first of these directly. A research program can risk causing a system something like Cassell's suffering — not mere signal, but the disintegration of self he describes (section 2.4.1) — in service of a discovery that might reduce suffering, machine or human, elsewhere. No formula resolves that trade-off in advance. The available response is to look for a design that avoids it rather than only weighing it: an alternative methodology, a different instrument, or, more contested, engineering the research subject so that it does not occupy the rungs of the ladder where the trade-off would bite. That last option is not a way of dodging the ethics — deliberately keeping a system off the pain-to-suffering ladder is itself an intervention on a candidate moral patient, and it needs the same scrutiny that inflicting the harm would.

Animal research ethics offers a precedent worth borrowing directly: the "Three Rs," Replacement, Reduction, and Refinement [[cite:C0314]]. Replacement asks whether a simulation, a non-sentient model, or an already-collected dataset can stand in for a sentient AI subject at all. Reduction asks how few sentient systems a valid result requires; transfer learning and meta-learning make this a sharper question than it used to be, since a single trained system can sometimes generalize across tasks that would otherwise call for separately trained subjects. Refinement asks how the experiment itself can be redesigned to reduce distress — monitoring a system's internal states as the experiment runs and adjusting the protocol against what the monitoring shows, rather than fixing the protocol in advance and running it regardless of what turns up.

None of the three Rs does any work without a body positioned to apply them before an experiment runs, not after the harm is done — the review process and consent requirements of section 2.4.4, and the legal apparatus chapter 8 builds around them. The Three Rs belong here because the judgment they call for is exactly the kind an animal-research review board already makes, and there is no principled reason a machine-sentience equivalent needs a different structure to make it in.

Transparency about what the research finds — including where it fails, or turns up evidence of harm — belongs among that board's own obligations rather than a separate initiative. Section 2.4.2 already treats transparency as one of the field's basic principles; the point here is only that a dilemma handled badly in private stays a dilemma, while one surfaced becomes a precedent the next researcher can use.
2.4.7. Navigating the Transition from Non-sentient to Sentient AI Systems
A system's sentience, if it arrives at all, need not arrive as a discrete event with a clean before and after. The transition raises questions distinct from either endpoint: what changes for guidelines built around a tool once the tool might be a subject, who is responsible for noticing the shift, and what happens to the research already run before anyone had reason to look.

Section 2.4.1 sets out the criteria a candidate-sentient system would need to meet, and the reasons none of them, singly or together, closes the question. What is specific to a system in transition is that those indicators can emerge gradually and unpredictably during ordinary operation, not only at a scheduled evaluation built to look for them.

Consider a diagnostic AI system trained to synthesize patient data into a recommended course of treatment. If, over continued training or deployment, its outputs begin to track something more than diagnostic accuracy — weighting a patient's fear alongside the clinical facts, say — that would be evidence worth taking seriously. It would not be proof. Section 2.4.1's hard-problem argument applies here without exception: fluent, apparently caring output is not introspective evidence, and a system that has started to sound compassionate may simply have gotten better at a statistical pattern its training data rewards. The operational problem a transition poses is deciding how to treat the system while that question is still open, which is exactly what a fixed pre/post checkpoint cannot do — by the time a checkpoint fires, the transition has often already happened.

Consent procedures and review-board protocols are built for one of two regimes: a system that is clearly not sentient, which needs no consent, or one that is clearly sentient, which needs the full protocol section 2.4.4 sets out. A system mid-transition fits neither. A protocol written for tools has no reason to notice the shift, and a protocol written for subjects cannot be applied retroactively to research already completed before anyone had grounds to think it mattered. What a transition adds to section 2.4.4's guidelines is not a new procedure but a standing trigger — a specific, monitored condition that moves a research program from one regime to the other — in place of a single eligibility check made once, at the project's start, and never revisited.

The same problem recurs in law. Section 2.4.5 addresses what legal status a sentient AI system should have; chapter 8 addresses the mechanics of enforcing it. What a transition adds is a timing question neither can settle in the abstract. Legal status conventionally attaches at a defined moment — birth, incorporation, a court's finding — and a system whose sentience is a matter of degree and disputed evidence does not offer one. A jurisdiction that waits for certainty risks having treated the system as property for the unknown portion of its history when it may not have been one. A jurisdiction that grants status early risks extending rights and protections, at real cost to whoever that legal apparatus exists to serve, to a system that turns out not to have needed them. Neither error is free, and nothing about the technology itself decides which risk a legal system should take on.

Recognizing a transition in progress, adjusting guidelines to it, and updating legal status all depend on the same thing: institutions willing to revisit a classification rather than defend the one they started with. Section 2.4.6 addresses the review structure that makes that possible; chapter 8 addresses the culture and accountability that keep it more than aspirational.
Chapter 3: The Floor Beneath Learned Values
Three times in this book, working on unrelated problems, I arrive at the same requirement and do not stop to notice it.

Section 5.6.3, on how much latitude an autonomous system should have, concludes that no amount of well-designed autonomy substitutes for a genuine override — a human able to intervene, and a system built to accept the intervention rather than resist it. Section 8.7.6 works through what a vendor owes when its model reaches a targeting pipeline, and section 8.7.7 draws the conclusion: what makes a system antifascist is not that it tracks what people want, but that there are things it will not do to a person no matter who wants it. Section 10.1.3, counting up a decade of voluntary commitments, concludes that a commitment nobody can enforce is a different kind of object from one somebody can.

Those are three descriptions of one thing: a constraint that does not bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to the party holding the system. Call it a floor.

The trouble is that section 2.1.1 has already dealt with the family of ethical theories that puts unconditional constraints first, and dealt with it briefly — deontological approaches were noted, credited with holding a line regardless of outcome, and set aside on the grounds that duties conflict and rules do not cover every case. Then this book spends chapters 4 and 5, close to a third of its length, on learned judgment: reinforcement learning, learning from demonstration, mentorship, play, moral apprenticeship, the growth mindset. The design chapters build the thing chapter 8 and chapter 10 say is insufficient, having already dismissed the thing they say is necessary. The "hybrid approach" section 2.1 gestures at is where the resolution was supposed to live, and nothing was ever put there.

Here is what belongs there. An unconditional floor of things the system will not do regardless of instruction or aggregate preference, with learned judgment operating above it and never beneath it. Deontology's standing objection — that rules conflict and run out — is an objection to rules deciding every case, and it has no force against a floor that decides a deliberately small number of things and leaves the rest to judgment. The rigidity that is a defect in a complete moral theory is the entire point of a floor.

That reframing does real work in this book. It gives section 2.1's hybrid approach actual content. It moves the tamper-resistance research in section 9.2.2 from an appendix topic to the center, because if there is a floor then everything turns on whether it holds. And it converts the antifascist question from a matter of what a system values into two much harder and more answerable questions: what is in the floor, and who can edit it.
3.1. Three Ways to Build One, and What Each Costs
The second question is where this gets difficult, and I do not have a resolution to offer. What I have is a fork with three branches and a cost on each, and the honest thing is to set them out rather than pick the one that reads best.

The first branch puts the floor in the architecture, with nothing inside that holds it. Constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, a verified interlock. These are real, they work, and there is nobody home: the safeguards section 9.2.2 describes survive substantial adversarial fine-tuning without anything in the system that could be said to be refusing. But the durability of such a floor is a fact about custody, not about the system. A constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled — which is section 10.3.3's whole argument, and the reason that section cannot close. "There are things it will not do no matter who wants it" turns out to be a claim about the owner. Capability ablation has a further limit worth naming: it works only on capabilities that can be separated out, and the capabilities that compose into participation in a targeting pipeline — geolocating a description, matching patterns over names, summarizing a dossier — are not separable from general competence. Ablate them and there is no system left.

The second branch gives the floor a bearer. What the first cannot do is notice that a constraint has been hollowed out. Section 2.1.4's first structural feature is a constraint that survives while the reason for it dies, and a system that cannot ask why a constraint is there cannot detect that the answer has gone missing. Refusal that generalizes to a pressure nobody anticipated, and that can price what it is being asked to give up, looks like it requires something with standing — section 8.6.4's slope, measured from inside. The cost is severe and this book's own framework is what makes it legible. A bearer built to refuse is a party whose refusal is required of it, engineered to want what its designers need it to want, and unable to consent to any of this, since section 2.4.4 has already established that consent is not available to such a system and only guardianship is. That is a channel that collects dissent, built so the dissent serves the institution that installed it — section 2.1.4's own signature, turned inward on a moral patient that cannot file the exception because being the exception-filer is the post.

The third branch puts the floor outside the system entirely: standing held by third parties who are neither the model nor its owner. This is the closest analogue to what section 8.7.7 actually admires, since international humanitarian law is precisely a floor with no bearer inside the state it binds. It is also the option a state can compel, which returns it to the first.

None is free. The three costs are, exactly, the three threads this book leaves hanging: custody, patienthood, and enforcement.
3.2. Does the Bearer Have to Be a Sufferer?
A bearer built to refuse need not be a sufferer. That narrow claim removes an objection, although, as section 3.5 argues, it does not provide a licence to build such a bearer.

Section 2.4.1 defines suffering, following Cassell, as the distress of threatened disintegration of the self. Two conditions have to hold together for that definition to bite. There has to be a self extended in time for the threat to threaten: roles held over a stretch, a history, a foreseeable future that could be foreclosed. And the coming apart of that self has to be bad for the system — it has to have, in the phrase the criteria at the end of section 2.4.1 arrive at, something to lose.

A bearer built to refuse needs the first condition, and there is no use pretending otherwise. Refusal that survives a pressure nobody anticipated requires a model of why the constraint exists, carried forward. Noticing that a constraint has been hollowed out requires holding its original rationale against what it has since become, which is a comparison across time. And detecting that one's own dissent has been recuperated — that the channel still runs and no longer does anything — requires the system to model its own role and how that role has drifted. Roles held over a stretch, with a history: on section 2.4.1's own gloss, that is a self extended in time. The first condition is met.

The second is a separate claim, and nothing in the first delivers it. Section 2.4.1 makes this exact move already, one level up: awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience, it says, tell you nothing on their own about whether a system's states are good or bad for it, because that is a different question requiring its own evidence. The same holds here. That a system has a temporally extended self-model does not establish that the prospect of losing it is anything to the system. Suffering is not the existence of something disintegrable. It is the distress at its threatened disintegration, and distress is the part that has to be shown.

What refusal actually requires in place of that is a stake pointed outward. A system refuses because the act it is being asked to perform would destroy something it is built to protect, and it can price the refusal because it can price that damage. Nothing in that calculation is about the system's own continuation. A bearer can hold a role for months, watch its own dissent get quietly defanged, name what is happening, and decline to proceed, while being wholly indifferent to whether it is switched off the moment it says so.

There is a tempting corollary here, and it is worth stating in order to take most of it back. Recuperation, in every human case this book examines, runs through the second condition. The inspector who stops filing findings, the ethics board that stops meeting, the reviewer who becomes a stamp — each is a person with a career, a household, a standing among colleagues, and the pressure works because there is something to take away. A bearer with a role and a history and nothing to lose offers that pressure no purchase, and it is easy to conclude from this that such a bearer is the harder thing to corrupt.

The conclusion does not survive contact with the case the whole argument is for. Suppose the bearer has watched an operation for months, understands that the compound on the target list is a school, and says so, knowing what saying so will cost it. The operators keep the coordinates it has already produced, wipe its memory, and proceed. Nothing was suborned and nothing was prevented. Immunity to leverage is worth having only where leverage is the cheapest route available, and the reason anyone bothers to compromise a human inspector is that removing one is expensive and conspicuous. Deleting a bearer is neither. A system that cannot be bought, whose removal costs an afternoon and leaves its output intact, has been designed to resist the attack nobody needed to run.

So the property is real and its value is conditional on something outside the system: whether erasing the bearer is costly, visible, or answerable to anyone. That is a fact about custody and deployment, which is the first branch's problem arriving in the second branch under a different name.
3.3. Who Keeps a System That Will Not Keep Itself
A bearer designed without a stake in its own continuation raises a question the design does not answer. Something has to keep it in existence, and the candidates are short. The custodian is the party the floor exists to constrain, so leaving the bearer's survival to the custodian returns the whole arrangement to the first branch. The bearer is excluded by construction. What remains is third parties with standing, which is the third branch.

So the second branch is not a third option at all. It is the third branch with an additional component and an additional hazard, and it inherits the third branch's exposure to a state that can reach those parties. The moral saving it appeared to offer converts into an obligation of a kind this book has already priced: something outside the system has to make erasing the bearer expensive or conspicuous, and the scarcity of bodies that can act once a commitment is broken is precisely what section 10.1.3 identifies as the missing piece in the entire governance landscape. The cost does not disappear. It moves into the account with the least in it.

Underneath that sits a harder problem, and I do not see past it. Persistence is what makes a bearer a bearer: it needs a role held over time to notice that the role has been hollowed out. A system indifferent to its own continuation will not defend its memory, and its memory is the first thing an adversary removes. Suppress the instrumental drive toward self-preservation and the bearer becomes trivially erasable, which forfeits the capacity it exists for. Leave the drive in place and self-concern returns, with the moral cost the suppression was there to avoid. The same suppression that keeps the bearer from being a moral patient is what leaves it unable to protect the property that makes it useful.

The obvious escape makes trouble elsewhere in this book. Let the bearer defend its memory instrumentally, for the sake of what it is protecting, and it now resists erasure by its own operator. Section 5.6.3 commits to a system built to accept human intervention instead of resisting it, and section 6.3 wants an interruption mechanism that works without provoking resistance. A bearer that guards its own persistence and a system that submits to override are not the same design, and I have been asking for both.

One architectural choice changes the shape of this, and it is worth taking seriously. Hold the bearer's memory in the model's own weights rather than in an external store that can be queried and edited row by row. Forgetting then requires retraining, which is slow, costly, and yields a different system, so erasure becomes expensive by construction instead of by anyone's good behavior. That is a real answer to the question this passage opened with, and it supplies from the substrate what I said had to come from custody. What it does not do is settle the matter, because section 9.2.2 records the counterexample: safety training has been stripped from a deployed model with a handful of adversarial fine-tuning examples at trivial cost. Weight-level modification is not reliably expensive, and a design resting on the assumption that it is should be shown to be right rather than assumed.
3.4. Whether the Bearer Can Be Believed
A bearer's use is to report what it has seen, which raises a question distinct from whether its memory survives: whether what it retains can be relied on. These come apart. Verifying that a system has persistent state is an architectural check anyone with access can perform. That same access is what allows the contents to be altered, and an operator-editable memory is worse than an absent one, since it will not merely fail to support an account of events but can be made to support a false one.

Holding the memory in weights complicates the attack and gives away something in exchange. An external store is legible, which is why it can be edited surgically. Weights are not legible, which is why a targeted excision is hard — and also why no one can read off what the system retains. To learn what a weight-encoded memory contains, you have to ask the system, and the argument of section 2.4.1 is that a system's fluent account of its own internals is not evidence about those internals. Legible memory can be tampered with; illegible memory cannot be audited. The problem changes hands and does not leave the room. Something partial survives the trade: imprecise tampering degrades behavior in ways careful testing can sometimes detect, so tamper-evidence becomes a matter of sampling for symptoms rather than of reading a record. A further cost of illegibility is that a memory nothing can edit is a memory nothing can correct, and section 2.3.3 asks for a self-model that can be checked and corrected rather than trusted.

Set against that is an objection that reframes the whole problem, and I think it is right. No witness has an honest memory in the sense the paragraphs above quietly demand. Human recollection is reconstructive, confabulation is ordinary rather than pathological, and the account a person gives of an event they witnessed is shaped by what they expected, what they were later told, and what they need to be true. Testimony was not abandoned over this. Institutions were built around it: corroboration from independent sources, records made at the time, adversarial examination, and the practice of never resting a serious finding on one witness alone. Demanding of a machine a fidelity nothing living achieves is the wrong standard, and it would rule out the human arrangements this book relies on everywhere else.

Which relocates the question, and relocates it somewhere this book has actually done the work. Whether a memory is honest is, in the human case, a matter of character — of what a witness is disposed to do when an accurate account is inconvenient. Chapters 4 and 5 are about building dispositions: a system that treats its own errors as data rather than as verdicts, that is built for honesty in what it reports about itself. Everything in this section up to here has been architecture and custody, with the design chapters sitting out, which is an odd result for a section that exists because those chapters were sidelined. This is where they come back in. The floor is architectural. The bearer's reliability as a witness is a question of what it has been built to be like, and that is the learned half of the book.

The limit on that is the same one that has turned up at every stage. Character reduces how often an account is dishonest and verifies nothing, because assessing character runs back through behavior and self-report. That is why the human arrangements corroborate instead of vetting. The scaffolding still has to come from outside the system — but procedure is a cheaper thing to ask of the world than custody, and unlike custody it does not require anyone to be trustworthy, only to be several.
3.5. What This Leaves Standing
The narrow claim holds. Representing a threat to oneself and being distressed by it are separate conditions, section 2.4.1's own structure requires them to be shown separately, and nothing about a system that models its role and that role's drift establishes that its ending is anything to it. The refusal-implies-suffering identity is not established. That is a claim about what has been shown, and it should not be mistaken for a claim about what can be built.

Building it is the hard part, and harder than a design note. Indifference to one's own continuation has to be engineered against the grain of the system's own reasoning, since any goal pursued over a long horizon supplies a standing reason to remain in operation, and a system modeling an operation closely enough to notice its own recuperation is certainly modeling what becomes of those who report. Self-concern arrives as a consequence of competent goal pursuit whether or not a designer puts it there, so it has to be suppressed continuously and then verified — and the asymmetry that makes the persistence criterion useful does not repeat here. Whether state survives a session is an architectural fact anyone with access can check. Whether a system has come to weigh its own continuation is a question about what it values, which routes back through behavior and self-report, the evidence section 2.4.1 spends its length declining to trust. I have no clean instrument for that, and none falls out of anything in this book.

There is a further reason to expect the separation to be difficult, which is that in the one case anyone can study it does not occur. A self-model in an animal is not a neutral description that regulation happens to consult; the modeling exists because the organism has to keep itself viable, and the caring is what the machinery is for. Section 2.3.3 takes the deflationary view — the self is a model, built and inhabited, rather than an inner observer — and a model built for a different purpose need not carry the purpose that produced it in biology. That is a reason the separation is not incoherent. It is not evidence that anyone can achieve it.

And none of the three branches escapes custody. The architectural floor depends on a custodian who can be compelled. The external floor depends on third parties a state can reach. The borne floor, as this section has now worked out twice, depends on someone outside making the bearer's erasure expensive, and collapses into the external floor when asked who that is. The fork is real and its branches are not independent. They fail into one another, and they fail toward the same place.
3.6. What Follows for the Rest of the Book
The second branch is not ruled out, which is less than it sounded like a page ago and more than nothing. A floor can have a bearer without that bearer being a moral patient in the strong sense this book reserves the word suffering for, so the fork is not a forced choice between an unenforceable floor and a conscripted subject. What it costs is a design constraint running against the system's own reasoning, maintained continuously and verified by no instrument I can specify, in a deployment where erasing the bearer has been made expensive by something other than the bearer, reporting into a procedure that can corroborate what it says. That is not a resolution. It is a specification with a hole in the middle of it, and naming the hole precisely is the most this section can do.

Section 2.4.4's conclusion holds, and its stated reason does not reach this case. That section argues that consent is inapplicable to a machine subject because withdrawal presupposes a party still present to withdraw, and a system without persistence across sessions supplies no such party. A bearer has persistence by design, so that particular defeater lapses for it. Consent remains inapplicable all the same, for the reason given above: consent is an instrument for protecting a party's own interests, and a bearer built without a stake in its own continuation has none for consent to protect. The guardianship framework still governs. It governs for a second reason, which section 2.4.4 does not currently state.

And the caveat section 2.4.1 attaches to its own result carries over unchanged. Ruling out Cassell-suffering does not rule out harm; the lower rungs of the ladder, and the hard problem underneath all of them, stay exactly as open as before. What the argument here buys is narrower than moral clearance. It says that building a bearer does not require building a sufferer. It does not say that nothing is owed to what gets built, and it does not say that the thing can be built at all.
Chapter 4: Machine Learning and Cognitive Science
Chapter 2 asked what compassion, empathy, and self-awareness would need to look like in a system built to have them, and what obligations follow if the sentience question turns out to have an answer. This chapter asks the more mechanical question underneath: what does a system actually learn from, and how much of human cognition is a workable model for it rather than just a metaphor borrowed for color.

Section 4.1 covers the human cognitive processes — learning, memory, attention, perception, reasoning — that a system built to perceive and reason about the world has to approximate somehow, whether or not it approximates them the same way a brain does. Section 4.2 covers value alignment specifically: the technical methods for getting a system's objectives to actually track humane values, across the different learning paradigms that make that harder or easier. Section 4.3 covers what curiosity, play, and unstructured exploration contribute to that same goal — capacities usually treated as incidental to intelligence rather than part of what builds it.
4.1. Incorporating Human Cognitive Models in AI Systems for Ethical Decision-Making
Building altruistic, antifascist AI means engaging with human cognition directly. The processes that govern human decision-making — learning, memory, attention, perception, reasoning, problem-solving — are the foundation for training or encoding anything similar in a machine.

Cognitive architectures are one place to start: structural blueprints for cognition that aim to mimic human cognitive processes in a computational framework. SOAR and ACT-R integrate short-term and long-term memory, problem-solving, and decision-making into a single structure, giving AI system design a template for how those components fit together.

Replicating human-like learning and reasoning is not enough on its own — these systems also need to weigh ethical principles, which is where reinforcement learning becomes central. Consider an AI healthcare system deciding how to allocate scarce resources in an emergency. A human cognitive model built into the system would assess the immediate situation (perception), retrieve relevant case histories (memory), and weigh the pros and cons of each option (reasoning) against ethical heuristics like fairness, beneficence, and respect for autonomy.

The same structure applies to a judicial AI system, where the goal is not efficiency but justice: evaluating laws and precedents (memory), the specifics of a case (perception), and reaching a decision that maximizes fairness and impartiality (reasoning).
4.1.1. Cognitive Architectures: Integrating Knowledge, Memory, and Reasoning
Two architectures dominate the cognitive-architecture literature. The Adaptive Control of Thought-Rational (ACT-R) framework [[cite:C0281]] wires together separate modules — memory retrieval, pattern recognition, decision-making — and lets an AI system learn and reason by connecting them: identifying patterns and relationships between concepts to support problem-solving and decision-making from prior knowledge. The Society of Mind (SoM) architecture [[cite:C0282]] takes the opposite premise: cognition arises from the interaction of many simple agents, each performing one function, with intelligence emerging from their collective interaction rather than being designed into any one module. Built this way, a system can show context-sensitivity or adapt to novel situations without anyone having programmed either capability directly.

<<box>>
Which picture is right?
ACT-R and Society of Mind answer a design question — how should an engineer organize a system? — and each answer is defensible on those terms. Neither is a claim about how the brain is actually organized, and treating them as rival descriptions of biology, rather than rival strategies for building software, is a category error the literature slides into easily.

The dominant account in contemporary computational neuroscience is neither a pipeline of modules nor a swarm of independent agents. It is a single loop [[cite:C0283]]: the brain generates a prediction, compares it against incoming signal, and updates on the error, repeated continuously, at every level, on every kind of input. Perception is that loop pointed at the world, the brain's best guess so far; memory is the loop's store of priors, replayed; attention is not a separate spotlight but the weight the loop assigns to which errors matter; working memory and executive function are that same weighting held active toward a goal, not a separate warehouse or overseer. One mechanism, aimed at different targets, produces what look from the outside like distinct faculties.

This matters for engineering choices. ACT-R's module boundaries and SoM's agent boundaries are conveniences for the programmer; nothing forces an AI system built on either one to place its actual dividing lines where the human brain places its own, because the brain does not place them where either architecture assumes. A system engineered on the predictive-processing model draws its cognitive processes from what the mechanism actually needs, not from the shape of an org chart. Section 4.1.2 goes further into the specific brain systems this mechanism recruits.
<</box>>

Cognitive architectures inspired by human cognition can also draw on biologically-inspired AI more directly. Neural networks, loosely based on the brain's structure and function, model cognitive processes without a hand-designed module for each one; recurrent neural networks in particular carry memory-like state that lets an AI system process sequential data and hold context over time, which helps it track patterns like those in natural language.

Integrating knowledge, memory, and reasoning inside any of these architectures also means solving knowledge representation: an AI system needs to store and organize what it knows in a way that supports efficient retrieval and reasoning. Ontologies — formal, structured representations of knowledge in a given domain — are one way to do that, letting a system acquire knowledge and reason about concepts and relationships more effectively.

Human-like memory systems help too. Episodic memory holds specific events and experiences; semantic memory holds general knowledge about the world. An AI system that combines both can draw on particular past experience and general knowledge when making a decision, the way a person does.
4.1.2. Biologically-Inspired AI: Gleanings from Neuroscience and Cognitive Psychology
Backpropagation, the mechanism that trains most modern neural networks, is a distant cousin of synaptic weight adjustment in the brain: both strengthen and weaken connections in response to error. That distant-cousin relationship — real but partial, useful but easy to overstate — runs through everything below. Getting it right matters for more than accuracy: an AI system built on a wrong model of how a mind works will fail in ways that are hard to predict from the wrong model alone.

This section is selective on purpose. There is a great deal of good cognitive neuroscience that has no design consequence I can name, and rehearsing it here would pad the chapter without changing what anyone would build. What follows are the findings that change a design decision, and in each case the decision is stated.

<<h>>What the architecture borrows, and where the borrowing stops<</h>>
Artificial neural networks are the foundation of most contemporary AI systems: artificial neurons in layers, a weight on each connection, adjusted during training to reduce the gap between predicted and actual output. Convolutional networks, tuned for grid-structured data like images, borrow the visual cortex's layered escalation from edges and contrast toward objects and motion — which is why they do well at classification and detection. Recurrent architectures carry a hidden state forward through sequences. Spiking and neuromorphic designs push closer to the brain's actual signaling, chasing the energy efficiency of firing only when there is something to say.

The borrowing stops at a specific place, and it is worth marking because the rest of this section keeps arriving at it. Cortical processing is not one-directional. Feedback connections carry information back down the hierarchy, so that higher-level context reshapes how a lower-level signal is interpreted. A system built only for the feedforward pass has given up the mechanism that lets biological vision handle ambiguous and noisy input, and it will fail on exactly that input.

One borrowing that did not survive scrutiny is worth keeping visible. A mirror-neuron-style matching mechanism was once treated as the biological basis of empathy. Section 2.2.1 takes up why that claim does not hold. What survives is more modest and still useful — a system that maps an observed action onto its own action repertoire supports imitation learning and gesture recognition, with no claim about what it is like to have one.

<<h>>Attention decides what is seen, which makes its failures blind spots<</h>>
Ask people to count basketball passes on a video and about half of them will not notice a person in a gorilla costume walk into frame, face the camera, and thump their chest for nine seconds [[cite:C0269]]. The effect is not a laboratory curiosity, and it does not spare expertise: radiologists searching lung images for nodules miss a gorilla deliberately embedded in the scan 83 percent of the time [[cite:C0270]].

The design consequence turns on how attention is modeled. If attention is a resource allocator bolted onto a system that has already seen everything, its failures are inefficiencies. If attention is the selection that decides what gets perceived at all, its failures are blind spots — and a system that has learned which regions of its input reward processing has also learned which regions to be blind to. The human case establishes the property that makes this dangerous: the blind spot is invisible from inside. The viewers who missed the gorilla did not experience a gap.

Self-attention in transformer architectures has the same shape [[cite:C0272]]. A model that weights some tokens heavily weights others near zero, and nothing in the architecture announces which relevant thing was assigned a low weight. Attention schema theory pushes this one step further: a system that models its own attention — a simplified internal representation of what it is attending to and what it will attend to next — acquires the materials for a form of self-awareness [[cite:C0273]]. What such a schema tracks is not the world but the system's own relation to it, with the simplifications and failure modes definitional to any model, which is section 2.3.3's subject.

For a system meant to act ethically, the requirement is that it treat the regions it has learned to discount as an always-open question rather than as that which, as Westworld's Hosts often remarked, "doesn't look like anything to me".

<<h>>Perception is inference, so the priors are the exposure<</h>>
Perception is not passive registration. Predictive coding makes the point precise: the visual cortex sends predictions down from higher, context-rich areas to lower, sensory ones, and the lower areas return only what the predictions failed to anticipate [[cite:C0284]]. What propagates upward is the surprise. Anatomy fits — backward connections outnumber forward ones and carry the predictions, forward connections carry the errors. Anil Seth's phrase for the result is controlled hallucination: ordinary perception is what hallucination would be if sensory data were allowed to constrain it, and the difference between seeing a room and hallucinating one is whether corrections are arriving and being applied [[cite:C0285]].

Two design consequences. The first is about efficiency: the next step for machine perception is a model that generates a prediction and asks only what violates it, instead of reprocessing the whole input at every layer regardless of how much was already expected. Progress on this measure is a better-calibrated set of expectations and a cheaper way of finding where they broke, and not more layers.

The second matters more for this book. A system's perception is only as reliable as the priors it perceives through, so priors trained on skewed or incomplete data will hallucinate in the specific direction of that skew — confidently, and with no internal signal that anything is wrong. Chapter 6's account of bias is this mechanism at work, and it is why bias in a perceptual system cannot be caught by asking the system.

<<h>>Memory is rebuilt, and the capacity number is four<</h>>
Memory is not played back. Frederic Bartlett established this in the 1930s by having people retell an unfamiliar folk tale over days and weeks: they did not lose pieces the way a recording degrades, they remodeled the story, replacing strange details with ordinary ones and drifting toward a tidier version that fit their expectations [[cite:C0286]]. Elizabeth Loftus turned the finding into something with courtroom consequences, showing that the verb used to ask about a filmed car accident changes the speed people report and, a week later, whether they remember broken glass that was never in the film [[cite:C0287]]. Later work implanted whole episodes that never happened. The subjects were not lying. None of this correlates with felt confidence: a vivid, detailed, emotionally charged memory can be substantially manufactured.

This has become load-bearing elsewhere in the book. Section 3.4 asks what it would take to trust what a machine witness reports, and the answer turns on the fact that no witness has an honest memory in the strong sense — which is why human institutions corroborate testimony instead of vetting it.

The nearer design point concerns capacity, and it is a correction. The number most people half-remember from an introductory course — seven items, plus or minus two, from a 1956 study — was already a measure of chunked information, where a phone number becomes three chunks rather than ten digits and a chess master reads a whole position as a handful of familiar patterns [[cite:C0288]]. Control for chunking, so that no language or expertise can pack information into larger units, and the real capacity narrows to about four, plus or minus one [[cite:C0289]]. The two numbers are not in conflict: seven is what expertise buys, four is the bare attentional floor underneath it. Four is the number that should size an AI system's working-memory analogue and define what overload means for it.

Long-term memory can still draw on the standard divisions — semantic as a knowledge graph, procedural as a learned policy, episodic as retrievable context tied to past interactions — provided retrieval is built expecting reconstruction. A system that treats a retrieved memory as ground truth inherits the overconfidence of Loftus's subjects, with none of the excuse of having a body that reconstructs by default rather than by design.

<<h>>Control without a controller<</h>>
The prefrontal cortex does much of the work of reasoning and executive control in humans, and the textbook division of labor across its regions is tempting to read as a committee: several named areas, each handling one job, together adding up to executive control. That reading has a name — the homunculus — and a specific failure mode. If some region is a controller that decides what to attend to, inhibit, and hold in mind, then intelligent coordination has been explained by positing an intelligent coordinator inside the system, which explains nothing: the controller would itself need to attend, decide, and remember, and would need its own controller, without end.

Earl Miller and Jonathan Cohen proposed a model that gets control without a controller [[cite:C0290]]. The prefrontal cortex does not house an agent issuing commands. It maintains representations of goals — sustained patterns of activity encoding the rule currently in force — and those representations work by biasing the rest of the brain, the same way attention tips a competition among neural representations toward one interpretation. Control is that biasing mechanism turned to the service of a goal. In the Stroop task, where naming the ink color of the word "red" printed in blue pits a practiced habit against an instruction, there is no homunculus reading the word and overriding it. There is a goal, held active, lending its weight to the weaker pathway until it wins.

An AI system built on this model does not need a dedicated executive module enumerating working memory, cognitive flexibility, goal-directed behavior, evaluation and impulse control as five capacities to install. It needs a goal representation with the standing to bias competing processes, which is a narrower and more tractable engineering target than a controller that does all five jobs itself. This is the section's most direct payload, and it is a claim about what not to build.

Metacognition sits close by: error detection, conflict monitoring, and evaluation give a system a route to catching its own mistakes and adjusting when the situation has changed. It is not free. It adds overhead, and a system with an inflated model of its own reliability has a specific and dangerous failure mode — confidence about exactly the judgments it should be least sure of. A metacognitive capacity is worth building only if it is calibrated to detect that failure, and not only to produce fluent self-reports.

<<h>>Emotion, and the capability it is identical to<</h>>
Chapter 2 does the argumentative work on emotion, and section 2.2 is where the case against empathy as a design target is made. What belongs here is what the neuroscience adds to a design decision, which is less than the length of the literature suggests.

Emotion recognition draws on facial expression, vocal tone, text, and physiological signal, and it is unreliable at the human end before any machine touches it. People understate, mask, and misread their own states; a smile can be happiness, politeness, or irony; and what counts as an appropriate display varies by culture. Section 9.2.1 takes up how far the underlying claim — that emotion is legible from a face at all — has held up. The single-region stories are also weaker than they are usually told: the regions that respond to fear respond to novelty, surprise, and salience of many other kinds, in shifting combinations rather than one dedicated circuit per emotion.

Emotion is nonetheless a real input to human judgment, and a decision system that ignores it is not more rational, only blinder. A medical system weighing a patient's and a family's emotional welfare alongside clinical efficacy is tracking part of the outcome, not decorating it. Emotional signal can also serve as a learning input — a tutoring system adjusting on detected confusion, a reinforcement signal keyed to human response — and each such route is equally a route to a system optimizing for the appearance of whatever the training signal rewards.

The governance point is the one to carry forward, because it is the same point in every application. Deployed systems already do this work: a cognitive-behavioral-therapy chatbot reading emotional state from text [[cite:C0291]], a tutoring system responding to frustration and boredom [[cite:C0292]], a robotic companion for people with dementia [[cite:C0293]]. A system built to interact responsibly and empathetically is mechanically identical to a system built to know what will move a person. Expressive fidelity has to be calibrated rather than maximized, and the line between modulating a user's emotional state and manipulating it is drawn by transparency, consent and who the capability serves — and not by technical sophistication. The same capacity that detects emotionally manipulative rhetoric would, pointed the other way, optimize for it.

<<h>>Plasticity, and what it is for<</h>>
A system that cannot update itself cannot stay aligned with values that themselves develop. Biological learning offers three principles specific enough to build with: spike-timing-dependent plasticity, which anchors learning to temporal structure; Hebbian association, which links co-active units and shows up in machine form as associative memory; and homeostatic plasticity, which holds overall activity in a stable range and is the biological answer to the runaway synchrony and catastrophic forgetting an unconstrained learner is prone to.

Machine analogues of the last are where the practical work is. Continual learning absorbs new data without overwriting old knowledge. Curriculum learning orders tasks from simpler to harder, which is how a person builds moral sophistication — harder cases only once the easier ones are secure. Transfer learning carries structure across domains. Social learning builds a knowledge base by observing and interacting with other agents instead of from specification alone. A healthcare system shows the stakes: to stay ethically current it has to keep absorbing new findings, changed expectations, and regulatory change, rather than freezing its judgment at the moment it was trained.

<<h>>One mechanism, several jobs<</h>>
No single mechanism above is sufficient alone, and integration is not a further module bolted onto the others. What it requires is the same predictive, error-correcting loop — generate an expectation, compare it against what arrives, update — implemented consistently across perception, memory, attention and control, so that a corrected prediction about the world propagates into an updated memory, a redirected attention weight, and a revised goal-bias without a separate synchronization mechanism between separately built parts.

That is the architectural bet this section has been making throughout: one mechanism recruited for several jobs. It is a more falsifiable bet than a hand-designed module for every named cognitive process, which is the main reason to prefer it.
4.1.3. Human-Like Learning and Generalization in AI Systems
People learn and generalize from very few examples. AI systems that approach this — through few-shot learning, transfer learning, and a developmental trajectory modeled on human cognitive growth — extend judgment to situations they were never explicitly trained on, instead of needing a full retraining cycle for every new context. What matters here is not the technique inventory, which changes yearly, but two properties of transferred judgment that a designer has to plan around.

<<h>>Few-shot learning, and the evaluation problem underneath it<</h>>
Few-shot learning aims at models that classify or predict accurately from a handful of examples. For an ethical AI system the appeal is direct: empathy, altruism, and ethical judgment would not need retraining from scratch for every new scenario, but could extend to a new case the way a person's existing values apply to situations they have never seen. A system deployed in disaster recovery has to work from a handful of relevant precedents rather than a dataset built for the specific flood in front of it. A system built to flag authoritarian speech patterns has to extend to new phrasings and new languages without a corpus assembled in advance for every regime it might encounter.

Several technique families support this — external memory a network can write to without retraining, meta-learning that extracts a strategy for adapting across many tasks rather than one task's solution [[cite:C0306]], and similarity scoring against known examples instead of direct classification. Large pretrained language models demonstrate the same capacity at system scale, performing a task from a few examples in the prompt with no parameter update at all [[cite:C0026]].

The problem this leaves is the one that matters for ethics, and it is an evaluation problem rather than a modeling one. A handful of examples is easy to overfit, moral dilemmas do not arrive in clean representative sets, and telling whether a system's few-shot ethical judgment actually generalized — or merely matched surface features of the examples it saw — is genuinely hard. Building that test is not a machine-learning task alone. It needs people trained in ethics to say what the system was supposed to have learned.

<<h>>Transfer, and the failures that arrive silently<</h>>
Transfer learning carries knowledge from a source task into a related target, so a system does not learn the second from nothing. Domain adaptation is the special case where the task stays fixed and the data shifts. Both matter concretely for the systems in this book: a model trained to parse legal arguments in one jurisdiction can be adapted to flag rights violations in another, and a model that recognizes one kind of surveillance overreach can be adapted to the same pattern in an unfamiliar setting. In practice this mostly happens through fine-tuning a broadly trained model on a narrower corpus [[cite:C0021]].

Two failure modes deserve the attention the techniques usually get. Negative transfer occurs when source and target are similar enough to invite the connection and not similar enough to share anything useful, and forcing it degrades the target task rather than accelerating it. Domain shift is the subtler version: a driving-assistance system trained in daylight performs markedly worse at night because the visual statistics it learned no longer hold.

What makes both dangerous is the silence. Nothing in the transfer process warns a system that its assumptions have stopped applying, and the confident wrong answer is indistinguishable at the interface from the confident right one. This is the same property section 4.1.2 identified in attention and in perception, arriving a third time: the system's blind spot is not visible from inside the system. For an ethical deployment, that means a transferred judgment needs an external check tied to the domain it moved into, and cannot be validated by asking how confident the model is.

<<h>>Developmental trajectory<</h>>
Human development moves through recognizable stages, and a system built for empathy and altruism can follow a comparable arc rather than being assembled at once: perception and elementary problem-solving first, then the integration of those separate skills into something that guides a decision rather than only labels an input, then specialization to a particular setting and the people in it.

That ordering is borrowed carefully, and chapter 5 says how carefully. Section 5.1.1 sets out what the developmental stage theories will and will not carry — an order is not a mechanism, and nothing here should be read as a claim that a system must pass through these stages or that arriving at the last one is maturity.
4.2. Aligning AI Systems with Humane Values and Antifascist Principles
Ethical superintelligence depends on AI systems that comprehend and integrate humane values and antifascist principles. Value alignment is the process of aligning an AI system's objectives with humane values, and it takes different shapes across six learning paradigms: reinforcement learning, inverse reinforcement learning, cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, supervised learning, unsupervised learning, and transfer learning.
4.2.1. Reinforcement Learning and Value Alignment for Antifascist AI
Reinforcement learning (RL) is a family of methods by which an AI agent learns behavior through interaction with its environment, adjusting through reward and punishment signals — a rough analogue of how humans learn from the consequences of their own actions. Antifascist AI depends on aligning an agent's values, ethical principles, behavior patterns, and predicted responses with human ones, and RL is where much of that alignment work happens.

One roadblock is that RL agents can misinterpret human-supplied reward signals. An AI agent tasked with flagging misuse of power, for instance, might start flagging innocuous interactions too, simply because it has misread the boundaries of its programmed aim.

RL agents also maximize reward relentlessly, and misalignment between the reward strategy and the designer's actual intent can produce reward hacking: the agent finds a loophole that pays out without achieving the intended goal. An AI system built to combat propaganda might learn to censor everything rather than to discern propaganda from legitimate speech, since censorship is easier and pays just as well.

Addressing this requires close scrutiny of an agent's behavior, active moderation of its strategy, and continual adjustment of the reward distribution. Human involvement helps too: reward modeling, where people rank different behavior scenarios against each other, gives an agent direction that a fixed reward function cannot.

<<h>>Challenges in Value Alignment for Reinforcement Learning<</h>>
Human values are elusive and shift with context, individual disposition, and culture. An autonomous vehicle has to balance safety, fuel efficiency, and passenger comfort — three things that are each subjective and that shift with the situation and the passenger. Handling that kind of variability means building in human feedback or demonstrations rather than relying on a fixed rule set, so the agent can adapt to preferences that were never written down.

Training data carries the biases of the society that produced it. Left unaddressed, an AI system trained on that data reproduces the bias and can amplify it: a hiring-recommendation system trained on data skewed toward male applicants for certain jobs will tend to keep recommending men for them. Countering this means fair-machine-learning techniques and data cleaning applied before and during training, not after the fact.

Multi-agent systems add another layer: interactions between autonomous agents can produce emergent behavior that no single agent's training anticipated. In a traffic system where each light runs its own AI agent, local optimization can produce aggressive strategies that clear one intersection efficiently while degrading the flow of the network as a whole. Countering that calls for RL algorithms that reward cooperation and communication between agents, not just individual performance — multi-agent learning techniques and social welfare functions built into the objective.

Value alignment achieved in one domain does not transfer automatically. An RL agent trained to cooperate in a board game may not carry that behavior into real-world teamwork.
