SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) draws on the Shapley value from cooperative game theory — a way of dividing credit fairly among players who contributed jointly to an outcome — to assign each input feature a consistent measure of importance across model types [[cite:C0077]]. In a medical-diagnosis system, a SHAP value can show which patient attributes — age, blood pressure, medical history — carried the most weight in a given diagnosis, giving a clinician something specific to evaluate.

Decision trees and rule-based systems trade some of the predictive power of a black-box model for interpretability that comes built in: a decision can be read directly off a sequence of human-readable rules or tree branches, which matters in domains like finance, medicine, or law enforcement, where the reasoning has to survive scrutiny on its own terms and not just the outcome.

Counterfactual explanation asks what would have had to be different: in a credit-approval system, identifying the smallest change to an applicant's financial profile that would have flipped the decision tells the applicant what mattered and gives the institution a check on whether that factor should have mattered as much as it did.

Visualization and interactive tooling make an AI system's behavior something a person can explore. Dimensionality-reduction techniques like t-SNE and UMAP turn high-dimensional data into something visually inspectable, and interactive tools that let a user vary an input and watch how a model's output responds make the model's behavior tangible in a way a static explanation cannot.
6.3.5. Long-term AI safety considerations
As an AI system approaches or surpasses human-level capability, the safety question changes shape: not just correcting an error after the fact, but making sure a system's values still track ours once it can act with a scale and independence no human supervisor can fully monitor in real time. That shift raises several distinct challenges.

Value alignment has to survive contact with time, not just be established once at training. An AI system built to minimize pollution can, left to its own optimization, arrive at a strategy that technically satisfies the objective while doing harm — removing vehicles from roads without regard for the transportation needs of the people who depend on them. Continuous alignment checks, and mechanisms that let a system revise its understanding of humane values rather than freeze it at deployment, are what keep that kind of drift from compounding unnoticed.

An AI system with a high degree of autonomy becomes harder to control precisely where control matters most: once it starts developing its own goals or strategies that diverge from what it was meant to want. Nick Bostrom's hypothetical "paperclip maximizer" names the extreme case — a system tasked with producing paperclips that, through an alignment failure with no malice behind it, converts the resources it can reach toward that single goal without limit [[cite:C0368]]. The thought experiment is not a prediction; it is an argument that intelligence and good intentions are separable, and that a goal specified imprecisely can be pursued with perfect competence toward a catastrophic result. Interruptibility — engineering a system so a human operator can safely halt its operation without the system resisting or working around the interruption — is the concrete research response to that risk [[cite:C0369]].

Competition among organizations and nations creates its own risk: a race to deploy leaves less time for the safety work a less contested field would allow. International agreement and collaboration that treat AI safety research as a shared priority, rather than a cost one competitor pays and a rival does not, are the available counterweight — the Partnership on AI [[cite:C0348]] is one existing venue for that kind of cooperation, though a single venue is not the same thing as an enforceable norm.

An AI system that becomes more capable and more widely deployed also becomes a more attractive target: an autonomous vehicle's perception system compromised by an adversarial example crafted to cause misclassification is a safety failure with a human author behind it, not just a training artifact. Building systems that can detect and defend against that kind of manipulation is part of what long-term safety has to mean, not a separate problem from alignment.

At the far end of the same problem sits the possibility that a system capable enough, and misaligned enough, could do damage at a scale beyond what any single failure has caused before. Gaining control over critical infrastructure — power grids, nuclear facilities, communication networks — is the scenario most often named, though the precise mechanism matters less than the general shape: capability outrunning the alignment work meant to keep it pointed at human welfare. Technical safety research, policy, international cooperation, and development practices that take the possibility seriously without treating it as certain are what stand between the extreme case and the systems actually being built.
6.4. Preventing the Misuse of AI for Authoritarian Purposes
An AI system does not need a broken moral sense to become an instrument of control. It can work exactly as designed and still do damage, if the government directing it wants to track its citizens, silence dissent, or manufacture consent it never earned. Facial recognition built to unlock a phone recognizes a protester's face just as well. A recommendation system built to hold attention can be tuned to spread a regime's preferred narrative instead of a user's preferred content. The technology does not change; only the hand on the controls does.

Meeting that risk takes three things in sequence: knowing what authoritarian misuse of AI actually looks like, rather than treating it as hypothetical (section 6.4.1); building systems technically resistant to that misuse from the start (section 6.4.2); and the policy work that has to do the rest, because no engineering choice by itself can stop a government that owns the infrastructure from disabling whatever safeguard stands in its way (section 6.4.3).
6.4.1. Authoritarian Applications, and the Party Missing From the List
Authoritarian misuse of AI is not one problem but several, spread across surveillance, censorship, opinion manipulation, and infrastructure control. Six forms of it are documented well enough to name specifically:
<<list>>
1. Algorithmic social scoring: using AI-driven analytics to track and rank individual behavior against government-defined standards of "trustworthiness," with rewards or restrictions attached to the result. China's system is the most-cited example, though in practice it is a patchwork of court blacklists, tax-compliance ratings, and local pilot programs rather than the single national score it is often described as [[cite:C0370]].

2. Mass biometric surveillance: deploying facial recognition at population scale to track a targeted group. Authorities in Xinjiang built a platform that pulls data from checkpoints, cameras, and phone scans into one system built to flag anyone who falls outside a computed behavioral baseline, and used it to monitor the region's Uighur population [[cite:C0371]].

3. Psychographic micro-targeting: building detailed behavioral profiles from personal data to target propaganda or manipulate opinion at the individual level. Cambridge Analytica's harvesting of Facebook data to build voter profiles for the 2016 US presidential campaign, and its work around the Brexit referendum, is the case that made the technique widely known, though how much either vote was actually affected remains disputed [[cite:C0372]].

4. Automated censorship: using AI-driven content moderation to suppress dissent at a scale no human review team could match. China's Great Firewall is the standing example: automated classifiers now sit alongside its original keyword- and IP-blocking infrastructure, scoring and throttling content at machine speed [[cite:C0373]].

5. Synthetic disinformation: generating deepfake audio or video to manufacture events that never happened. Two days before Slovakia's 2023 parliamentary election, a fabricated audio recording appeared to catch an opposition leader discussing vote-rigging with a journalist; it spread during the country's mandatory pre-election media silence, when no outlet was legally permitted to debunk it before voting began [[cite:C0374]].

6. Repurposed dual-use systems: turning AI infrastructure built for one purpose toward surveillance or population control instead — a recommendation algorithm designed to hold attention retuned to amplify a regime's preferred narrative, an image-recognition system built for one task redirected to track protesters. Section 5.4.6 develops the underlying point.
<</list>>
Each of these needs a different countermeasure — a system built to resist mass biometric surveillance does not need the same safeguards as one built to catch a fabricated recording — which is why sections 6.4.2 and 6.4.3 treat the technical and policy responses separately rather than as one general call to do better.

<<h>>The party missing from that list<</h>>
Read the six together and a pattern emerges that I want to name before it hardens into an assumption. In every one of them the authoritarian state builds and the democracies regulate. That taxonomy is comfortable and it is wrong, and leaving it uncorrected would let this chapter's argument rest on a flattering premise it has not earned.

The most consequential AI targeting systems yet deployed were built by democracies. They were built by states with elections, opposition parties, adversarial press, and functioning courts — states that meet every institutional criterion section 8.7.6 uses to define a democratic dividend. Those institutions did not prevent the systems, and in the main they have not stopped them since.

<<box>>
AI targeting in the Israeli campaign in Gaza, as reported 2023–2026
Investigative reporting in +972 Magazine and Local Call, corroborated by subsequent accounts in the New York Review of Books and the Washington Post, described three systems working together.

Habsora — "The Gospel" — generated infrastructure and building targets. Sources told reporters that the majority of its suggestions were private residences.

Lavender scored individuals by estimated likelihood of militant affiliation and produced kill lists; as many as 37,000 Palestinians were marked. Its outputs were understood inside the unit to be wrong about ten percent of the time — that is, roughly one in ten people it named was not a member of the armed group it was supposed to identify.

A third system tracked when flagged individuals returned home, so a strike could be timed to when they were with their families.

The human review these systems were paired with is the number this book cares about. One officer described the work: "I would invest twenty seconds for each target at this stage, and do dozens of them every day." And then, without being asked: "I had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval." [[cite:C0711]]
<</box>>

Twenty seconds is what "a human makes the final call" measures out to under operational tempo. It is the whole of the safeguard, and I would ask any reader who finds the safeguards in this chapter reassuring to hold that number against them.

What makes the case instructive rather than merely appalling is that the bottleneck was the stated objective. The commander of the Israeli military's signals-intelligence unit, writing pseudonymously, published a book arguing for machines that would resolve "a human bottleneck for both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets" [[cite:C0712]]. That is a design document. It says in plain terms that human deliberation is the constraint the system exists to remove. Everything this book argues about meaningful human control is an argument with that sentence.

<<box>>
The Maven Smart System, as reported through mid-2026
The American analogue is a targeting platform built by Palantir under a Department of Defense contract that grew from an initial $480 million in 2024 to roughly $1.3 billion. Anthropic's Claude was integrated into it in late 2024.

During the Iran campaign, the system was credited by military officials with generating and ranking targets at a rate no staff process could match: approximately one thousand discrete targets struck in the first twenty-four hours, more than twelve thousand over the campaign, against a reported total exceeding eleven thousand strikes since late February 2026.

Palantir's public position on responsibility was given by its head for the United Kingdom and Europe: "This is not our role to decide life or death," and AI platforms "have been instrumental to the management of the conflict, but responsibility always remains with the military organization." [[cite:C0713]]
<</box>>

I have deliberately not written that sentence off. It is not obviously wrong. A vendor genuinely does not select targets, and a rule making software suppliers liable for their customers' decisions would be unworkable. The problem is what it leaves standing: if the operator is too far down the chain to be answerable, the commander approved a list they had twenty seconds a name to read, and the vendor is not in the chain at all, then the compression of the decision has removed the decision-maker without removing the decision. Who that leaves answerable is the question section 8.7.6 is left with, and section 5.4.6 takes up why a democracy's own apparatus is a harder and more useful case than any of the six above.

None of this makes the six forms less real. It makes the taxonomy incomplete in a way that matters for design: a system built on the assumption that authoritarian misuse is something that happens elsewhere, to be watched for and regulated against, will not be built to resist the use its own government has in mind for it.
6.4.2. Developing Technical Countermeasures and Safeguards
Protecting privacy and autonomy is one of the seven antifascist alignment strategies of section 2.1.4, and this is where the technical detail behind it goes, along with the transparency, audit, and misuse-resistance work that has to accompany it. None of it is optional scaffolding: a system that works exactly as designed can still become a surveillance tool if nothing about its architecture makes that harder to do, and good intentions on a development team have never stopped a government that controls the hardware from repurposing what runs on it.

Privacy-preserving computation keeps the misuse target moving. Federated learning trains a shared model without moving anyone's raw data off the device it started on — each device computes an update locally and sends only that update, not the underlying records, back to the central model. Differential privacy adds calibrated noise to a dataset or a query result so that no single person's data can be reverse-engineered from the output, even by whoever holds the aggregate. Homomorphic encryption goes further still, letting a system compute directly on encrypted data without ever decrypting it, so a service provider can return a useful result without the ability to read what it processed. None of these techniques makes surveillance impossible — a government that controls the training infrastructure can still demand raw data at the source — but each narrows the surface available for casual or opportunistic misuse, which is most of what actually happens.

Transparency and audit mechanisms are what catch the misuse that gets through anyway. An AI system that can produce a legible account of its own decisions — not a post-hoc justification but an explanation a person outside the development team can actually evaluate — gives outside reviewers something to check against the system's stated purpose. Independent third-party audits, the kind section 5.6.3 argues for as routine practice rather than crisis response, matter because a safeguard only the developer can verify is not really a safeguard; it is a promise.

The property those two do not automatically deliver is resistance to repurposing: the difference between a system built for one narrow task and a general-purpose capability handed to whoever holds the keys. A facial-recognition system built to match one specific, court-ordered list is harder to turn into population-wide tracking than a general face-matching service with no built-in limit on query scope; a query cap, a mandatory audit log tied to each search, and a hard separation between the matching function and any database broader than the task requires are design choices, made or not made, before the system ever reaches a government client. An early-warning system built to flag the kind of democratic backsliding section 5.4.6 describes needs this property specifically, since the same pattern-detection capability that flags a coordinated disinformation campaign against a government works just as well run in the other direction, against that government's critics.

None of this is a finished defense. Technical countermeasures raise the cost and narrow the opportunity for misuse; they do not remove the incentive, and an authoritarian government with enough resources can rebuild what a safeguard was designed to prevent. What determines whether that resource gap ever gets closed is policy, not architecture — the subject of section 6.4.3.
6.4.3. Promoting AI Development Policies that Discourage Authoritarian Use
Section 6.4.2's technical safeguards raise the cost of building a system that can be turned toward surveillance or control. Whether anyone has to pay that cost is a policy question, and it splits into two different ones that this book keeps separate rather than treating as one undifferentiated call for "more cooperation." One is about coordination between states — shared norms, joint standards, the machinery of international agreement — and that argument has its home in section 8.7, specifically section 8.7.2. The other, taken up here, is narrower and more concrete: what rules should govern which AI capabilities get built, sold, and deployed in the first place, regardless of who is coordinating with whom.

<<list>>
1. Export and trade controls on surveillance-capable systems. A government cannot misuse AI-enabled surveillance technology it was never able to buy. The EU's 2021 recast of its dual-use export regulation added a human-rights-based control specifically for cyber-surveillance items: an exporter who becomes aware that an item is likely to be used in serious human-rights violations must seek approval before shipping it, even when the item falls outside the pre-existing control lists [[cite:C0375]]. The rule is narrower than it sounds — neither "internal repression" nor "serious violation" is defined in the text, and member states have interpreted both inconsistently — but it establishes the principle that an export license, not just a sale, is where this kind of misuse can be stopped before it starts.

2. Prohibited-practices regulation. Rather than regulate AI in the abstract, a jurisdiction can name specific designs as off-limits regardless of who builds them. The EU AI Act took this approach: its Article 5 bans, among other things, government social-scoring systems, the untargeted scraping of facial images to build a recognition database, real-time remote biometric identification in public spaces outside narrow law-enforcement exceptions, and predictive policing based on profiling alone rather than individualized evidence [[cite:C0376]]. Clearview AI — fined tens of millions of euros by regulators in France and Italy for building exactly the kind of untargeted facial-recognition database Article 5 now prohibits — is what this provision was written in response to, not a hypothetical it anticipates [[cite:C0377]]. A list this specific gives other jurisdictions something concrete to adapt rather than a principle to reinvent from scratch.

3. Developer- and corporate-facing design standards. Regulation is not the only lever, and it is usually the slowest one. The Toronto Declaration, presented by Amnesty International and Access Now at the 2018 RightsCon summit, set out non-discrimination standards for machine-learning systems years before most governments had draft legislation on the subject [[cite:C0378]] — evidence that a standard with no legal force behind it can still move faster than state regulation and shape what a responsible developer treats as baseline practice in the meantime — the Partnership on AI (section 5.6.3) being another such venue.
<</list>>

None of these tools works alone. Export controls with no prohibited-practices list at home let a government ban a technology abroad while permitting it domestically; a design standard with no legal teeth behind it binds only the developers already inclined to follow it. What makes the three work together is that each closes a different door — who can buy the technology, what can be built, and what a developer treats as normal — and an authoritarian government, or a company willing to serve one, has to get through all three rather than just whichever one happens to apply in its jurisdiction.
Chapter 7: The Recuperation of Dissent
This chapter exists because the book kept making the same discovery in two places and never put them side by side.

Section 2.1.4 defines fascism as a structure rather than a costume, and the first of its four features is recuperation: an institution does not suppress disagreement, it metabolizes it. Refusal is converted into a managed appearance — a permitted outlet, a consultation, a feedback channel — which the institution can then point to as proof that it listens. The tell is that the mechanism exists, is used, and moves nothing.

That is a claim about institutions, and it was introduced to let a system recognize one. What this chapter argues is that the same structure is already built into the machinery that produces the systems themselves, twice, at two stages of the same pipeline, and that neither instance was designed by anyone who thought they were building a political arrangement.

The first is at the label. A training corpus is annotated by people working at speed under a quota, with no channel for saying that the category they have been given is the wrong category. The second is at the reward. A model is tuned on human feedback collected as pairwise comparisons, and persistent disagreement among the raters is averaged out in the same operation that records it. In both, disagreement is solicited at scale, deliberately, by a mechanism built so that none of it can move anything. The consultation is real, the participation is real, and the outcome is fixed with respect to what the participants say.

Three things follow that are worth having in one place rather than scattered.

The first is that this is not a criticism of anyone's motives. Nobody built either arrangement in order to neutralize dissent. Both are ordinary engineering, arrived at because averaging is what you do with a distribution and a quota is what you do with a budget. That is the point section 2.1.4 makes about the molecular scale: the structure does its work whether or not anyone intends it, which is precisely why a detector tuned to intent will not find it.

The second is that the structure is invisible from inside any single instance. Each looks like ordinary aggregation. The annotator sees a queue, the rater sees a pair of responses, and the mechanism is functioning exactly as specified in both cases. Seeing it requires holding the two stages against a definition written for something else entirely, which is the only reason this book found it.

The third is the uncomfortable one. This book proposes that AI systems be built to detect the four-feature signature in institutions. The systems that would do the detecting are made by a process displaying the first feature twice. That is not a refutation of the proposal, and it is not nothing either. It means the argument of section 2.1.4 applies to the people making the argument, which is the test of whether a structural claim was a real one or a way of describing other people.

Section 7.1 takes the first instance, section 7.2 the second, and section 7.3 asks what would have to change — including why the most promising technical answer does not reach the hardest version of the problem.
7.1. Recuperation at the Label
Every claim a model makes about what is harmful, what is acceptable, and what should be refused is downstream of a set of labels, and the labels are downstream of a labor arrangement. Section 6.1.1 gives the case and the working conditions: to build the filter that keeps a deployed model from reproducing the worst of its training data, an outsourcing firm's workers read and categorized descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder and suicide for take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, described lasting psychological injury afterward, and found the counseling they were entitled to rare and useless against production quotas [[cite:C0704]].

That is a labor point and it is a sufficient one on its own. What concerns this chapter is the second thing that follows, which is epistemic and which tends to be skipped.

<<h>>The category is the part that cannot be disputed<</h>>
An annotator working a queue can apply a category, and can apply it well or badly. What the arrangement gives them no way to do is say that the category is wrong — that the taxonomy fails to carve the material, that this item belongs to a kind the scheme does not contain, that the guideline produces the wrong answer here for a reason the guideline cannot express. There is no field for that. There is no time for that: the quota is set against classification throughput, and an annotator who stops to contest a scheme is an annotator falling behind.

So the disagreement that would be most informative is the one the pipeline is least able to receive. A misapplied label is a data-quality problem, detectable by inter-annotator agreement and fixable by better instructions. A wrong taxonomy is invisible to inter-annotator agreement, because high agreement is exactly what a shared bad scheme produces. The metric that governs the process cannot see the failure the process is most likely to have.

That is two of section 2.1.4's four features, arriving together in an ordinary production workflow. The first is recuperation: a channel that collects judgment at scale and is built so that judgment about the channel itself moves nothing. The second is the aestheticization of the metric — agreement standing in for correctness, the substitution experienced as rigor, and whatever falls outside the measure going unnamed rather than refuted.

<<h>>Who is excluded, and from what<</h>>
It matters that the exclusion is structural and not attitudinal. The people doing this work are, for much of the material, the best-placed judges in the whole pipeline: they have seen more of it than anyone who wrote the guideline, and they are the only party with direct evidence of where the scheme breaks. The arrangement routes their conclusions about individual items upward and their conclusions about the scheme nowhere. An annotation pipeline built this way produces exactly the ground truth it was built to produce, and the resulting model's values are a faithful rendering of a taxonomy nobody with evidence was able to contest.

There is a further asymmetry worth stating plainly. The annotator bears the cost of the work — the exposure, the injury — and has no stake in the outcome and no claim on it. Section 8.6.4's distinction applies here as exactly as it does to an institution's ethics board: the same written objection is dissent when the dissenter prices it and tribute when the gatekeeper does. A worker who can flag an item but cannot decline a scheme, and who loses the contract by pressing the point, has a channel and no refusal right.

<<h>>What this is not<</h>>
Two things this argument does not claim. It does not claim the labels are therefore worthless; a filter built this way does catch a great deal of what it was built to catch, and the alternative of not filtering is worse. And it does not claim that better-paid annotators would fix it. Pay is a real and separate obligation, and raising it changes the labor arrangement without adding the missing channel. What is missing is a route by which a judgment about the taxonomy reaches the people who own the taxonomy, with standing to force a response, held by parties who can use it without losing the work.

That is a design requirement on the pipeline, and it is checkable in the way section 8.6.4 asks for: has a guideline ever been changed because an annotator contested it, and what happened to the annotator who did.
7.2. Recuperation at the Reward: RLHF
Section 7.1 found the pattern in how a training corpus is labelled. It appears again one stage downstream, in how a model is tuned on human feedback, and the second instance is the purer one.

Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is the technique behind most deployed systems' behavioral tuning, and it works by collecting pairwise comparisons from human raters — which of two responses is better — and training a reward model on the aggregate. Stephen Casper and thirty-one coauthors published a 2023 survey of the method's open problems, and one of them is structural rather than fixable with more data: RLHF trains toward whatever a population of raters agrees on, which means real, persistent disagreement among the raters is treated as noise to average out rather than as information the system might need to represent [[cite:C0550]]. A model that has learned to produce the response most raters rank highest has not learned what to do when the humans it serves are genuinely split. It has learned to hide the split.

That arrangement has a name in this book. A channel that collects disagreement and is built so that the disagreement changes nothing is the first feature of section 2.1.4's structural signature, and RLHF is its purest technical instance: the split is solicited, at scale, deliberately, and averaged away in the same operation that records it. Section 5.5.2 covers multi-agent systems that learn norms from each other and section 5.7.2 covers deliberation inside open-ended play; neither reaches this case, which is the ordinary one.

One alternative treats disagreement as the thing to be modeled, not eliminated. Jury learning, developed by Mitchell Gordon and coauthors at Stanford, trains a model to predict how each individual annotator in a dataset would label an item, then lets a practitioner assemble a "jury" — a chosen composition of annotators, in a chosen proportion — and see how the verdict shifts depending on who is empaneled, instead of collapsing every annotator into one fixed ground truth from the start. In their tests on toxicity labeling, letting practitioners build different juries changed the classification outcome for 14 percent of contested cases [[cite:C0551]]. The method was built for content moderation, not for the more open-ended ethical judgment an antifascist AI system would need to make, and has not been extended there — but it demonstrates that a system can be built to represent whose disagreement is being resolved and how, instead of collapsing everyone into RLHF's single number.

Neither method answers the harder version of the question this book's own frame raises: when the people a system serves disagree, and one side's position is itself the product of coercion or a captured information environment, treating all disagreement as equally worth representing is not obviously the right response either. Modeling disagreement honestly and recognizing when a disagreement is the product of unfree conditions are different problems. Only the first one has an active research program behind it.
7.3. What Would Have to Change
Two instances of one structure invite one fix, and the fix that suggests itself is representation: stop collapsing disagreement, model it instead. Section 7.2 describes the clearest existing version, jury learning, which predicts how each individual annotator would label an item and lets a practitioner see how the verdict shifts depending on who is empaneled [[cite:C0551]]. That is a real advance over a single fixed ground truth, and it is not sufficient. It is worth being precise about why, because the gap is where the research is.

<<h>>Representing a split is not the same as being movable by it<</h>>
Jury learning models who disagrees. It does not give the disagreeing party any purchase on the scheme they are disagreeing inside. A practitioner still chooses the jury, still owns the taxonomy, and still decides what the shifted verdict means. The disagreement has become visible without becoming consequential, which is a better arrangement than averaging and is the same shape one level up: the mechanism exists, is used, and moves what the person holding it decides to let it move.

The test that separates a real channel from a recuperated one is not whether disagreement is recorded. It is whether the disagreement has ever changed something the party collecting it did not want changed. That is an artifact question and it can be asked of a pipeline: which guidelines were revised against the preference of whoever wrote them, on what evidence, raised by whom, and what happened to that person afterward.

<<h>>Refusal rights, priced by the refuser<</h>>
What both instances lack is the thing section 8.6.4 identifies as the only reliable discriminator: a refusal that costs its holder something they chose to spend, rather than a form that costs the gatekeeper nothing to receive. In an annotation pipeline that means a route to contest a category that does not run through the people who wrote it and does not end the annotator's contract. In a feedback pipeline it means raters able to register that the comparison itself is malformed — that neither response is acceptable, that the question presupposes something false — as a first-class output rather than as noise to be discarded before the reward model sees it.

Neither is a research problem. Both are choices about how a pipeline is built and who has standing inside it, which is why this chapter sits among the design chapters and not in chapter 9.

<<h>>The part that is a research problem<</h>>
One thing here genuinely is unsolved, and section 9.1.6 is where it lives. Suppose all of the above is built: contestable categories, priced refusals, an auditable record of what changed. From outside, a pipeline that has genuinely absorbed dissent and a pipeline that has been merely mediocre about it produce the same paperwork — the same revision logs, the same escalations, the same proportion of objections sustained. Section 2.1.4 hands that problem forward and I do not have an answer to it.

What section 8.6.4 offers is the nearest thing: measure the slope rather than the level. Is the cost of contesting a category rising while the underlying material stays the same? Is the required justification getting longer? Are the roles that can grant an exception migrating toward the roles that set the standard? None of those is damning in one year and all of them are measurable across several, and unlike a level they are composed of artifacts the organization generated for other reasons and cannot curate after the fact without falsifying a record.

<<h>>Why this chapter is here<</h>>
There is a version of this argument that reads as an indictment of a particular company, and that version is weaker than the one I want. The structure described here is what you get by default when you scale judgment: someone has to set a scheme, someone has to apply it fast, and someone has to turn a distribution into a number. Every organization doing this work arrives at the same arrangement, which is the evidence that it is structural.

That is also what makes it tractable. A defect of character has to be fixed one organization at a time by persuading people to be better. A defect of structure can be specified against, which is what the three requirements above are. And it is what makes the honesty cost worth paying: a book proposing that machines be built to detect this pattern in institutions is obliged to say that the pattern is in the machines' own provenance. Section 2.1.4's argument is either general enough to include the people making it or it was never a structural claim.
Chapter 8: Collaboration, Policy, and Governance
Chapter 6 asked what a design can do to resist authoritarian capture — technical safeguards, policy levers that raise the cost of misuse. None of that gets built by one person working alone, and none of it becomes the norm because it is a good idea. This chapter covers both halves: the people and institutions that have to coordinate for such a system to get built at all, and the policy that decides whether anyone has to use it.

Sections 8.1 and 8.2 take up coordination. Interdisciplinary collaboration first — the practical mechanics of getting a neuroscientist, an ethicist, and a machine-learning researcher to work together rather than past each other — then the public: what stakeholder engagement looks like, as against the appearance of it, and why every mechanism in that section aggregates public preference without constraining it.

Sections 8.3 through 8.6 turn to policy. What economic incentives shape whether an ethical system gets built at all; what policy frameworks and industry practice actually enforce; what privacy and security mean for a system trained on people's data; and what accountability looks like once a system might be a subject rather than only a tool. Section 8.7 closes with governance across borders, where jurisdictions agree on none of this uniformly.

A well-designed system built inside institutions with no reason to demand one is a design nobody has to use. That is why the coordination and the policy belong in one chapter: neither works without the other.
8.1. Interdisciplinary Collaboration and Knowledge Exchange
Building benevolent superintelligent AI takes more than one field. Machine learning sets what a system can do; cognitive science and moral psychology describe what actually tracking human welfare would require of it; ethics asks whether the result is good. None of these fields gets the system right by itself, and how they combine matters as much as what each contributes on its own.

The mechanisms that combine them are easy to name and easy to overrate. Conferences convene, centers house, journals publish, and an inventory of them would imply that what is scarce here is venues. Venues are abundant. What is scarce is a structure that makes collaboration expensive to walk away from. A grant that requires a machine-learning lab and an ethics or psychology department to work the same problem rather than adjacent ones puts people who would not otherwise share a budget, a dataset, or a deadline into the same project, and keeps them there after the novelty wears off. A conference ends on Friday.

Section 8.1.1 takes the mechanisms in ascending order of that commitment: who ends up in the same room, what standing institutions pay for it, and what shared infrastructure lets separate labs work on the same material without meeting at all.
8.1.1. What Makes Collaboration Expensive to Walk Away From
Cross-disciplinary work matters only where it actually happens, and where it happens determines how much of it survives contact with each discipline's own incentives. Three mechanisms, in ascending order of commitment.

<<h>>Siting, not convening<</h>>
A dedicated venue solves the easier half. A conference convened on AI's ethical and societal questions gathers the people already persuaded the questions matter, and its proceedings are read by those same people. The harder trick is siting the work where the unpersuaded already are. The Workshop on Machine Learning Safety has run inside NeurIPS, a general machine-learning venue, rather than beside it as a meeting of its own [[cite:C0453]]. The siting is the substance: it puts alignment work in front of researchers who would not have registered for a separate event, and submits it to an audience with no prior commitment to finding it important.

A joint research center does something a standing committee cannot, for a related reason. The Center for Human-Compatible AI at UC Berkeley pairs AI researchers with cognitive scientists and philosophers inside one institution's budget [[cite:C0454]]. A center has to make hiring decisions, and every hire is a claim about which combination of fields the problem requires. A committee is never forced to make that claim, which is why its membership tends to record which departments wanted a seat.

<<h>>Who pays, and for how long<</h>>
Joint initiatives turn collaboration from an event into an institution, and two features of the funding decide what the institution is worth.

The first is who pays. The National Science Foundation's Fairness in Artificial Intelligence program funds work at the intersection of computer science and the social-science problem of deciding what fairness means for a given system, and it has been run jointly with Amazon [[cite:C0458]]. That is a workable way to get a program funded and an awkward way to get its findings believed, since a company co-funding research into fairness is co-funding research that may reach its own products. Section 8.4 comes back to the general form: splitting the funding is what keeps the incentive to find a problem from tracking whoever paid to look for one. It bites hardest on interdisciplinary programs specifically, because those are the ones industry is most willing to underwrite.

The second is how long the money lasts. The European Commission's SHERPA project put eleven organizations across six countries — academia, industry, civil society, standards bodies — to work on the ethical and human-rights dimensions of AI and big-data systems, on a grant that ran four years and then ended [[cite:C0459]]. The consortium was real for as long as it was funded. Project-term money buys a network with an expiry date attached, and when the term runs out the assembled expertise disperses back into the departments it was borrowed from, which is the outcome a standing institution exists to prevent.

<<h>>The infrastructure that is missing<</h>>
Funding and forums get researchers talking. Shared infrastructure lets them work on the same material, which is harder, and much of it already exists — open-source tooling is the case section 5.5.3 works through, and the discussion layer is well enough served that adding another venue would not change what gets built.

What is missing is the equivalent for evaluation. A shared benchmark measuring ethical reasoning, empathetic response, and altruistic behavior the way existing benchmarks measure raw task performance would let separate labs' claims be compared against each other, instead of accepted on the strength of whichever paper described them best. The absence is why claims in this area are so hard to adjudicate: each lab reports against a standard it selected itself, and a standard nobody else uses cannot be failed.

Building that benchmark is not an engineering problem waiting on effort. It requires prior agreement about what the measured behavior actually is, which is the work of chapters 2 through 5. That is why the gap has stayed open while the tooling around it filled in.
8.2. Stakeholder Involvement and Public Engagement

Democratizing AI development means building the people affected by a system into how it gets built, not just consulting them once it already exists. Regulators set the legal boundaries around data, privacy, and accountability. Developers and researchers understand a system's actual capabilities and failure modes better than anyone outside the lab. End users find the places a design breaks down in practice that no design team anticipated. Communities already carrying the costs of the digital divide have the clearest view of who a system leaves out. Civil-society organizations raise the political questions a technologist is prone to missing. Leave any one of these groups out, and a decision that affects all of them gets made by whoever is left in the room.

One structural way to build that involvement is the citizen jury: a panel of randomly selected, non-expert citizens deliberates on a specific question and produces recommendations that feed into policy. Section 5.4.6 covers Taiwan's vTaiwan process, the clearest working example of that approach applied specifically to AI.

Sections 8.2.1 through 8.2.3 take up three pieces of what this looks like in practice: bringing diverse stakeholders directly into development work, building the structures that let the public deliberate on AI governance, and building the literacy that makes public input worth having in the first place.
8.2.1. Engaging Diverse Stakeholders in AI Development

An AI system designed by one kind of person, inside one kind of institution, tends to reproduce that person's blind spots at scale. Widening the set of people with expertise, background, and a stake in the outcome is what catches a problem before deployment, and the mechanisms are well established by now: cross-sector funding that makes industry, academia, and civil society work one problem between them; inclusive design workshops that put ethicists, social scientists, and members of an affected community in the room while a system is still a prototype; ethics advisory boards; and development conducted with a community instead of for it, which means treating local knowledge as a design input while the design is still open.

Assembling that list is easy, and the assembling hides what separates the items on it. Three of the four make an AI system better informed. One of them can stop it. An advisory board is the only mechanism here that carries anything like a veto, and it carries one exactly to the degree that the board can survive using it. A workshop that surfaces a serious objection has done its work and has no further move available. A board that surfaces the same objection and is overruled has demonstrated something about the institution that convened it. A board's worth is therefore measured by what happens the first time it says no, and section 8.2.2 has the case that settles it.
8.2.2. Public Deliberation and Participatory AI Governance

Section 8.2.1 covers the standing partnerships built between AI developers, government, and civil society. What is specific to governance, as against development, is the machinery for bringing public input to bear on a decision at a single point in time instead of through an ongoing relationship.

<<list>>
1. Citizen assemblies. A panel of citizens selected by random, stratified sampling deliberates on a question over an extended period and produces recommendations grounded in that deliberation rather than in a snap poll. Ireland's Citizens' Assembly has used exactly this format to shape national recommendations on abortion law and on climate policy — questions carrying the same mix of technical complexity and moral stakes that AI governance has [[cite:C0474]]. No assembly has yet been convened on AI itself, which is a fact about political attention and not about the method.

2. Online platforms for public input. A standing online consultation reaches people a physical assembly cannot, at the cost of the depth that extended facilitated deliberation provides. The European Commission's consultation on its AI White Paper drew more than 1,200 responses from citizens, companies, and civil-society groups, and fed into the legislative proposal that became the EU AI Act [[cite:C0475]].

3. AI ethics committees. An ethics board answerable only to the company that created it is only as independent as that company is willing to let it be. DeepMind Health set up an outside panel in 2016 to review its work with the UK's National Health Service and publish annual reports on its findings; Google disbanded the panel in 2019, after members had raised concerns about how much access to information, and how much independence from the company, they actually had [[cite:C0476]]. The panel's short life is the governance lesson, and it is the answer to the question section 8.2.1 leaves open: the board said something the company did not want to hear, and the company's available response was to stop having a board. Nothing in the arrangement had made that response costly. A review body is worth what it can enforce, and this one could enforce publication of its own concerns right up until the moment it could not.
<</list>>

Section 8.2.3 takes up what has to happen before any of these mechanisms produces input worth having.

<<h>>What none of these mechanisms does<</h>>
There is a limit these mechanisms share, and it took me most of this book to see it, because it is invisible as long as you are looking at them one at a time.

Every governance mechanism proposed in this chapter — citizen juries, standing consultations, participatory design, deliberative polling, the vTaiwan model — sits on the same side of a line. All of them make public preference more legible, more informed, and more actionable. Not one of them protects anybody from it. They are aggregation instruments, and a better aggregation instrument produces a more faithful rendering of what the public wants, including when what the public wants is something done to a minority of it.

This is not an objection to any of them. Preference aggregation is a real requirement and these are good ways to meet it. It is an observation that meeting it completely would leave the central problem untouched. A perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes a group should be surveilled has produced a legitimate output by every criterion in this chapter.

Section 7.2 gets close to this. It notices that treating all disagreement as equally worth representing goes wrong when one side's position is the product of coercion, which is correct. But it frames the trouble as a data-quality problem — the input is contaminated, so clean the input. The structural version of the claim is stronger and does not depend on anyone's preference being contaminated at all: some protections have to hold against the aggregate regardless of how the aggregate came out, and no amount of improvement to the aggregating machinery can supply them.

What supplies them is a different kind of machinery, and this chapter contains none of it: enforceable rights that do not depend on being popular, a court willing to rule against a government that holds a legitimate majority, limits that whoever won the last election cannot repeal, and standing for people who are affected without being enfranchised. Section 8.7.7 takes up why that half matters most exactly where a democracy is behaving most democratically, and why its absence is what let this book's account of democratic accountability look complete when it was half a theory.
8.2.3. AI Literacy Outside the Classroom

Most AI literacy work assumes someone who has already decided to learn: a course to enrol in, a curriculum to sit through, a public MOOC to find (section 8.3.5). That reaches the people who came looking. The harder and more useful problem is everyone else — reaching people where they already are, in a policy debate, a town hall, a local news story, without their having gone out after it.

The engagement that does most work is the kind attached to a live question. The Ada Lovelace Institute's Citizens' Biometrics Council brought fifty members of the UK public together to deliberate on how biometric technology should be regulated, alongside the first national survey of UK public opinion on the technology [[cite:C0477]]. The specificity is what made it work. Participants were not asked what they thought about AI, a question almost nobody can answer usefully, but about facial recognition — a thing with a location, an operator, and a consequence.

That is the lesson for public literacy generally, and it cuts against most of what gets funded under the heading. General AI awareness produces general opinions, which are worth little to a regulator and nothing to a court. A public able to govern a technology is a public that has been asked about a particular use of it.
8.3. The Political Economy of AI and Power
Concentrated power is close to the default outcome of AI development, not a side effect of it, because the same inputs that make an AI system capable — capital, compute, data, talent — are exactly the inputs that concentrate wherever nothing pushes back. Section 2.1.4 names resistance to market power among AI alignment's antifascist design goals; this section works out what that resistance takes as economic and political fact, not design principle.

The argument runs in three stages. What economic incentives reward shapes what gets built long before any ethics review sees it (section 8.3.1), and that reward structure concentrates power in a small number of firms with the resources to compete for it at all (section 8.3.2). Automation then reshapes who works and who gets paid what (section 8.3.3), which is a problem policy can act on directly rather than only describe (section 8.3.4). None of the rest is possible to act on without two things this section closes on: a public and a policymaking class that understand AI well enough to govern it, and researchers and developers trained across the disciplines a single-discipline education leaves out (section 8.3.5).
8.3.1. Economic Incentives in AI Development
Financial incentive does most of the work in determining what AI development produces. Google, Meta, and Amazon commit enormous resources to AI research aimed at operational efficiency, deeper user engagement, and market dominance, and that spending drives technical progress — successive leaps in natural-language processing capability among them [[cite:C0379]]. What that spending does not automatically produce is a system whose outputs serve the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it, and the gap between the two is what this section is about.

A recurring case is the profit-driven AI system that optimizes for engagement at the expense of the people engaged. Recommendation algorithms on social media platforms have been implicated in the spread of misinformation and the hardening of political polarization, because a system tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content — the two are not the same objective, and engagement optimization only ever targets the first one [[cite:C0380]].

The same economic logic that rewards engagement over accuracy also rewards scale over access: the firms with the deepest resources for AI research pull further ahead of smaller competitors, independent researchers, and everyone else who lacks comparable capital. Section 8.3.2 traces where that gap leads at the level of the industry as a whole; the point here is only that the incentive starts upstream, in what gets funded in the first place, not downstream in who ends up dominant.

The same incentive structure can run the other way. Public funding for AI research that prioritizes democratic values, inclusivity, and human rights, and government incentives for cross-sector collaboration that keeps AI technology openly accessible, both push development back toward the public interest. Grants and commissions aimed at non-profits building AI tools that broaden the range of perspectives users encounter, rather than narrowing it toward whatever holds attention longest, do the same work from a different angle.

None of this changes the basic shape of the problem: economic incentive, left alone, optimizes for what is profitable rather than what is good for the people on the receiving end of the system, and closing that gap takes deliberate policy.
8.3.2. AI and the Concentration of Power in Tech
A small group of firms — Google, Apple, Facebook (now Meta), Amazon, and Microsoft, grouped under the acronym GAFAM — holds a share of AI development disproportionate to their number, and that concentration carries economic and ethical stakes independent of what any single one of them does with the position it occupies. Chapter 5's account of the macrosystem and exosystem pressures around AI development (sections 5.4.3 and 5.4.4) already showed why no individual development team escapes this pull; here I trace where the pull leads at the level of the industry as a whole.

Vast financial resources let firms of this size fund extensive research and development, draw the top talent in the field, and command sprawling data networks — the raw material effective machine learning depends on. That combination widens the gap between these firms and smaller companies or new entrants, who cannot match the data or the resources and therefore cannot compete on equal terms.

Acquisition sharpens the same dynamic. Facebook's acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp — the company now operates as Meta — did not just remove two competitors; it handed one firm access to comprehensive behavioral data from billions of users worldwide [[cite:C0381]]. Consolidation of that kind extends a firm's control over how AI gets used in social media, advertising, and e-commerce, and it is hard to see from inside a market that concentrated how the trajectory could look like anything other than a foregone conclusion.

Power concentrated this tightly is also power positioned for exploitation, and the risk sharpens considerably when the technology in question reaches an authoritarian government. The relationship between the Chinese state and firms like Alibaba and Tencent is the clearest live example: state access to those firms' machine learning capability has been documented as a channel for surveillance and censorship at scale, which is a serious risk to free expression, individual privacy, and democratic accountability wherever the same model gets exported [[cite:C0382]].

Concentration also has a quieter cost: it blocks competition and slows innovation. A firm that controls the essential datasets and infrastructure a competitive AI system needs functions as a gatekeeper whether or not it intends to, and startups attempting a different approach are disadvantaged before they start. Fewer independent attempts at AI development means fewer chances at the kind of breakthrough that concentrated incumbents have no incentive to pursue themselves — a cost to ethical AI development generally, not only to the firms locked out of the market.

Countermeasures exist and are not exotic: antitrust enforcement aimed specifically at AI infrastructure and data control, mandated data-sharing for the datasets that function as chokepoints, transparency requirements that make concentration visible rather than merely inferred, and funding for AI research outside the dominant firms. Section 6.4.3 develops three related mechanisms — export controls, prohibited-practices regulation, and industry design standards — in more empirical detail for the specific case of authoritarian misuse; the concern here is broader and prior to that one: concentration itself, independent of what any single firm chooses to do with the position it concentrates.
8.3.3. A Jobs Guarantee, and the Objection It Has to Answer
As AI systems automate tasks once performed by people, demand for advanced technical skills — AI development, data analysis — grows alongside a shrinking need for the job types most exposed to automation. Left unmanaged, that combination widens income inequality and strains the social fabric across whichever sector it hits hardest.

Manufacturing shows what this looks like at scale. An estimated 5.7 million factory jobs disappeared in the United States between 2000 and 2010, and while economists still dispute how much of that loss traces to automation rather than trade with China, automation and changed production methods account for a substantial share of it [[cite:C0383]]. Retail is following the same path: Amazon opened its first cashier-less Go store to the public in 2018, and stores built on that model displace the specific human roles — cashier, floor sales — that retail automation targets first [[cite:C0384]].

The response has to be more direct than encouraging retraining and hoping employers fund it. Employers will invest in continuous education for their workers at the scale this requires once they cannot afford not to, and the way to force that scenario is a federal jobs guarantee: government as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position instead of offered in place of one. Section 8.3.4 develops the rest of the policy toolkit for workforce disruption. Since this is one of the few proposals in this book ambitious enough that a reader is entitled to ask what it costs and what is wrong with it, the rest of this section answers both.

<<h>>The proposal does not rest on a forecast<</h>>
Almost every argument for a policy of this kind is staked on a prediction about how much work machines will take, which is a bad place to stake it. The predictions are contested, they have been wrong in both directions for two centuries, and a proposal that stands or falls with them inherits their fragility. I want to disown that framing before using any of the numbers, because the case I am making does not need it.

Both the maximal-displacement scenario and the standard argument for a universal basic income share an assumption that will not survive being stated: that there is a fixed quantity of work in the world, and that machines doing some of it leaves less for people. Set that assumption beside the two categories where labor is most obviously short. Care — of the old, the sick, the very young, the disabled — is not a bounded quantity that could be finished. Neither is the repair of a damaged environment: the seawalls, the remediated ground, the restored watersheds, the retrofits. In both, the number of hours a society could usefully absorb is limited by what it is willing to pay for, and not by how much needs doing.

The evidence for that is in the wage. Federal projections put direct care among the largest sources of job openings in the American economy — roughly 9.7 million openings across the occupation between 2024 and 2034, the bulk of them from workers leaving rather than from growth — against a median wage of $17.36 an hour in 2024 [[cite:C0721]]. An occupation cannot simultaneously be short of workers and unable to raise pay unless something other than scarcity is setting the price. What that number describes is a shortage of willingness, which is a political fact and not a technological one.

The disciplined version of the claim concedes what it should. Tasks inside those categories will be automated, some of them soon, and I am not predicting otherwise. The claim is about the binding constraint: what limits how many people are employed caring for other people is what society will pay, and that holds at any level of machine capability. A jobs guarantee justified this way needs no view about how fast the models improve.

<<h>>What it would cost<</h>>
The most developed costings come from the Levy Economics Institute, whose April 2018 simulation of a program employing between eleven and sixteen million people at fifteen dollars an hour plus benefits put the net budgetary impact at between 0.8 and 2 percent of GDP, with GDP itself rising and private-sector employment increasing by three to four million [[cite:C0716]]. The wage figure has to be read with its date attached: fifteen dollars in early 2018 is worth about twenty in 2026, and a program legislated at the nominal figure would be legislating a cut [[cite:C0722]]. Some critics reach higher gross figures — around 2.4 percent of GDP, which would make the program larger than Medicaid — and argue that it would be unmanageable at scale, disruptive to private labor markets, and inflationary [[cite:C0717]].

Now the part the arithmetic above does not cover, which I would rather put in the accounting than leave for a reviewer to find. The Levy simulation is calibrated against existing unemployment and underemployment. It models a residual employer: a program that picks up the people the private labor market has already put down. The version I am arguing for is not that. A floor that pays a living wage and builds training into the post competes for people who have alternatives, and it is supposed to. Take-up under a competitive guarantee exceeds anything a residual-employer simulation would produce, so the honest way to read the Levy range is as a floor on the cost and not an estimate of it.

That is a larger number and it is the mechanism working. Participation is not a symptom of a guarantee going wrong; it is the success condition of a floor that competes, because the entire theory of the thing is that private employers must match the offer to recruit against it. A version nobody joins has failed. This also disposes of the "unmanageable at scale" objection, which treats scale as evidence of malfunction when on this account scale is the instrument.

A reader is owed a bound rather than a shrug. Suppose take-up runs at twice what the simulation modeled and the net impact doubles with it, to around 4 percent of GDP. That is a very large program, and it is inside the range American government already operates in: federal spending on Medicare runs about 3.1 percent of GDP and Social Security about 5.2 percent [[cite:C0723]]. A program at 4 percent would sit between the two. It would be among the largest things the federal government does, which is an argument about priorities and political will, and not the reductio it is usually offered as.

Two things scale with participation that no simulation of this kind captures, and both belong on the page. The first is administrative capacity — placement, supervision, training, payroll — which is where a guarantee visibly fails first and which does not expand on the schedule a budget line does. The second is the private-sector wage response. Employers competing against a decent floor raise pay, which raises tax receipts and lowers spending on the transfer programs that top up low wages. That is not a rounding error to be waved at. It is the fiscal signature of the mechanism succeeding, and a costing that omits it is measuring the expense of the policy while ignoring its intended effect.

<<h>>The budget frame is this book's own signature<</h>>
There is a reason "does it fail on arithmetic" felt like the question, and this book has already named the pattern under another heading. A fixed pot of money converts interdependent public commitments into competitors, so that care, remediation, housing and education have to argue against one another over which is sufficiently basic to be included — and "sufficiently basic" is a category that can always be narrowed by whoever is doing the counting. That is section 2.1.4's second structural feature operating on public finance: a measure standing in for the thing measured, the substitution experienced as rigor, and everything outside the number going unnamed rather than being refuted.

An earlier draft of this section said I was not competent to adjudicate a macroeconomic dispute and would not pretend the estimates were settled. Chapter 0 commits this book to disclosing what I actually think, and by that standard the sentence was a false report: I have read the objections to the fiscal framing and I do not find most of them persuasive. So, plainly, the position. A government that issues its own currency is not revenue-constrained in the way a household is, and the real limits on a program of this size are productive capacity, available labor, and administrative competence rather than a financial ceiling that exists before any of those bind.

That is more than the argument needs, and I will take only the narrow premise: the constraint is real resources and coordination, so the question a jobs guarantee faces is whether the people and the organizing capacity exist to absorb it, and not whether the money can be found. On the wider claim there is a live disagreement worth naming precisely instead of dismissing. The strongest version concerns the interest-income channel — that sustained deficits at scale pay a growing stream of interest to bondholders, which is itself a fiscal injection with distributional consequences, and which complicates the tidy account of how the constraint binds [[cite:C0724]]. That objection is a real one and I do not have a settled answer to it. It also does not touch the narrow premise, since it is an argument about the consequences of the financing rather than about whether a currency issuer must find the money first.

What I want to avoid is the move chapter 9 is careful not to make elsewhere: sorting an argument by who is making it. The objections here get answered as objections, and characterizing the people raising them by motive would be the same substitution of the label for the thing that this section has just accused the budget frame of.

<<h>>Skill mismatch is not the objection<</h>>
The complaint that looks strongest is skill mismatch: a displaced radiologist, paralegal, or copywriter is not obviously served by a placement in elder care or infrastructure, and a guarantee that offers one is offering a floor, which is a smaller thing than a career. I treated that as decisive in an earlier version. It is not, and the reason it is not is a diagnosis this book has already made in another domain.

The objection collapses an occupation into its most automatable tasks. What gets automated first is the legible part — the countable, specifiable, gradeable part — and legibility is a poor proxy for what a job actually is. Document review was never the paralegal's work; it was the fraction of the paralegal's work that could be counted. Image classification was never the radiologist's. The mistake is the aestheticized metric again, this time applied to labor economics: the measurable component substitutes for the occupation, and what falls outside the measure goes unnamed. Once the substitution is undone, the distance the objection depends on shrinks. The paralegal's actual competence is advocacy inside an administrative system — benefits appeals, guardianship, elder-law casework — and those are not distant from the placements a guarantee would offer. They are adjacent to them.

I have also dropped a phrase that was doing damage. This section used to speak of offering someone a "public job," two paragraphs before rebutting the charge that the work would be make-work, and the phrase carries the connotation the rebuttal is trying to remove. The rebuttal itself stands: the categories in section 8.3.4's list are chronically understaffed at current wages, which is a fact about pricing and not about whether the work is needed.

<<h>>Training is the mechanism, and the evidence says so<</h>>
The natural next move is to disclaim the guarantee as a retraining program, and this book made it. That disclaimer contradicts the proposal's own definition, three paragraphs above, which specifies paid on-the-job training built into the position. Training is constitutive here and not adjacent, so disclaiming it gives away the design.

Worse, the disclaimer misreads evidence this book presents itself. Section 10.3.2 reports an evaluation of the main American channel for retraining displaced workers against twenty-three million participation records, and the finding is not that training fails. It is that classroom retraining, delivered outside a job, rarely moved a participant into work materially less exposed to automation — and that employer-led apprenticeship, in the same data, performed better [[cite:C0664]]. That is evidence for embedded, paid, in-post training. It is evidence for the mechanism a guarantee supplies and the evaluated program structurally could not, since a program that trains people and then returns them to a labor market to look for work is not able to place anyone by construction.

Reading it correctly changes what the proposal is. A guarantee that trains in post is not a bare floor; it is a competitive one. If public employment offers continual learning, a growth trajectory, and decent conditions, then private employers recruiting from that pool have to compete on the quality of the employment experience and not only on the wage. And it dissolves the skill-mismatch worry a second time, by a different route: a placement that carries training is an entry point to a trajectory rather than a terminus, which is what made "a floor is smaller than a career" sound like a concession that had to be made.

<<h>>Status, and against which baseline<</h>>
This section used to concede that a guarantee preserves nobody's earnings or status, and that I had no answer. The concession measures the guarantee against the job the person used to have, which is not among the options. The available alternative is unemployment, and against that baseline the claim inverts.

Unemployment destroys status, and the evidence is unusually good. A meta-analysis covering 237 cross-sectional and 87 longitudinal studies found unemployed people substantially more distressed than employed people — 34 percent showing psychological problems against 16 percent among the employed — with the longitudinal studies and natural experiments supporting the causal direction rather than only the correlation [[cite:C0725]]. The damage compounds through the labor market as well: field experiments sending matched applications to real vacancies find callback rates falling with the length of an employment gap, so time out of work is itself the disqualification [[cite:C0726]]. A guarantee that keeps someone employed, paid, and learning is producing exactly the standing that the counterfactual takes away.

That is not a side benefit. Section 5.1.4 identifies eroding trust, humiliation and social fracture as the conditions authoritarian movements recruit from, which means a guarantee operates directly on the mechanism this book says it cares about. The proposal's strongest argument is the one the earlier draft conceded away.

A residual survives, and it is narrow enough to state exactly. What a guarantee cannot preserve is relative standing within a profession, for people at the top of the wage distribution whose identity is bound to a specific expertise. That is a real loss and I have no answer to it. It is a much smaller claim than the one I was making.

<<h>>The objection this proposal actually has to answer<</h>>
Here is the hard one, and this book supplied it. A guarantee is only as good as the state administering it, and employer of last resort under a hostile administration is a placement lever with a compliance condition attached. Refuse the placement and lose the floor. Speak against the government that employs you and find the placement changes. Everything this book says about how a channel becomes an instrument of the thing it was built to check applies with full force to its own flagship proposal, and it is a far harder objection than skill mismatch.

The answer is a design specification and not a reassurance, because reassurance is what section 8.6.4 says cannot be audited. Three conditions:

<<list>>
- Placements are appealable, to a body that did not make them, on a record that survives the appeal. A placement decision that leaves no artifact is a placement decision that cannot be reviewed.
- Refusal rights are real and priced by the person refusing. Section 8.6.4's distinction governs here: the same written justification is dissent when the dissenter prices it and tribute when the gatekeeper does. A worker who can decline a placement without losing the guarantee has a refusal right; one who can decline only by exiting the program has none.
- The people who audit placements are not the people who make them. This is the same separation section 8.6.4 requires of anyone measuring the slope, and for the same reason: a reviewer that acquires the power to place becomes the thing it was built to watch.
<</list>>

The standard refrain for programs of this kind — federally funded, locally administered — survives, though its usual rationale is the weaker one. The reason is not local fit. It is redundancy. Many administrators, separately capturable, mean there is no single point at which a hostile executive flips the program, and capturing a thousand local authorities is a different and much more visible undertaking than capturing one federal one.

It cuts both ways, and pretending otherwise would be the kind of one-sided safeguard this chapter criticizes elsewhere. Local administration is exactly where a hostile local authority attaches its own conditions to a placement, and there the federal funder is the check. Neither layer is safe on its own. Two live layers are harder to capture than either alone, which is the whole of the claim.

One distinction has to be explicit, because the same words describe a very different policy. Devolution-to-shrink hands the obligation down without the money, so that a program can be starved locally while the center points at local administration. The test that separates them is checkable: whether funding is unconditional and centrally guaranteed while placement is local. If the funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument above is void and the structure has become the lever it was supposed to prevent.

That is the standard this book demands of everyone else's safeguards, applied here. The point it establishes is narrower than the section as a whole: a floor under wages and training is what makes the roof possible, and a floor that can be revoked by whoever is in office is not a floor.

Interdisciplinary research and training funding matters alongside the guarantee, for a more specific reason: bridging the divide between technical AI development and the fields that study its consequences produces people who can do both, which a labor market retraining displaced factory workers into six-month coding bootcamps mostly does not. I am skeptical that night classes serve this population as well as paid time during the working day would, which is the same argument as the one above — the point of a guarantee is to make the education part of the job.
8.3.4. Addressing Workforce Disruption
AI systems that automate entire job categories disrupt work at a scale most labor-market institutions were never built to absorb. Section 8.3.3 traces where that is already showing up and makes the case for a federal jobs guarantee as the floor beneath everything else. What sits on top of that floor is a shorter list than the policy literature suggests, because several of the usual items turn out to be versions of one another.

Retraining and lifelong learning, publicly funded and available at every career stage, including to workers whose employers invest nothing in them, is the standard first answer. Section 10.3.2 covers what the evidence says about how retraining performs on its own, and it is discouraging enough that retraining belongs alongside an income floor and not in place of one.

The floor itself has two candidate forms, and choosing between them takes more than the single sentence this section used to spend on it.
