Finland's basic income experiment paid 2,000 unemployed people an unconditional monthly stipend and found modestly higher life satisfaction and lower psychological strain among recipients, with no significant negative effect on employment [[cite:C0386]]. That disposes of the standard objection to unconditional transfers, which is that they stop people working.

<<h>>The freedom argument, which is the one worth answering<</h>>
The usual case for a basic income rests on the obsolescence forecast — machines are coming for the work, so decouple income from it — and section 8.3.3 has already declined that premise, for both proposals equally. The stronger case does not depend on it. Conditioning a person's income on their being employed hands whoever controls the employment a lever over them, and a floor that can be pulled is a poor foundation for anyone's freedom to refuse. That argument deserves engagement rather than a sentence, partly because it is good and partly because it has the same shape as this book's own case for a floor: a protection that means anything has to hold when someone powerful would prefer it did not.

The answer is not that a basic income is conditional and a guarantee is not. Both are conditional, differently, and the difference is what matters.

A basic income is conditional on continued political willingness to fund it. That willingness is revisable by exactly the aggregation machinery section 8.2.2 admits constrains nothing — a majority that stops wanting to pay for it can stop, legitimately, by every criterion this chapter offers. And it can be withdrawn by means that are diffuse and deniable. Nobody has to vote to abolish a transfer that is quietly left unindexed; inflation does the work, and no individual decision-maker is ever answerable for the outcome. The dependence is total and it produces no artifact.

A jobs guarantee is conditional on a placement decision. Section 8.3.3 concedes without qualification that this is a lever, and that under a hostile administration it is a dangerous one. What it also is, when built to the specification that section sets out, is a decision with a decision-maker, a record, a counterparty, and an appeal. A placement can be denied unjustly, and the denial leaves something behind that a journalist, a court, or a future review can hold up.

That is the slope instrument of section 8.6.4 turned on this book's own proposal. Dependence that generates artifacts is safer than dependence that generates none, not because the first is benign but because the first can be measured and the second cannot. It is also, for the same reason, the more demanding proposal to build: everything in the comparison rests on the appeal being real, and section 8.3.3 says what makes it real.

I put a jobs guarantee ahead of a basic income on that ground, and on a second one the Finland experiment cannot settle: people ought to have the chance to contribute before anyone decides they are unemployable.

The remaining measures are real and smaller. Broadened social insurance — unemployment benefits, healthcare, affordable housing — protects people who cannot or choose not to work through a period of displacement. Designing work so that AI systems augment human judgment instead of substituting for it produces roles needing both, which neither full automation nor unassisted labor generates on its own. Giving smaller firms access to AI resources and expertise keeps employment from concentrating in step with the capability, the dynamic section 8.3.2 describes. Each of these keeps the floor from being the only thing holding.
8.3.5. Literacy, Training, and Where Each One Sits
Section 5.6.3 names AI literacy among developers, policymakers, and the public as a safeguard against authoritarian misuse: a safeguard nobody understands is a safeguard nobody can invoke. Section 5.4.3 names cross-training among developers, ethicists, and legal scholars as a lever on a system's immediate environment. Both depend on a question the phrases hide, which is where the teaching sits.

<<h>>The public tier is the one nobody owns<</h>>
Developers and students are the easy case, and the mechanisms are familiar: AI concepts and their ethical stakes introduced before higher education, interdisciplinary degree programs, specialized training for working engineers that goes past a compliance checklist. All of it reaches people already inside the pipeline or heading for it, and it works about as well as curriculum reform generally works.

The public is hard because no institution has the job. Elements of AI, a free course built jointly by the University of Helsinki and the Finnish company Reaktor, is the clearest instance of somebody treating public understanding as an obligation rather than a market: Finland funded its translation into every EU member-state language during its EU presidency, specifically to carry it past its own citizens [[cite:C0390]]. That took a national government deciding a course was infrastructure. Nothing in the ordinary funding of AI education produces that decision, which is why the public tier stays thin while the developer tier fills in.

Section 8.2.3 makes the sharper version of this point about reach: general AI awareness produces general opinions, and a public able to govern a technology is a public that has been asked about a particular use of it.

Education for communities historically locked out of the field belongs here rather than in a separate section about diversity. A next generation of developers drawn from a narrow slice of the population reproduces exactly the narrow set of assumptions section 6.1.1 identifies as a source of AI bias, which makes access a technical requirement on the systems as much as a fairness claim about who builds them.

<<h>>Elective or embedded<</h>>
For ethics training the real choice is between a course a student can decline and a requirement they cannot. Embedded EthiCS, developed at Harvard by the computer scientist Barbara Grosz and the philosopher Alison Simmons, pairs philosophy graduate students with computer-science faculty to write ethics modules directly into standard CS courses [[cite:C0455]]. The placement is the design. A stand-alone ethics class is taken by the students who elect it, and its content stays separable from the technical work in a student's mind because it was separable in the timetable. A module inside the algorithms course is not separable from the algorithms. Grosz's own course works the same way at the level of content, built on cases with consequences — predictive policing, public-health interventions, wildlife conservation — instead of abstract dilemmas [[cite:C0392]].

Certification does the equivalent work for people whose formal education is finished. The IEEE's CertifAIEd program builds a professional credential around accountability, transparency, privacy, and bias, open to developers and equally to the policymakers, underwriters, and human-resources staff whose work touches an AI system without building one [[cite:C0394]]. A credential is a weak instrument: it certifies that somebody sat the assessment, and no more. It is also the only instrument here that reaches anyone who has already left a university, which is most of the people making decisions about these systems today.
8.4. Developing Policy Frameworks for AI Ethics, Safety, and Antifascism
Good design inside a single lab does not by itself keep an AI system ethical or resistant to authoritarian misuse. That takes policy, and policy here means four instruments, each reaching ground the others cannot.

Legislation sets the floor that does not depend on a company volunteering for it: obligations on developers and users that can require the same kind of ethics review or independent audit biomedical research already answers to, applied instead to AI development.

Self-regulation covers what legislation is slow to reach. The Partnership on AI, whose corporate members include Amazon, Apple, and Google, commits its members to "tenets" of safety, transparency, and broad benefit — voluntarily, and with no legal force behind the commitment [[cite:C0348]]. What a commitment of that kind is worth is the question section 8.4.2 takes up, and its answer bears on everything else in this section.

Joint funding addresses a narrower problem: research on AI safety and ethics funded solely by the companies whose products it evaluates concentrates the funding and the findings in the same hands. Splitting that funding between government and industry keeps the incentive to find a problem from depending on who is paying to look for one.

None of it holds up without oversight — a body positioned like an institutional review board but built for AI, interdisciplinary and resourced enough to act on what it finds instead of only reporting it. What such a body specifically has to guard against, section 5.6.3 already covers, and section 6.4.3 covers the export-control and prohibited-practices levers that keep a capability from reaching a government that would misuse it.
8.4.1. National Policy Guidelines for AI Development, Employment, and Antifascism
A national AI policy has a predictable shape: fund value-alignment research and the work of operationalizing ethical principles inside a system; build standing interdisciplinary capacity; train a workforce; consult the public through assemblies, consultations, and partnerships with organizations outside government; pay for robustness and long-term safety research; and update data-privacy, cybersecurity, accountability and legal-status law where AI has outrun it, on which sections 8.4.4 and 8.6 carry the specifics. Every country that has written a national strategy has written most of that list. The list is not where national policies differ.

They differ in two places, both having to do with leverage.

The first is harmonization. A regulation with teeth in one country does little if a developer can relocate to one without them, which makes a national framework partly an instrument for binding other national frameworks — through mutual recognition, through market access, through the cost of maintaining separate compliance regimes side by side. Sections 8.7.4 and 8.7.6 work through how little of that has actually happened and which mechanisms have carried anything at all across a border. A policy written as though the country were alone is a policy about domestic firms, and the firms that matter most are not domestic anywhere.

The second is what a government adds to industry self-regulation. Section 8.4.2 spends its length on why a voluntary code is worth less than it announces. What national policy contributes is exactly the part self-regulation lacks by construction: a standard or certification program backed by government monitoring carries a cost for noncompliance, where a purely voluntary one carries a cost only to reputation, which the company itself gets to price.

Neither of these is expensive. Neither needs a new agency, a new science, or an argument the public has not already accepted. That both remain the exception is a fact about political attention, and not about difficulty.
8.4.2. Encouraging Industry Best Practices and Self-regulation
Self-regulation is industries developing and enforcing their own standards without government intervention — agile and responsive to technological change in a way legislation rarely is, and just as easily a cover for avoiding the accountability legislation would impose. Both things are true at once, and the rest of this section works out which one dominates in practice.

<<h>>The Dangers of Corporate Doublespeak in AI Ethics<</h>>
Corporate doublespeak — deliberately ambiguous, evasive, or inflated language meant to project ethical seriousness a company's actual practice does not support — is how self-regulation fails without anyone having to admit it failed. A commitment costs a company nothing if nothing enforces it.

The gap between statement and practice shows up first as erosion: a company announces a value and does not act as though it holds it afterward. Google's AI Principles, published in June 2018 and pledging to avoid AI applications built for weapons or mass surveillance, were themselves a response to months of internal and public backlash over Project Maven, a Pentagon contract that used Google's machine learning to analyze drone footage — thousands of employees signed a letter opposing the company's involvement, and roughly a dozen resigned [[cite:C0400]]. Having a set of principles is not the same as having upheld them since; the test of the pledge is what happened after it was made, not that it was made. OpenAI's history makes a related point from a different angle: founded on a public commitment to open publication, it withheld the full version of GPT-2 in 2019 over misuse concerns — a safety-motivated retreat from its own transparency norm that was itself controversial — and in the same year restructured from a nonprofit into a capped-profit company to raise the capital its research increasingly required [[cite:C0401]]. Neither move was dishonest on its face. Both show how a stated commitment to transparency erodes the moment an organization has a competing incentive strong enough to test it.

Public trust erodes the same way, faster and more visibly. In 2020, Google forced out Timnit Gebru, a co-lead of its Ethical AI team, after a dispute over a paper examining risks in large language models; Margaret Mitchell, the team's other co-lead, was dismissed the following year after searching for evidence of how Gebru had been treated [[cite:C0402]]. Thousands of Google employees and outside researchers signed a letter in response, and the episode became a reference point for a specific kind of skepticism: a company can fund and publicize an ethics research team while still overriding it the moment its findings become inconvenient. Whether the internal changes Google made afterward addressed that problem or only the public exposure it created is a judgment the affected researchers have made publicly themselves, and it is not one this book needs to make on their behalf.

External oversight is what would catch doublespeak that internal incentives don't stop. Facebook's content-moderation Oversight Board is one attempt at building it: a body structurally separated from Facebook's day-to-day decisions and funded through an irrevocable trust that Meta alone capitalized — a design meant to answer exactly the criticism that self-funded oversight is not real oversight, though critics have noted that a trust a company creates and funds once is not the same thing as a board with no relationship to the company it reviews [[cite:C0403]]. Whether that distinction holds up is a fair question to ask of any self-regulatory body a company funds itself, not a specific indictment of this one.

Misrepresentation of what a system can actually do rounds out the pattern. Clearview AI marketed its facial-recognition technology with claims of near-100-percent accuracy [[cite:C0404]], a figure independent testing did not support, and regulators in France and Italy later fined the company on separate grounds — unlawful collection of the biometric data the system was trained on [[cite:C0377]]. A company that overstates what its own system can do is doing a milder version of what doublespeak does with ethics commitments: describing the product it wishes it had built rather than the one that exists.

None of this is fixable by asking companies to doublespeak less. The same incentive that produces vague ethics language also has an obvious interest in the regulation that would eventually replace it — a version of the same behavior that is harder to document than a corporate pledge, because it happens before any public commitment exists to measure it against. What actually stops doublespeak is external enforcement: regulation with penalties, audits a company cannot decline, and oversight bodies whose funding and staffing do not run back to the company they review.

<<h>>Consequences of AI Self-regulation and Potential Power Imbalances<</h>>
Self-regulation concentrates power by default, not by conspiracy: whoever writes the standard also decides what counts as compliant, and a company self-regulating its own product is answering both questions itself. Amazon's Rekognition facial-recognition software is a documented instance of what that produces in practice — an MIT Media Lab audit found significantly higher error rates for darker-skinned and female faces than for the lighter-skinned male faces the system had apparently been tuned and tested against most closely [[cite:C0405]]. The bias was not caught by Amazon's own self-regulation; it took an outside audit the company did not commission.

The opacity problem this section's first head already covers with Google's Project Maven applies here too, from a different angle: a company operating without mandatory external review can prioritize competitiveness over the societal impact of what it ships, and nothing in a self-regulatory framework forces that trade-off into the open before it has already been made. The conflict-of-interest problem the Facebook Oversight Board illustrates above is the same structural issue applied to review bodies specifically: a company-funded board faces a version of the independence question any self-funded oversight has to answer, however genuinely it is designed to answer it.

What self-regulation cannot supply on its own is uniformity. Without an enforceable baseline, standards vary by company; a smaller developer without the resources for a well-staffed internal ethics function competes at a disadvantage against one that can afford the appearance of one, and a consumer has no reliable way to tell which AI system's ethical claims mean anything. Section 6.4.3 covers what a binding baseline looks like in practice, including the EU AI Act's Article 5 prohibitions — the kind of enforceable floor self-regulation, by definition, cannot set for itself.

None of this argues that self-regulation is worthless, only that it needs something outside itself to answer to. A synergy between government-set baselines and industry-led standards — mandatory requirements at the floor, voluntary practice above it — gets more of the agility self-regulation offers without giving up the accountability it structurally cannot provide alone.

<<h>>Assessing the Effectiveness of AI Self-regulation<</h>>
Whether self-regulation is working is itself measurable: the transparency of a company's stated ethical policies, how quickly it responds when a problem surfaces, and whether its safety commitments hold up under outside scrutiny are things a reader can check rather than take on faith.

The Partnership on AI, the industry coalition section 8.4 introduces, is a useful test case precisely because its own design limits what it can enforce: member companies interpret its guidelines individually, and nothing in the partnership's structure obliges any of them to follow through [[cite:C0348]]. OpenAI's 2018 Charter is a narrower, single-company version of the same instrument, committing the organization to prioritizing safety research and to collaborating with other research and policy institutions rather than racing them [[cite:C0406]] — and the same question applies to a charter as to a coalition's tenets: a document is only as good as what happens when honoring it costs something. The Gebru and Mitchell dismissals this section's first head already covers are the clearest test of that question this chapter has: public scrutiny, not the company's own stated commitments, was what produced a response.

Third-party audits, external advisory boards with real authority, and cross-stakeholder collaboration between regulators, researchers, and social scientists are what turn a stated commitment to ethics into something checkable. A handful of concrete indicators do the checking:

<<list>>
- Clear, actionable ethical guidelines that are actually adopted and enforced, not just published.
- Transparent disclosure of ethical policies, stated objectives, and progress against them.
- A documented track record of responding to ethical incidents promptly, with accountability attached.
- Internal enforcement mechanisms, including employee training that goes beyond a compliance formality.
- Ongoing internal evaluation of AI-related practices, paired with active research into emerging ethical questions.
- Substantive participation in multi-stakeholder collaborations like the Partnership on AI, rather than nominal membership.
<</list>>

A company that clears most of these is doing more than most competitors. A company that clears none of them while describing itself as an ethics leader is the doublespeak this section opened with, restated in a different register.
8.4.3. Public-Private Partnerships for AI Research and Innovation
Public-private partnerships pool government and private resources in ways neither side manages alone, and the model is well established in AI research: national research-institute programs pairing federal money with industry co-funders across themes from safety to human-AI interaction to climate, and UN-agency platforms convening governments, universities and companies around applications in health, education and poverty [[cite:C0407]] [[cite:C0408]].

None of them was built around the goal this book argues for, and that is the observation worth making here. A public-private partnership does what its mandate says and nothing beyond it, because the mandate is what the private partner agreed to and the only thing that partner can be held to. A partnership convened on "AI for good" produces work its members already wanted to do, filed under a heading nobody objects to. One convened on resistance to authoritarian misuse of AI would produce something else, and would have to be founded that way, since no partner joins a general-purpose venue and then discovers an obligation to take a political side.

The self-regulation risk section 8.4.2 examines applies here in a sharper form. A company treating participation as public relations is a familiar failure, and a partnership is a more effective venue for it than a voluntary code is, because the government partner supplies credibility the company could not have manufactured on its own.
8.4.4. Legal and ethical implications of AI systems gaining personhood
Whether an AI system should ever hold personhood is worked out in sections 8.6.1 and 8.6.2. What belongs in a chapter about policy frameworks is a narrower question those sections do not settle: who decides, and when.

Every version of this question that has actually reached a legislature so far arrived as a response to pressure — a liability dispute, a public controversy, a product already on the market — rather than from a standing body asking it in advance. That ordering is the problem. A legislature deciding under pressure decides badly, and it decides against a factual record assembled by whoever had an interest in assembling one. The interdisciplinary review body section 8.6.1 describes only does the work it is designed for if some jurisdiction creates it before a forcing case shows up, which means creating it while the question still looks academic and nobody's damages are riding on the answer.

That is a policy choice rather than a doctrinal one, and it is cheap relative to almost everything else in this chapter. A national AI strategy that names an agency, gives it a mandate to keep the question under review, and sets a trigger for revisiting it — a capability threshold, a deployment scale, a category of use — decides in advance who will be in the room. It does not decide the answer, and it should not try to. It decides that when the answer is needed there will be a body qualified to give one, which is the part no forcing case ever supplies on its own.
8.4.5. Encouraging the Development of Altruistic, Antifascist, and Ethical AI Systems
Sections 8.4.1 through 8.4.4 covered the instruments — national guidelines, self-regulation, public-private partnerships, legal status. What they are instruments for is the subject of this closing section: AI development that is altruistic and antifascist by design.

<<h>>Policies to Counteract Authoritarian Uses of AI<</h>>
Section 6.4.3 covers the core policy toolkit here — export controls, prohibited-practices regulation, developer-facing standards — and section 5.6.3 covers the safeguards a system needs built in from the start. One piece belongs upstream of all of them: measurement.

A government cannot restrict what it cannot see. The Carnegie Endowment's AI Global Surveillance Index assessed 176 countries and found AI-enabled surveillance technology already in active use in at least 75 of them [[cite:C0409]]. That number is the evidence base for a countermeasure and is not itself one. It also demonstrates what a measurement instrument has to do to be worth the effort: count deployments instead of commitments, name the countries, and publish on a schedule that makes a change from one year to the next visible. An index reporting only that surveillance AI exists somewhere in the world would give a policymaker nothing to act on, and most published AI-governance indicators are closer to that than to this one.

<<h>>AI Systems That Support Democratic Values<</h>>
Section 5.4.6 works through what this looks like with working examples — content scoring that informs a moderator without removing the content, structured public input on contested policy questions — along with the caution that belongs next to each.

One gap those examples leave open is election-integrity monitoring specifically: a system built to flag anomalies in real-time reporting, or to detect coordinated interference during a vote. What would make such a system trustworthy is the list section 5.4.6 already gives for any AI role in a democratic process — privacy-preserving design, an audit trail, and a multi-stakeholder review body that includes more than whichever party happens to hold power. That last condition is the one that fails first, and it fails structurally: an election-monitoring system is procured by an incumbent government, and an incumbent that chooses who audits the count has already answered the question the audit exists to ask.
8.5. Privacy, Security, and Antifascism in AI Systems
Privacy and security are not separable from antifascist design; they are two of the pieces it is built from. An AI system that leaks the data it touches, or that a hostile actor can quietly subvert, is available for exactly the surveillance and control this book argues against, whatever values sit in its training.

The EU's General Data Protection Regulation is the clearest existing legal precedent for treating data protection as a requirement rather than a design preference [[cite:C0333]]. The harder point about it is the one section 8.7.6 makes in general form: a law of this kind is built to govern what a private company may do with data, not to block a government's own authoritarian uses of AI, and nothing forces the two categories to overlap. Extending comparable protections to close that gap is the unfinished part of the precedent.

The two sections that follow take up each half of the problem: section 8.5.1 on the trade-off between data privacy and the data an AI system needs in order to learn, and section 8.5.2 on hardening the AI system itself against attack, since a privacy protection a bad actor can simply break through was never a protection.
8.5.1. Balancing Data Privacy, AI Innovation, and Antifascist Goals
AI systems need data to learn, and the data they need often includes exactly the information people most want to keep private. Differential privacy is one working answer: it adds carefully structured noise to a dataset's outputs, letting a model learn from the data in aggregate without exposing what any individual record contains. Apple's iOS implementation, covered in section 5.6.3, is a deployed example [[cite:C0350]].

Federated learning solves a related problem differently: instead of centralizing raw data at all, it trains a model across many devices, where each device computes an update locally and only that update — not the underlying text or images — gets sent back and aggregated. Google's Gboard keyboard uses this to improve next-word prediction from what people type, without collecting their raw keystrokes on a central server [[cite:C0412]].

Homomorphic encryption and secure multi-party computation push the same idea further, letting a system compute directly on encrypted or secret-shared data and produce a result without any party — including the system doing the computing — ever seeing the underlying values in the clear. Estonia's tax authority piloted a version of this: Sharemind, a secure multi-party computation platform built by the Estonian firm Cybernetica, split VAT records across servers run by separate organizations and computed fraud-risk scores from the shares without any one party reconstructing a company's full data. The pilot showed the approach could process a month of national tax data on modest hardware; it was evaluated rather than carried into production [[cite:C0413]].

Data minimization is the plainer version of the same principle: collect and keep only what a specific task actually requires, rather than defaulting to keeping everything a system might plausibly use later. Anonymization techniques such as k-anonymity — altering a dataset so no individual record is distinguishable from at least k-1 others — serve the same end for data that has to be shared or published rather than only queried against.

These techniques matter past individual privacy because centralized data is exactly what an authoritarian regime needs to turn an AI system into a surveillance tool. Differential privacy applied to a surveillance system doesn't stop the surveillance, but it does mean the data collected can't be traced back to a specific person after the fact, which changes what the system can be used for. Federated learning cuts more directly at the same risk: a design where no single server ever holds everyone's raw data is a design a government cannot simply seize and repurpose, because the raw data was never in one place to seize.
8.5.2. Strengthening Cybersecurity in AI-Enabled Systems
AI systems are woven into daily life and critical infrastructure, which makes their vulnerability to attack a different order of problem than an ordinary software bug: exploiting one doesn't just crash a program, it can misdirect a decision the system was trusted to get right.

Adversarial attacks are one specific version of this: a bad actor exploits a weakness in how a model represents its input — a vulnerability first documented in image classifiers over a decade ago [[cite:C0364]] — to make the system produce a wrong answer with high confidence. A well-documented case is the "stop sign" attack: researchers showed that a small set of black-and-white stickers, placed on a real stop sign, caused a standard road-sign classifier to read it as a speed limit sign in the large majority of video frames captured from a moving vehicle [[cite:C0414]].

Countering that kind of attack takes more than a single fix:

<<list>>
- Adversarial training exposes a model to adversarial examples during training rather than only ordinary data, which measurably increases its resistance to the same class of attack [[cite:C0365]].
- Architectures built for resistance, through techniques like defensive distillation or randomized smoothing, aim to make a model's predictions change less under small adversarial perturbations. Defensive distillation's protection turned out to be weaker than first claimed once stronger attacks were tested against it [[cite:C0415]]; randomized smoothing has held up better, offering a certified robustness guarantee within a bounded radius around each input [[cite:C0416]].
- AI-driven defense turns the same machine-learning methods toward protecting the system: Darktrace's Enterprise Immune System is one deployed example, learning an organization's normal network behavior and flagging deviations from it in real time [[cite:C0417]].
- Security audits — penetration tests and red-team exercises run against the system before deployment, not only after an incident — catch vulnerabilities an adversary would otherwise find first.
- Shared threat intelligence between developers, security researchers, and other stakeholders means a technique used against one AI system gets recognized the next time it is tried against a different one, instead of every organization discovering it independently.
- Governance frameworks — industry standards and regulatory requirements written specifically for AI system security, not adapted after the fact from general software security rules — set a floor that does not depend on any one developer's judgment.
<</list>>

None of these measures work in isolation. A model hardened by adversarial training that runs on infrastructure nobody is monitoring is still exposed, and a well-monitored system built on an architecture that folds under a basic perturbation attack is exposed a different way. Security here means the whole stack, not the strongest link in it.
8.6. Accountability, Legal Responsibility, and Antifascism in Sentient Machine Experimentation
Chapter 2 did the definitional and procedural work this section builds on: §2.4.1 argued that "is it conscious" and "can it suffer" are different questions with different evidence, §2.4.4 worked out what informed consent and an oversight board would need to mean for a system whose capacity to consent is itself unresolved, and §2.4.5 laid out the options for a sentient system's legal status. Each of those sections ended by handing a specific, unfinished question to this chapter: what makes a review board's ruling binding rather than advisory, what a court actually does with a system whose legal status is unsettled, and who answers for harm a sentient system causes or suffers. This section takes up those three questions in turn, then closes on what keeps the whole apparatus more than a policy document nobody enforces.
8.6.1. Subjecthood as a Legal Category, Not a Scientific One
Section 2.4.1 argued that sentience is not a threshold a system crosses but a set of separate, gradable capacities — nociception, learned avoidance, a self-model, something like suffering — each with its own uncertain evidence. That conclusion is a problem for a legal system specifically, because law generally needs a decidable rule: a court or a regulator has to say whether a given system is or is not a covered subject, on a specific date, even when the underlying science offers a spectrum with no natural cut point.

Law has faced this exact mismatch before and did not solve it by waiting for the science to resolve. Fetal viability, brain death, and the age of criminal responsibility are all legal thresholds drawn across what is actually a continuous biological process, defended not as scientific facts but as the least-bad place to put an enforceable line given what's at stake on either side of it. The same move is available here: a jurisdiction can set an operational threshold — a specific, testable benchmark on self-monitoring, learned aversive response, or behavioral flexibility under novel conditions — without claiming that benchmark settles what sentience actually is, only that it is where legal protection starts to attach.

Two designs are available, and they trade off differently. A single bright-line threshold is easy to enforce and easy to game: a system built to clear the specific test without having whatever the test was meant to detect. A graded framework — obligations that scale with how many benchmarks a system meets, echoing §2.4.1's own suggestion that protection should track capacity rather than a baseline bar — is harder to game but harder to adjudicate, since a court now has to weigh degree rather than answer yes or no.

Either design needs a body to apply it. §2.4.4 already specified its composition: AI researchers, consciousness scientists, ethicists, and legal scholars, interdisciplinary by design because no single field's expertise is sufficient on its own. What that section left open is what happens once the body rules — whether its judgment carries any force beyond its own credibility. That is the question the rest of this section takes up.
8.6.2. Legal Frameworks for Sentient AI Subjects
Section 2.4.5 laid out the options for a sentient AI system's legal status: extend personhood outright, the way a corporation already holds it; scale rights and protections with capacity rather than attach them at one threshold; write in a sunset clause that forces the law to revisit itself as the underlying systems change. It closed on two concrete cases neither existing law nor those three options resolve by themselves. This section takes each in turn.

A medical-diagnostic AI system makes an error with fatal consequences. Who answers for it depends entirely on what the system's legal status is doing at the moment of the error. Property law has a ready answer already: strict product liability holds a manufacturer responsible for a defective product's harm regardless of fault, and if a sentient system stays classified as property, that doctrine applies to it exactly as it would to a faulty pacemaker [[cite:C0418]]. The doctrine stops making sense at the point a system has enough autonomy that "defective" is the wrong description of what happened — a system that reasoned its way to a mistake, rather than malfunctioned, resembles an employee more than a faulty part. Employment law already has a doctrine for that case too: an employer answers for an employee's actions within the scope of employment, without the employee ceasing to be a responsible party in their own right [[cite:C0419]]. Which doctrine applies is not a free choice — it is exactly the graded-versus-bright-line question section 8.6.1 raises, resolved case by case according to how much of the error a system's own judgment, rather than its design, produced.

A companion AI's owner dies, leaving it without a designated purpose or further instruction. Existing law is not actually silent here — it has simply never been asked this particular version of a familiar question. Most jurisdictions with pet trust statutes already let an owner set aside money and name a caretaker for an animal that depends on them but cannot inherit or manage property itself [[cite:C0420]]. The same instrument, extended, gives a sentient AI system an answer that is neither inheritance as property nor legal personhood in full: continuity of care and a named successor guardian, decided in advance rather than litigated after the fact by whoever happens to hold the hardware.

Both cases point at the same underlying design choice, and it has already been tried. The European Parliament's 2017 report on civil law rules for robotics floated creating a specific legal status of "electronic personhood" for the most autonomous systems [[cite:C0421]]. More than 150 AI researchers, roboticists, and legal scholars signed an open letter opposing it, arguing that granting personhood to a system with no consciousness would let a manufacturer offload liability onto a legal fiction with no assets of its own, and that the proposal reached for a solution to a legal problem before establishing that the entity in question had the properties that would justify it [[cite:C0422]]. That objection is exactly right for a system that is not sentient — and exactly wrong for one that is, once §2.4.1's threshold work in section 8.6.1 has been done. Legal status, on this account, is not a single policy to adopt or reject; it is a designation that has to track the threshold question honestly, which is why section 8.6.1 has to come first.
8.6.3. Making Review Binding
Section 2.4.4 worked out what an oversight board for sentient-AI research needs to look like — interdisciplinary, empowered to require a less invasive substitute for a risky protocol, able to trigger re-review when a system under study develops capacities its original consent didn't cover — and ended on the question this section answers: what makes that board's ruling something a researcher has to obey, rather than a recommendation a determined lab can route around.

Human-subjects research already solved a version of this problem, and the solution is institutional rather than criminal. An institutional review board's authority does not come from any power to fine or arrest a noncompliant researcher directly. It comes from a federalwide assurance: an institution promises, as a condition of eligibility for federal research funding, that all of its human-subjects research will go through review, and a federal office can suspend that assurance if the promise is broken — cutting off an entire institution's federal funding, not just the offending lab's [[cite:C0423]]. The leverage is economic, not punitive, and it works precisely because almost no research institution can function without the funding at stake.

The same structure transfers to sentient-AI research directly. A funding body, a journal's editorial standards, or a jurisdiction's research-licensing regime can condition eligibility on review-board clearance the same way federal grants already condition eligibility on IRB clearance — making noncompliance expensive to the institution rather than merely improper for the researcher. What section 8.6.1 leaves open is which cases the board needs to see at all: a graded framework means low-capacity systems can be exempted from full review the way minimal-risk human studies already qualify for expedited review, freeing the board's scrutiny for the cases where a system's status is contested.

<<h>>What the board applies<</h>>
Section 2.4.6 already named the substantive standard: animal-research ethics' Three Rs — replace an experiment with a non-sentient alternative wherever one exists, reduce the number of sentient subjects and the duration of exposure to the minimum an experiment actually needs, and refine the protocol to minimize whatever harm remains. A binding board is what makes those three questions something a protocol has to answer before it runs, not language a paper cites afterward to justify what already happened. In practice, applying the Three Rs to a candidate sentient system means asking, in order: can a simulation or a non-sentient model answer the same question; if not, what is the minimum exposure that still produces a valid result; and given that minimum, what escalation triggers stop the study before harm compounds. A board with teeth can require a negative answer to the first question be documented, not merely asserted, before it will consider the second.

The Three Rs' own logic — defaulting toward the least invasive option available — argues for a specific evidentiary rule at every stage of this apparatus: a system's default experimental status stays the more protective one until the case for the less protective status is made, not the reverse. That default has a cost, and section 2.4.7 is explicit that neither error it produces is free: it will sometimes extend protection to a system that turns out not to have needed it, and sometimes, before a threshold is met, withhold protection from one that did. The Three Rs answer only what a board does once a subject is inside its scope. Where the line sits at all is section 8.6.1's question, not this one.
8.6.4. What Keeps the Apparatus More Than Aspirational
Section 2.4.7 closed on the real risk this whole structure faces: institutions that defend the classification they started with rather than revisit it when evidence changes. A review board, a legal-status framework, and an enforcement mechanism tied to funding eligibility — sections 8.6.1 through 8.6.3 — solve the design problem. None of it survives contact with an institution that has stronger incentives to avoid a finding than to make one.

Transparency is the first defense, and it works by making avoidance visible rather than by appealing to good faith. A research program that publishes its protocols, its review-board submissions, and its outcomes — including the ones that didn't work — gives outside researchers, journalists, and future review boards something to check a claim of compliance against. A program that publishes only its successes is not transparent in any sense that matters here; selective disclosure is a more sophisticated way of avoiding scrutiny, not an alternative to it.

The second defense is the one section 8.6.3's enforcement mechanism doesn't cover by itself: protection for the people inside an institution who notice a problem the institution would rather not have found. A funding-eligibility penalty deters an institution from bypassing review, but it does nothing for the individual researcher who reports that a colleague did anyway — and an institution facing that penalty has every incentive to make the reporter's position uncomfortable rather than fix what was reported. Whistleblower protections, and a reporting channel that doesn't route through the people being reported on, are what make section 8.6.3's mechanism something a researcher can use rather than something that exists on paper and gets bypassed in practice.

The third is standing infrastructure rather than a one-time policy. The Partnership on AI, cited already in section 5.6.3 for its role pooling perspective across companies, researchers, and civil-society groups [[cite:C0348]], is one example of the kind of body that keeps a set of standards live — revisited as the underlying technology changes, rather than fixed at the moment a policy was written and left to age. A culture that treats its ethical commitments as a completed project is the same failure mode section 2.4.7 warned about, applied to institutions instead of individual classifications: having gotten it right once is not the same thing as staying right.

<<h>>Measure the slope, not the level<</h>>
All three defenses share a weakness, and it is the first feature in section 2.1.4's structural signature: an institution can keep every one of them and metabolize all of them. The channel exists, the protection is written down, the standing body meets. Dissent is permitted to appear, the institution points at the appearance as proof it listens, and nothing the dissent was meant to move moves.

There is no threshold that detects this. Any real institution has some overlap between the authority that sets a standard and the authority that grants exceptions to it, and registering an objection is always at least slightly more costly than staying quiet. Asking whether an institution has crossed into capture asks for a line nobody can draw.

The question that does work is a derivative rather than a level. Is the cost of registering dissent rising, year over year, while the underlying risk stays flat? Is the required justification getting longer? Are there more approvers than there were? Are the roles that gate an exception migrating toward the roles that set the standard being excepted from? None of those is damning in a single year, and all of them are measurable across several.

The reason to prefer the slope is that it is harder to fake. An institution can present a level — we have a whistleblower channel, we have an ethics board — and the presentation costs nothing. A trend in the cost of using the channel is composed of artifacts the institution generated itself for other reasons, and cannot be curated after the fact without falsifying a record. Where the record is public, the slope is not even self-reported: anyone outside can measure whether dissent has been getting more expensive.

Two cautions belong with this, because an instrument for detecting recuperation is an unusually easy thing to recuperate. The first is that whoever measures the slope must not also gate the exceptions; a review that acquires the power to block becomes the thing it was built to watch. The second is that the measurement has to be refusable — an audit that observes is a different object from a committee that approves, and the same hundred words of written justification are dissent when the dissenter prices them and tribute when the gatekeeper does. The text of the document cannot tell those apart. Only the refusal rights can.
8.7. International and Regional Governance
No single country can bind AI development to altruistic and antifascist values on its own, and no two regions have tried to do it the same way.

Sections 8.7.1 and 8.7.2 cover cooperation from the bottom up: how research institutions build the working relationships cross-border AI research depends on, and how the technical community writes standards for itself without waiting for governments. Section 8.7.3 asks what that kind of collaboration can and cannot fix once the underlying problem is climate, disease, poverty, or conflict instead of AI ethics as such. Sections 8.7.4 and 8.7.5 turn to jurisdictions: what a handful of regions have actually legislated, how little of it harmonizes, and what adapting a system to a local context does and does not license. Section 8.7.6 then takes the whole landscape at the scale of states — treaties, declarations, export controls, weapons, and what a democratic dividend is actually worth.
8.7.1. How Research Institutions Cooperate Across Borders

Altruistic, antifascist AI cannot be built inside one country's research institutions alone; training data, safety techniques, and researchers themselves cross borders more easily than most other engineering work does. What that cooperation actually looks like is narrower than a treaty and less formal than a certification body: shared tools, shared training runs, and shared publication norms — the layer underneath both the technical standards section 8.7.2 covers and the government-to-government structures section 8.7.6 covers.

The clearest example is also one of the oldest. OpenAI released Gym, an open-source toolkit for building and comparing reinforcement-learning algorithms [[cite:C0491]]. Any lab working on reinforcement learning, in any country, could build against the same benchmark environments instead of writing its own, and results from competing labs became directly comparable because their authors had trained against the same suite. That is not a partnership, and it produced something a partnership cannot: comparability between organizations that compete with each other for the same funding, researchers, and attention, and that had no reason to coordinate anything.

A more recent example runs the same logic at a larger scale. The BigScience workshop, coordinated by Hugging Face, brought together more than a thousand researchers from over seventy countries to build BLOOM, a 176-billion-parameter multilingual language model released under a license written specifically to restrict harmful use, its training data and code published openly for anyone to inspect [[cite:C0492]]. Nothing compelled any of those researchers to take part, and nothing compels the next comparable effort to happen at all. The model exists because enough institutions decided cooperating on that particular project was worth more than competing on it.

That is the limit of the mechanism and the reason it is worth naming anyway. Shared infrastructure binds nobody and is therefore available where nothing binding is: a lab in a country that has signed no convention can still train against the same benchmark and publish against the same baseline. It also stops at the resource line. A research institution without the compute to run the benchmark is outside the arrangement no matter how open the arrangement is, which is the digital-divide problem section 8.7.6 covers, showing up here as a membership condition nobody wrote down.
8.7.2. Standards Built by the Technical Community, Not by Governments
Section 8.7.6 covers the treaty-and-declaration landscape states negotiate directly: a binding Council of Europe convention, the OECD's recommendation, UNESCO's, the two industry-and-government partnerships, the safety-institute network built after the Bletchley Declaration. A different, older layer of standard-setting sits underneath all of it, built by engineers rather than diplomats, and it deserves its own account.

The IEEE's Ethically Aligned Design, first published in 2019, set out eight general principles for autonomous and intelligent systems and, more consequentially, a follow-on series of engineering standards — the IEEE 7000 series — that translate those principles into something a development team can actually test against: value-based requirements engineering (IEEE 7000, 2021) and a well-being impact assessment (IEEE 7010), among a growing list addressing bias, transparency, and algorithmic provenance [[cite:C0448]]. The document informed the OECD's own AI Principles the same year, among other governmental efforts — engineers wrote a vocabulary for what an ethical AI system does before most governments had written a rule about it [[cite:C0449]].

ISO/IEC 42001, published in December 2023, took that layer further: the first management-system standard for AI, structured like the standards a company already uses for information security or quality control, specifying what a functioning AI governance program inside an organization has to include — risk assessment, an impact assessment for each system, oversight of third-party suppliers — and auditable by an accredited certification body the way those other standards are [[cite:C0450]].

What a technical standard does that a treaty cannot is bind through the market rather than through a state's consent. A government uninterested in constraining AI has no reason to ratify a human-rights convention; a company that wants to sell into a market, or to a customer, that requires ISO 42001 certification has a reason regardless of what its home government thinks of the standard's content. That advantage has limits — a company operating entirely inside a market that never asks for the certification has no reason to seek it either, and certification measures whether a management system exists, not whether the system it describes is followed with any rigor day to day. It is a different lever from the ones section 8.7.6 covers, not a substitute for them, and it reaches an authoritarian government's own AI systems, built and used domestically with no export or customer relationship at stake, not at all.
8.7.3. What International AI Collaboration Can and Cannot Fix

AI collaboration reaches furthest where the underlying problem is inherently cross-border and no single country's data is sufficient on its own. Climate is the clearest case: weather and climate systems do not respect national boundaries, and a model trained only on one region's data predicts poorly everywhere else. The institutional machinery here is real and worth describing precisely, because descriptions of it are usually inflated. The Climate, Land, Energy and Water Systems framework — coordinated by UN bodies together with the International Atomic Energy Agency, with a capacity-building program that has reached participants in more than twenty countries — is an integrated systems-optimization framework, and the research literature around it treats machine learning as a promising addition still being built in, not a feature the framework already has [[cite:C0493]]. Where AI collaboration does the specific climate work this section is about, it currently looks more like national weather services training and benchmarking forecasting models against shared data than like a single named research consortium.

Disease surveillance is a similar case for the same underlying reason: a pathogen crossing from an animal population into a human one, or from one country into the next, does not stay inside the jurisdiction that first detects it. The Global Virome Project, proposed as a successor to USAID's PREDICT program, set out to catalog the viral families with the highest zoonotic spillover potential across dozens of countries before the next pandemic instead of after it [[cite:C0494]]. The AI in that picture sits downstream of the cataloging. Sequence data of the kind such a project generates is what spillover-risk prediction models get trained on, which makes the international data-sharing arrangement itself, and not any single algorithm, the part actually worth calling collaboration.

Poverty and economic development are where this kind of cross-border AI collaboration is least developed. The general claims are easy to make — AI-driven analysis revealing patterns of inequality that guide where aid and investment go, better agricultural forecasting, financial services reaching places that have none. A specific, verifiable international partnership doing sustained work at that scale is harder to find than those claims suggest. What exists at the institutional level is the digital-divide problem section 8.7.6 covers in detail: uneven data, uneven compute, uneven regulatory capacity, and a recurring set of proposed remedies, capacity building and technology transfer among them, that has not yet closed the gap.

Conflict prevention is a fourth domain where the underlying data problem — dispersed, multilingual, and produced by people with no interest in cooperating with researchers — makes international collaboration closer to a precondition than an option. PeaceTech Lab, an independent nonprofit that grew out of a US Institute of Peace project, is a working example: its hate-speech lexicon project has combined machine learning with local-language workshops and interviews to build region-specific glossaries of inflammatory language in at least eleven countries, including Kenya, Nigeria, Iraq, and Yemen — ground-level data collaboration that international peacebuilding tools depend on and rarely get [[cite:C0495]].

None of these four cases is evidence that AI collaboration works the way the treaty layer aspires to. Each one ran on the researchers and institutions involved choosing to participate, project by project, with no enforcement mechanism binding the next one to happen and no guarantee that a government hostile to the work could not simply keep its own researchers out of it. Section 6.4.1 catalogs what a government willing to point AI at its own population does with tools built for exactly these humanitarian purposes elsewhere. A government can fund pandemic-surveillance research of the kind described above and, separately, build the biometric-surveillance systems that section documents, and nothing about participating in the first constrains the second.
8.7.4. Comparing and Harmonizing Regional AI Rules
Section 8.7.6 compares how the European Union, the United States, the United Kingdom, and China actually regulate AI within their own borders: a comprehensive risk-tiered statute, a sectoral patchwork built on agencies that predate AI, principles routed through existing regulators, and a licensing regime built to police a communications channel rather than a product. Those four already diverge sharply. The regions that comparison leaves out diverge again, in different directions, and what actually crosses a border between any of them turns out to be narrower than a shared statute.

Canada regulates its own federal government's use of AI through a binding rule, the 2019 Directive on Automated Decision-Making, which requires an algorithmic impact assessment, scaled to the decision's stakes, before a federal system can make or assist a decision that affects a person [[cite:C0501]]. A broader statute meant to extend comparable obligations to private companies, the Artificial Intelligence and Data Act, spent three years moving through Parliament and then died with it: when Parliament was prorogued in January 2025, the underlying bill, C-27, died on the order paper, and had not been reintroduced as of mid-2026. A framework that depends on one government's survival is a fragile thing for a trading partner to harmonize with.

Singapore took the opposite structural bet: no statute, a voluntary Model AI Governance Framework that its privacy regulator and infocomm authority first published in 2019 and revised in 2020, giving companies detailed but non-binding guidance on internal AI governance [[cite:C0502]]. Since 2022, a companion toolkit called AI Verify has let a company test its own system against the framework's principles and produce a report a regulator, a customer, or the public can inspect. It is the UK's wager taken further: not just guidance routed through an existing regulator, but guidance backed by no regulator's legal mandate at all, only the market pressure of a public test result.

Taiwan took a third path, and only recently. Its Legislative Yuan passed an AI Basic Act in December 2025, promulgated the following month as the country's first dedicated AI statute: twenty articles built around seven principles, among them transparency and explainability, fairness and non-discrimination, and accountability, administered by the National Science and Technology Council [[cite:C0503]]. The principles echo the UK's five almost point for point. What differs is the instrument: Taiwan wrote them into binding statute rather than administrative guidance, landing somewhere between the EU's comprehensive-statute model and the UK's lighter one.

One place harmonization is actually happening, rather than being proposed, is narrower than any of this: cross-border data flows. In March 2022, the US Department of Commerce and Singapore's trade and infocomm authorities agreed to align their AI governance frameworks and expand Singapore's participation in APEC's Cross-Border Privacy Rules System, a certification that lets a company demonstrate it meets one common privacy standard and have every participating economy recognize that single certification rather than auditing the company separately in each [[cite:C0504]]. That is what cross-border harmonization actually looks like in practice: not a shared global framework, but a small set of governments willing to certify to the same standard and recognize each other's certifications.

None of this converges toward a single global standard, and nothing here suggests it will. What actually crosses a border is a certification a handful of regulators agree to recognize, a technical standard a company adopts because a customer requires it, or a treaty obligation written at the level of human rights rather than a specific rule — section 8.7.2 covers the first two mechanisms, and section 8.7.6 covers the third. Harmonizing regional AI rules is less a matter of writing one shared framework than of finding, case by case, a mechanism a government working from a different premise still has reason to follow.
8.7.5. Adapting Regulatory Frameworks and Ethical Considerations to Local Contexts
Adapting AI regulation and ethical practice to local context means responding to the actual needs, preferences, and values of a given community. It does not mean accepting the reduction that has a state's own population consenting, culturally, to whatever is done to them — that Chinese people are content with pervasive surveillance because harmony is a Chinese value. A system built to work well has to be sensitive to real cultural, social, and moral differences: one developed in a Western country may treat individual autonomy as the default good, one developed in an East Asian country may treat group harmony as the default instead, and both assumptions have something to recommend them, since autonomy supports creativity and innovation while harmony supports cooperation and stability. Recognizing the difference is a design constraint. It is not a political alibi, and the two collapse into each other exactly where a government wants them to.

Holding that line takes something more specific than good intentions, because the difference between adaptation and alibi is invisible in the design itself. It is visible in who did the adapting. A system whose local adjustments were specified by the people subject to it has been adapted. A system whose local adjustments were specified by the government that will operate it is something else, and both produce identical documentation.

The composition of the design process is therefore the operative test, and it is testable from outside. The AI4D program is the clearest instance at scale: launched as AI4D Africa, a partnership between Canada's International Development Research Centre and Sweden's international development agency, later extended with the UK's Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office into South and Southeast Asia and the Pacific [[cite:C0505]]. What carries the weight there is the direction of the work: local researchers, policymakers, and practitioners building the system, instead of receiving one built elsewhere and configured on their behalf.

The same test explains why inclusion is a performance requirement here and not an ethical add-on. A healthcare AI system built around the access, resources, and assumptions of a well-funded hospital underperforms everywhere else. An education system built around a single learning style, or a single set of accessibility assumptions, serves only the learners who already fit it. Both are failures of adaptation by the standard just given: somebody specified a local context, and it was not the context the system was deployed into.
8.7.6. The Geopolitics of Ethical AI

AI capability is now national infrastructure, and states treat it accordingly: as a source of economic advantage, military reach, and administrative control over their own populations. Section 5.4.6's point about dual use is the one that governs here, raised to the scale of a state: what decides how a capability is used is who controls it, what law constrains that control, and whether anyone outside the controlling government can check the result.

<<h>>AI as a Strategic National Asset<</h>>
China's 2017 "New Generation Artificial Intelligence Development Plan" set an explicit national target: becoming the world leader in AI theory, technology, and application by 2030 [[cite:C0233]] [[cite:C0232]]. Other governments have made comparable, if less centrally coordinated, commitments, treating AI leadership the way they treat energy security or advanced manufacturing — a capability no state wants to depend on a rival for. The competition is not limited to the two states with the deepest research base: the United Arab Emirates created the world's first cabinet-level minister of state for artificial intelligence in 2017, treating a national AI strategy as worth its own ministry well before most governments had drafted one at all [[cite:C0424]].

That competition has increasingly focused on a single physical bottleneck: the advanced chips AI systems run on. Three companies sit at the center of the supply chain that produces them — Taiwan's TSMC, which fabricates roughly nine in ten of the most advanced chips in the world, and the Netherlands' ASML, the sole source of the extreme-ultraviolet lithography machines that fabrication requires — which is why a dispute over Taiwan's status is also, mechanically, a dispute over who gets to build the next generation of AI systems at all [[cite:C0425]]. The United States has restricted, and periodically revised, the export of its most advanced AI accelerators and chip-manufacturing equipment to China since 2022, on the stated ground that unrestricted access would accelerate Chinese military and surveillance capability, and has pressed the Netherlands and Japan, the two other governments whose companies sit at chokepoints in the same supply chain, to restrict their own exports in parallel [[cite:C0426]]. Whatever one thinks of that specific policy, it makes a general point concrete: AI capability depends on physical infrastructure that a small number of states and companies control, which means AI strategic competition is also, underneath the software, a competition over hardware supply chains.

That competitive framing carries a cost. A government racing to field a capability first has a structural reason to defer the harder question of how the capability gets used, against whom, and under what oversight. Section 6.4.1 catalogs what AI infrastructure looks like once turned toward those uses: mass biometric surveillance, automated censorship at a scale no human review team could match, and the patchwork of systems popularly, and inaccurately, described as a single Chinese "social credit score." None of it requires different technology from the uses that fill the rest of this book; it requires a government willing to point an existing capability at its own population, and no external constraint stopping it.

States are no longer the only actors this competition runs through. The cost of training the most capable AI models has grown several-fold every year for the better part of a decade, a trend that puts frontier development out of reach for all but a handful of well-capitalized companies and concentrates effective control of the most capable models in whichever government those companies happen to be headquartered under, regardless of how that government's own public AI spending compares to a rival's [[cite:C0427]]. A small number of American and Chinese companies now do most of the work this section's own vocabulary describes as a contest between states, which means a government's actual leverage over its own national AI capability depends on how much of that capability sits inside companies it can regulate, tax, or requisition, and how much sits inside companies it can only ask.

The same infrastructure argument runs the other way. A state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press, and courts a citizen can actually use gets a democratic dividend from identical technology: fraud detection that does not double as a loyalty test, disaster response that does not double as population tracking. Stated that way the dividend is a property a state either has or lacks, and I no longer think that is right. Section 8.7.7 works out the three ways in which having the institutions fails to be sufficient, and the third of them is the one this book had been missing.

Strategic competition over AI is not, by itself, the problem. It is the setting the rest of this section's arguments have to work inside, whether the subject is a weapons program, an international standard, or a domestic statute.

<<h>>Military Applications and the Limits of Autonomy<</h>>
Lethal autonomous weapons systems raise a specific version of the general problem: a machine making a targeting decision that used to require a person is a machine exercising judgment where the law of armed conflict has always assumed a human exercised it. Four questions recur across the debate:

<<list>>
- Autonomy versus oversight: how much decision authority a system can hold before human oversight becomes nominal, present on paper but not inside the timeline the system actually operates on.
- Discrimination and proportionality: whether a system can reliably distinguish combatants from civilians and weigh military advantage against civilian harm the way international humanitarian law's principles of distinction and proportionality require [[cite:C0428]].
- Accountability: who answers for a wrong engagement, the operator who deployed the system, the command that authorized it, or the developer who built it, when no single person made the specific decision.
- Auditability: whether the reasoning behind a targeting decision can be reconstructed and reviewed afterward, or disappears into a system no one outside its development team can examine.
<</list>>

International criminal law already has a doctrine built to answer the accountability question for a human subordinate: command responsibility, under which a commander who knew or should have known a subordinate was committing a war crime, and failed to prevent or punish it, bears criminal responsibility without having pulled the trigger personally [[cite:C0429]]. The doctrine does not transfer cleanly to a machine subordinate. A commander cannot know an algorithm's intentions the way they can infer a soldier's; punishment does not deter a weapon the way it deters a person who fears it; and the chain a court would need to reconstruct runs backward through training data and design decisions made years before the engagement, not through an order given minutes before it. Whether an autonomous weapon fits inside an accountability framework built for human chains of command, or needs one built from scratch, is unresolved by more than a technicality — existing law was not built with a nonhuman subordinate in mind.

There is a fourth party the list above does not name, and by the time this book was finished it had become the live one. The operator, the command and the developer are all inside the military. The developer of the model is not. A targeting platform assembled by a defense contractor now routinely incorporates a general-purpose model licensed from a commercial vendor — a vendor with a published acceptable-use policy, no contractual obligation to log how its model is used downstream, and no right to inspect the deployment. Section 6.4.1 gives the concrete case.

Neither doctrine reaches that party, and the reasons are worth stating because they are structural rather than accidental. Command responsibility does not reach the vendor because the vendor is not in the chain of command; nobody at the company can order a strike, prevent one, or punish anyone for one. Product liability does not reach it either, because the model is not defective in the sense the doctrine requires — it did what it was built to do, and it was integrated by a sophisticated intermediary who chose the application. The vendor sits in the gap between the two, and the gap was not designed; it is what you get when you apply two bodies of law written for soldiers and for manufacturers to an actor who is neither.

The vendor's own account of this is the one quoted in section 6.4.1: responsibility remains with the military organization. Taken as a claim about who decides, that is true. Taken as a claim about who could have known, it is a choice presented as a fact, which brings up the failure mode I think is most useful to name.

<<h>>"I do not know whether my system was used"<</h>>
Section 9.1.1 covers the responsibility gap in the sense Matthias gave it: as control over a learning system's behavior distributes across designers, trainers, and operators, no one party retains enough of it to be fairly held answerable, and the gap grows as the system becomes more autonomous. That is a gap in causal control.

What the vendor case exhibits is different and, I want to argue, more tractable. It is a gap in causal visibility, which is a different thing. The vendor cannot say whether its model contributed to a particular engagement — not because responsibility is distributed, but because nobody instrumented the question. No logging requirement was written into the contract, no inspection right was reserved, no audit channel exists. The company can truthfully say it does not know, and the truth of that statement is the product of decisions it participated in making.

That distinction matters because the two gaps have different fixes, and only one of them has a fix at all. Matthias's gap is a genuine property of learning systems and may not be closable. Absent visibility is an instrumentation choice. Logging is a solved engineering problem. Contractual inspection rights are ordinary commercial instruments. A vendor that wanted to know could require, as a condition of licensing into a targeting platform, that categories of use be recorded and made available to a named auditor. That such terms are not standard is a fact about market power and procurement leverage, not about the difficulty of the problem.

Naming it as fixable is the useful move, because it converts a lament into a specification. And it is worth being fair about what the record shows: the same commitments are not all of one kind. A vendor that refuses to permit fully autonomous weapons targeting or mass domestic surveillance, and holds that line when a government threatens contract termination and a supply-chain-risk designation and the dispute ends in litigation, has made a commitment with a price attached and paid it [[cite:C0714]]. A commitment to a use policy nobody can verify because nobody is logging anything is a different object wearing the same words. The first kind can fail and be seen to fail. The second cannot even be tested. Section 8.4.2's distinction between a claim checked by a party with a stake and one checked by a party without applies here exactly, and this is the case that shows why it is not an academic distinction.

DARPA's LongShot program is a useful test case precisely because it resists the extremes. It develops an uncrewed aircraft, launched from a crewed fighter, that carries and fires air-to-air missiles at a target while the crewed aircraft stays farther from the engagement [[cite:C0430]]. That is autonomy in the sense of independent flight and weapons carriage, not autonomy in the sense of a machine deciding on its own whom to kill: the engagement decision still runs through the launching platform. Where later variants of this kind of system draw that line, and whether "keep the pilot farther from danger" quietly becomes "remove the pilot from the decision," is the design question this technology eventually forces on whichever country builds it.

No binding international agreement governs any of this. States have negotiated inside the United Nations' Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons framework for more than a decade without producing one, converging on general language — meaningful human control matters, existing international humanitarian law applies — without settling what "meaningful" requires at machine speed [[cite:C0431]]. What exists instead is a set of practices individual militaries adopt on their own. The United States' Department of Defense Directive 3000.09, most recently updated in 2023, is the clearest published example: it requires that autonomous and semi-autonomous weapon systems be designed to allow "appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force," and distinguishes a fully autonomous system that selects and engages targets without further human intervention, "human out of the loop," from a human-supervised system and a semi-autonomous one that only engages targets a person has already selected [[cite:C0432]]. What "appropriate" requires is left to the officers approving each system, which is either a sensible acknowledgment that context varies or a loophole big enough to drive a fully autonomous system through, depending on who is answering. None of this binds a state that declines to adopt a comparable policy, the same enforcement gap section 6.4.2's account of technical safeguards runs into: a safeguard only the building state can verify is a policy, not a check.

The dynamic that worries most people who study this is not a single rogue system but an arms race in speed. If one state fields autonomous targeting because it computes faster than a human-supervised alternative, its competitors face pressure to match the speed rather than the oversight, and the state that keeps meaningful human control loses whatever tactical edge the state that dropped it gains. Slowing that dynamic is not a problem any single government's domestic policy can solve on its own; it needs the kind of coordination the next section takes up.
