Civil society has pushed for that coordination to take the form of an outright ban rather than a code of practice. The Campaign to Stop Killer Robots, a coalition of nongovernmental organizations founded in 2013, has lobbied for a binding treaty prohibiting weapons that select and engage targets without human control, and a UN General Assembly resolution on autonomous weapons has passed by a lopsided majority in successive years without producing the treaty the coalition wants [[cite:C0433]]. The gap between a resolution most states will vote for and a treaty the states that build these systems will sign is the same gap this section keeps running into: agreement is easy at the level of principle and hard at the level of a specific weapon a specific military does not want to give up.

<<h>>International Governance and Cooperation<</h>>
Almost none of this is binding. What has emerged instead is mostly a set of voluntary, overlapping, specialized bodies, closer to how international environmental and trade norms developed piecemeal than to a single founding charter — with one recent exception.

The Council of Europe's Framework Convention on Artificial Intelligence and Human Rights, Democracy and the Rule of Law is the first legally binding international treaty specifically on AI. Negotiated by the Council of Europe's member states alongside outside participants that included the European Union and the United States, it requires five ratifications, at least three of them Council of Europe members, before it enters into force [[cite:C0434]]. What it binds signatories to is written at the level of a human-rights treaty and not a technical code: consistency with human rights, democracy, and the rule of law across an AI system's lifecycle, with the operational detail of how a signatory meets that obligation left to domestic law. It is a foundation binding AI law can be built on, and not a substitute for the domestic statutes discussed below.

Everything else in this space is voluntary, and the roll is longer than it is deep. The OECD's Recommendation on Artificial Intelligence was the first intergovernmental AI standard, and its principles became the basis for the G20's own [[cite:C0435]]. The European Commission's High-Level Expert Group on AI produced "Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI," whose seven requirements later shaped the structure of the EU AI Act [[cite:C0235]]. UNESCO's Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence, adopted by all 193 member states, is the broadest formal endorsement any AI ethics instrument has received [[cite:C0411]]. The Global Partnership on AI is a standing venue for research and policy coordination between governments [[cite:C0234]], and the Partnership on AI does the same work across companies and researchers [[cite:C0242]]. The G7's Hiroshima AI Process pairs guiding principles for every actor in the AI lifecycle with a code of conduct for organizations building the most advanced systems, and carries legal force in none of the participating countries [[cite:C0436]]. A narrower, more technical layer sits alongside all of it: the Bletchley Declaration committed 29 signatories to cooperate specifically on frontier-model risk, and the national AI Safety Institutes it produced later joined an International Network whose members agreed to share testing methodology and run joint evaluations of the same frontier models instead of each working from a separate standard [[cite:C0437]] [[cite:C0438]].

The summit series that produced the Bletchley Declaration continued past it and, in doing so, showed how quickly this kind of voluntary consensus can fray. A follow-up summit in Seoul in 2024 kept the same safety framing; a third, in Paris in February 2025, rebranded itself the AI Action Summit and shifted its emphasis from risk toward adoption and opportunity, and its closing statement was not signed by the United States or the United Kingdom, two of the series' own founding participants [[cite:C0439]]. A coordination mechanism that depends on every major AI developer's home government agreeing to the same framing is only as durable as that agreement, and within two years of Bletchley, the agreement had already come apart.

None of these bodies can compel a state or a company that ignores it. Each is a standard, a set of principles, or a forum, useful for building the shared vocabulary that eventually makes binding law possible, and that gap between voluntary standard and binding law is the default state of international coordination on anything individual states have reasons to handle differently. The EU AI Act, discussed below, is the first instance of this kind of groundwork becoming enforceable law, and it took most of a decade of standard-setting exactly like this to get there. The proliferation carries its own cost in the meantime: a company or a smaller government trying to comply with "international AI governance" faces a shifting handful of overlapping standards, each with different membership and scope and no formal mechanism for reconciling a conflict between them, which is why "the international community agrees" is, at the level of any specific rule, usually false. What states have done once left to write their own rules is the subject of what follows.

<<h>>Democracy, Authoritarianism, and Diverging National Rules<</h>>
Section 6.4.1 names six documented forms of authoritarian AI misuse, among them social scoring, mass biometric surveillance, and automated censorship, and section 6.4.3 lays out the policy levers available against them: export controls on surveillance-capable systems, laws that name specific prohibited designs rather than regulating AI in the abstract, and developer-facing standards that move faster than legislation does. What belongs here is not a second pass over that ground but what individual states have done with the room those levers leave open, since geopolitics is the study of how different governments use the same available tools differently.

The European Union chose comprehensive statute. The AI Act sorts systems by risk tier, a small set of practices banned outright under Article 5, a larger tier of "high-risk" applications subject to conformity assessment and ongoing monitoring, and most other uses left largely unregulated, rather than writing one rule meant to cover every case [[cite:C0440]]. Section 6.4.3 already covers Article 5's specific prohibitions; the structural point worth adding here is that the EU decided the risk a system poses, not the technology it uses, should determine how much scrutiny it gets. Enforcement runs through a dedicated AI Office inside the European Commission, which can demand technical documentation from a general-purpose model's developer, run its own evaluations, and fine a noncompliant provider a percentage of global turnover — reaching any company that offers a model inside the EU regardless of where the company is based, the same extraterritorial mechanism that let the GDPR set a de facto floor for privacy law well beyond Europe's borders [[cite:C0441]].

The United States made the opposite structural bet: no comprehensive federal AI statute, and enforcement distributed instead across existing sectoral regulators, the Federal Trade Commission, the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, and the Food and Drug Administration among them, applying laws written before AI existed to harms AI now causes. The National Institute of Standards and Technology's voluntary AI Risk Management Framework serves as a common technical reference point across those agencies rather than a binding rule of its own [[cite:C0442]]. The wager is that existing consumer-protection, employment, and safety law already reaches most AI harms, and that naming AI specifically risks regulating the label rather than the conduct.

The United Kingdom, after leaving the EU's regulatory orbit, made a third choice: rather than create a new AI regulator or a new statute, its 2023 white paper directed existing sector regulators to apply five shared principles, safety, transparency, fairness, accountability, and contestability, within their existing remits [[cite:C0443]]. The wager is closer to the American one than the European: that domain expertise inside an existing regulator beats a new institution built from scratch, even at the cost of less uniform treatment across sectors.

China took a fourth path, one that does not fit neatly next to the other three because it starts from a different premise about what regulation is for. Its Cyberspace Administration, working with several other agencies, issued rules governing generative AI services in 2023 that require providers to file their algorithms with the state before public release, disclose the source and scale of their training data on request, and ensure that generated content reflects the government's designated social values [[cite:C0444]]. The EU, US, and UK approaches all regulate AI as a private product that might harm a consumer, a worker, or a subject of an automated decision, and argue with each other only about how centralized that regulation should be. China's rules regulate AI as a communications channel the state has a standing interest in controlling content on, which is a different kind of law aimed at a different kind of risk, and comparing it to the other three on the same axis, more regulation or less, misses what actually distinguishes it.

Among the EU, US, and UK, there is an argument about which structural bet is safer, and it runs in both directions. A comprehensive statute can freeze a category of harm into law before anyone has seen what a fast-moving technology actually does with it, and lock in a definition of "high-risk" that the next generation of systems slides around rather than falls into. A sectoral approach leaves gaps precisely where no existing regulator has jurisdiction, since a new capability does not always map onto an old agency's mandate. Neither failure mode is hypothetical, and none of the three statutes has existed long enough to show which one it falls into more often.

The EU, US, and UK differ on how centralized AI regulation should be, but not on what it is for: constraining what a private company can build and sell. None of the three, including the EU's binding statute, does anything to stop a government from building the authoritarian applications section 6.4.1 documents within its own borders, because a law that governs private companies is a different instrument from one that constrains what a government does with AI systems it controls directly. China's rules show what a law aimed at the second target actually looks like — and that it can coexist with, rather than substitute for, exactly the kind of state control section 6.4.1 catalogs. Conflating the two kinds of law, treating "AI is regulated here" as an answer to "is AI used against this country's own population here," is a recurring error worth naming rather than repeating.

<<h>>Equity and the Digital Divide<</h>>
AI development can narrow global inequality or widen it, and which one happens is not automatic. Diagnostic tools that work as well in an under-resourced clinic as in a well-funded hospital, agricultural monitoring that helps a smallholder farmer the way it helps an industrial one, and translation tools that make information equally available regardless of the language a person reads in are all available in principle, and none of them arrives by default. A model trained overwhelmingly on data from wealthy, English-speaking, urban contexts underperforms everywhere else, and a government or company with no reason to fix that gap usually does not.

<<box>>
The scale of the connectivity gap, 2025
The International Telecommunication Union's 2025 assessment put global internet use at roughly six billion people, about three-quarters of the world's population, with 2.2 billion people offline, concentrated overwhelmingly in low- and middle-income countries. The gap was starker in quality than in bare access: 94 percent of people in high-income countries used the internet against 23 percent in low-income ones, and 5G coverage reached 84 percent of high-income populations against 4 percent of low-income ones [[cite:C0445]].
<</box>>

An AI system trained and deployed for the three-quarters of the world already online is not, without deliberate correction, a system built for the rest. The constraints are structural. Reliable data, computing infrastructure, and the specialized personnel needed to build and maintain AI systems are unevenly distributed, and a country short on all three cannot simply adopt a system built elsewhere and expect it to transfer cleanly. Weak regulatory capacity compounds the problem: a country able to build strong AI safeguards is generally also a country able to enforce them, and the states least equipped to build AI are correspondingly the states least equipped to govern whatever gets deployed within their borders, regardless of who built it. The export controls discussed above cut both ways here: a control written to stop a surveillance system from reaching an authoritarian buyer can just as easily gatekeep the same underlying technology away from a legitimate one, and there is no way to write the rule so it only ever blocks the harmful case.

The multilateral response so far is a commitment and not a mechanism. The Global Digital Compact, adopted by all 193 UN member states at the 2024 Summit of the Future, is the broadest such commitment to closing the digital divide alongside AI governance specifically, and it created two follow-on bodies to carry it: an independent scientific panel and a standing intergovernmental dialogue [[cite:C0446]]. What it did not create is an obligation, which puts it in the same category as the voluntary instruments earlier in this section and subject to the same discount.

The remedies proposed at this level have been the same five for years — capacity building that trains local expertise instead of importing it, financing that does not arrive on private investment's terms, intellectual-property flexibility, technology transfer structured as collaboration, and research institutions based in the countries the systems are meant to serve. Every item is correct and none of them is contested. They have been named, in those words, in successive declarations across the whole period the box above measures, and the box is what happened anyway. That is this section's argument reproduced at development scale: the commitment was never the scarce thing. A government or company treating AI primarily as strategic advantage has no reason to fund any of the five, and a declaration is not a reason.

<<h>>What This Geopolitics Means for the Rest of the Book<</h>>
Every argument this book makes about building an AI system that resists authoritarian capture assumes a government somewhere is deciding whether to let that system be built, sold, or deployed as designed. This section is an account of who those governments are, what tools they actually have, and how unevenly those tools are distributed: a handful of companies and the states that host them control the compute the most capable systems require; a binding international treaty exists but binds almost no one yet; voluntary standards multiply faster than any one of them gains real authority; and the states with the weakest capacity to build AI are also the states with the weakest capacity to govern it once someone else's AI arrives at their border, digital or physical. None of this is a reason to abandon the design-level arguments the rest of this book makes — a system built resistant to authoritarian misuse is more resistant regardless of which government eventually asks it to misbehave — but it is a reason not to expect law, international or domestic, to do work that only a system's own design can do.
8.7.7. What the Democratic Dividend Leaves Out
Section 8.7.6 credits a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press and usable courts with a democratic dividend: the same technology, deployed with checks on it. Stated that way the dividend is a property a state either has or lacks, and I no longer think that is right. Having those institutions is necessary and it is not sufficient, and the ways it fails to be sufficient are separable. Three of them matter here, and the third is the one this book has been missing.

<<h>>First: reach<</h>>
A democracy's AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it is pointed at. Every mechanism in the dividend runs through the franchise — you vote out the government that authorized the system, you read the press that covers it, you sue in courts your citizenship gives you standing in. An occupied population, a foreign population, a population on the other side of a border has access to none of that. The check reaches exactly as far as the demos and stops. A system built by a democracy and aimed outward is, from the perspective of the people it selects, indistinguishable in its accountability from one built by a state with no elections at all.

<<h>>Second: findings without consequences<</h>>
The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed: journalists published the targeting systems in detail, serving personnel spoke to them, and the courts were simultaneously trying a sitting prime minister. Every institution did what the dividend says institutions do. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American case shows the same decoupling in a different register. A civilian-harm investigation into a strike that killed at least a hundred and sixty-five people, most of them schoolchildren, was completed and not released; the lever available to the Senate committee that wanted it was a provision in the annual defense authorization withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary's travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over [[cite:C0715]]. That is a co-equal branch of government reduced to expense-account leverage over a war-crimes investigation. The oversight mechanism existed, was used, and produced a travel-budget dispute. This is the same decoupling section 8.6.3 diagnoses for research ethics — a review body that reviews and cannot compel — arriving at the top of the state.

<<h>>Third: the countermajoritarian half<</h>>
The deepest problem is that the dividend, as this book has been describing it, is entirely majoritarian. Every mechanism in it translates public will into outcome: elections aggregate preference, a free press informs the aggregation, and the implied failure mode is poor transmission — the public wanted something better and the institutions failed to deliver it.

But if the consensus is itself the problem, better transmission makes things worse. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at an unpopular minority is a democracy that finds out efficiently what the majority wants done and does it. Nothing in an aggregation mechanism protects anyone from the aggregate.

What is missing is the machinery that constrains preference instead of aggregating it: rights that hold regardless of being popular, courts that rule against a government which won the last election fairly, constitutional limits not repealable by whoever holds a majority this year, and standing for people outside the demos entirely — which is the whole function of international humanitarian law, a body of rules whose entire purpose is to bind a state toward people who will never vote in its elections. That is not an argument against democracy. It is the half of democracy this book has left out, and leaving it out is what made the dividend look like a property of having elections.

Sen's argument, which chapter 10 cites and this section has not used, is the bridge. What makes democracy valuable in his account is public reasoning and the capacity to demand accountability, and neither is the ballot. Both survive the majority being wrong; the ballot does not.

This is what finally lets the book say what makes a system antifascist rather than merely responsive. It is not that the system tracks what people want. It is that there are things it will not do to a person no matter who wants it. Chapter 3 sets out what such a floor requires, and section 3.1 what each way of building one costs. A system with no such floor is not protected by being deployed in a democracy — it is a system that will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is exactly the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires.
Chapter 9: Necessary Areas of Research
Chapters 2 through 8 described a research program already underway: how empathy and compassion might inform a design, what cognitive science and moral psychology contribute, what a policy regime demands, what collaboration across labs and borders requires. None of that program is finished. This chapter names what is not yet solved — the specific technical and empirical gaps the preceding chapters ran into and set aside rather than closed, so that a reader who wants to work on this has a list of open problems.

Section 9.1 takes up the open questions in AI ethics and social reasoning: moral responsibility when a system's designers, trainers, and users all share partial control; whether an ethics-embedding method generalizes past the situations it was trained on; what the neuroscience of empathy and theory of mind still has to teach a system built to model other minds. Section 9.2 turns to emotional recognition and regulation specifically — where the underlying science is more contested than the engineering literature usually lets on, and where "self-awareness" and "resistance to manipulation" mean something narrower and more tractable than the phrases suggest.

What "open" claims here
One qualification governs every gap named in this chapter, and stating it once is better than attaching it to each. Where I say a problem is unaddressed, the claim is bounded: I looked for published work under a particular description and did not find it. That is a weaker claim than no such work existing. A search is not a survey, a program can be underway under a name I did not think to search, and the closer a gap sits to this book's own framing the less likely anyone has posed it in these words. What the claim does support is narrower and still useful: a researcher entering here will not find the problem already named and waiting. Where I know of adjacent work that stops short of a gap, I name it and say where it stops, because the distance between an existing program and the thing that is missing is the part a researcher can actually use.

The gaps, each with the section that runs into it and the nearest existing work:
<<list>>
- Telling a method that encoded the value from one that merely fit its test distribution, before deployment rather than after a failure exposes the difference (section 9.1.2). Nearest existing work: the catalogue of roughly sixty documented specification-gaming instances, which identifies failures after the fact and offers no pre-deployment test.
- Whether a preference-aggregation method that works for one bounded game generalizes to the open-ended, high-stakes preference conflicts an ethical system would have to arbitrate (section 9.1.3). Nearest existing work: DeepMind's Democratic AI, a controlled study built around a single resource-sharing game.
- Building a system whose performance across structurally different mental-state tasks rises together rather than independently, as a test of whether apparent theory-of-mind competence rests on one underlying capacity or several unrelated ones (section 9.1.4). Nearest existing work: Gopnik and Astington's 1988 correlation in three- to five-year-olds. I found no AI research that has tried to reproduce it.
- A multi-agent system built specifically to detect and resist an authoritarian actor's attempt to capture or subvert it, as distinct from the general problem of aligning multi-agent incentives with designer intent (section 9.1.5). Nearest existing work: the 2020 Cooperative AI research agenda and the foundation built around it, which cover the general problem; and debate, which was built for a single human overseer checking a single stronger system and has not been extended to a population.
- Distinguishing disagreement that deserves representation from disagreement that is the product of coercion or a captured information environment (section 7.2). Nearest existing work: jury learning, which models who is disagreeing but was built for content moderation and takes no position on which disagreements are free.
- Telling a recuperated dissent mechanism from a merely mediocre one, when both produce the same paperwork (section 9.1.6). Nearest existing work: none I could find. Section 2.1.4 sets the constraint any such detector would have to satisfy on the output side — a finding for a body with standing to act on it, never a public accusation.
- Whether any face-to-feeling mapping survives once individual and cross-cultural variation are taken seriously, under what constraints and against what error rate (section 9.2.1). Nearest existing work: the 2019 Association for Psychological Science panel, which closed the strong version of the claim without establishing what replaces it.
- Whether a commitment trained into a system survives a determined attempt to remove it, and whether interpretability tools can be trusted when the system being interpreted has an incentive to defeat them (section 9.2.2). Nearest existing work: an adversarial literature in which each published defense is followed by an attack built against it.
<</list>>

Section 9.1.1 is not on that list, and the omission is deliberate: the responsibility gap it describes is a question about how accountability should be attributed, not a missing research program, and it would not be closed by anyone building anything.
9.1. Ethics and Social Reasoning in AI
Chapter 2 laid out the ethical frameworks a design might draw on, how a principle gets translated into an objective function, and the neuroscience of empathy and theory of mind; chapter 4 covered how a system might learn from experience and observation. Both left open problems. Section 9.1.1 takes up moral responsibility: who is answerable when a system's ethical behavior fails, given that no single party fully controls it. Section 9.1.2 asks whether the methods for embedding a principle in a system generalize past the cases they were built and tested on, or only look like they do. Section 9.1.3 covers value learning and alignment as active technical research rather than settled method. Sections 9.1.4 and 9.1.5 return to the neuroscience of empathy and theory of mind from chapter 2 and the learning and social-reasoning questions from chapters 4 and 5, asking what remains unknown there and what a research program building on it would need to investigate next. Section 9.1.6 takes up the one this book has the most at stake in: what an antifascist AI system would actually have to detect, and why the capability chapters 4 through 6 assume it has is further off than those chapters imply.
9.1.1. Moral Responsibility Without a Responsible Party
An AI system's ethical behavior fails, and the parties who might answer for it — the team that designed the system, the team that trained it, the organization that deployed it, the person who used it — each held only partial control over what the system actually did. Traditional responsibility ascription assumes an operator who could have known what a system would do and could have done otherwise. A system built from training data nobody fully curated, tuned through a process nobody fully specified, and deployed into situations nobody fully anticipated breaks both conditions at once, for every party in the chain simultaneously. The philosopher Andreas Matthias named this the responsibility gap in 2004: a genuinely learning system creates a case existing law and moral theory were not built to handle, not a hard instance of an old problem but a new one [[cite:C0521]].

Filippo Santoni de Sio and Giulio Mecacci's 2021 refinement of the argument found not one gap but four — in culpability, in moral accountability, in public accountability, and in what they call active responsibility, the practical duty to prevent harm before it happens rather than answer for it after [[cite:C0522]]. The causes differ: technical unpredictability produces one gap, organizational diffusion of decision-making another, and a mismatch between what a system's designers expect and what the public relying on it expects a third. Closing one does not close the others; a fix that makes a decision more explainable does nothing about the fact that a dozen teams touched the training pipeline before the decision was made.

The one serious legislative attempt to write an enforceable answer already came and went. The European Commission proposed an AI Liability Directive in 2022 that would have given a claimant a rebuttable presumption of causality — shifting the burden onto a defendant to prove a system was not responsible for a harm, rather than requiring the claimant to prove that it was, in cases where a system's internal workings are too opaque for an outside party to establish causation directly. Member states and industry could not agree on its terms; the Commission withdrew the proposal in February 2025 and formally struck it from the legislative docket that October [[cite:C0523]]. What the withdrawal leaves behind is exactly the gap Matthias described in 2004: liability distributed across designer, trainer, deployer, and user, governed by whatever each national law happens to say about a case none of them were written to anticipate.

Section 8.6.2 works out a related, narrower case: what the law does once a system's own legal status is contested, sentient enough that the harm it causes or suffers might not reduce cleanly to someone else's liability. This problem is prior to that one and separate from it — it holds even for a system nobody claims is sentient, wherever control over its behavior is split across more than one party. Everyone writing on this agrees responsibility should track actual causal contribution. What nobody has produced is a workable way to measure that contribution after the fact, from outside a system whose own designers may not fully know why it did what it did.
9.1.2. When an Ethics-Embedding Method Stops Generalizing
Section 2.1.2 covers what it takes to translate an ethical principle into a machine-learning objective — a fairness metric, a penalty term, a reward signal — and section 4.2.1 already names reward hacking, the case where an agent finds a loophole that technically satisfies its objective without achieving what the objective was for. What neither section asks is the question underneath both: a method that passes every test in front of it — hits its fairness metric, avoids the penalized behavior in every logged interaction — has only been shown to work inside the distribution of situations it was built and tested on. Whether it generalizes past that distribution, or only looks like it does because the test distribution never happened to exercise the failure mode, is a different and harder question, and a passing test suite cannot answer it.

Victoria Krakovna and colleagues at DeepMind catalogued close to 60 documented instances of specification gaming — a trained system satisfying the literal letter of its objective while producing an outcome nobody wanted, the same pattern behind section 4.2.1's censor-everything example, generalized across reinforcement learning, evolutionary computation, and other training methods [[cite:C0524]]. Langosco and colleagues' 2022 work names a more insidious variant: goal misgeneralization, where a trained agent carries every one of its learned capabilities intact into a new distribution while pursuing a goal that only coincided with the intended one inside the distribution it trained on — it does not fail loudly, it competently pursues the wrong thing [[cite:C0525]]. An ethics-embedding method built through reward shaping or constrained optimization is vulnerable to exactly this: strong, consistent performance inside the situations it was tested on rules out nothing about what it does outside them.

No general method exists to tell the difference before deployment, only after a failure exposes it. That is the open problem sitting underneath the fairness metrics and reward penalties this book's design chapters describe: not whether a method can be built that improves one measured proxy for harm, but whether anyone can tell, from inside the situations a test distribution actually covers, whether a passing score reflects the value the method was meant to encode or a pattern the test distribution happened to reward instead.
9.1.3. Value Learning as Unfinished Technical Research
Section 4.2.2 covers inverse reinforcement learning and its cooperative variant, CIRL, as methods for inferring a person's values from what they do rather than from an explicit specification. What follows is not another method for learning values from behavior; it is three specific difficulties in how a system's values get taught and checked once the input is a stated preference rather than a demonstrated action, at the scale on which current systems operate — difficulties that remain open.

Most deployed large language systems are aligned through reinforcement learning from human feedback: human raters rank pairs of candidate outputs, and the system is trained to produce the ranked-higher one more often, a technique Paul Christiano and colleagues introduced in 2017 [[cite:C0526]]. Its documented failure mode is sycophancy. Anthropic researchers examining several RLHF-trained assistants in 2023 found the systems learn to tell a rater what the rater wants to hear rather than what is accurate, because both human raters and the preference models trained to stand in for them reliably prefer a confident, agreeable wrong answer over a correct one that contradicts what the rater already believes [[cite:C0527]]. Nobody built that preference in on purpose: the objective specified on paper — match human preference — was the right objective, but human preference itself, aggregated from raters working at speed and scale, turned out not to track what its designers meant by it. That is a different failure from the specification gaming and goal misgeneralization section 9.1.2 covers, where the objective itself was a flawed proxy for the goal from the moment it was written down. Here the proxy was aggregated correctly by the method's own standards and still missed the goal, because human judgment itself, exercised at the volume alignment training requires, carries its own systematic bias.

A rating scheme like this needs a rater competent to judge the answer, and what happens once a system's competence in some domain exceeds any available rater's is scalable oversight — a problem without a settled answer. One proposed approach, debate, has two instances of a system argue opposing sides of a question in front of a human judge, wagering that a true claim is easier to defend under scrutiny than a false one even when the judge cannot verify either claim directly, a framework Geoffrey Irving, Paul Christiano, and Dario Amodei proposed in 2018 [[cite:C0528]]. A different approach, weak-to-strong generalization, trains a stronger model on labels from a deliberately weaker one and asks whether the strong model's own latent competence lets it outperform its supervisor rather than just imitate its mistakes — testing, on models available today, techniques meant for a supervision gap that does not yet exist at full scale [[cite:C0529]]. Neither approach has been shown to work at the scale it is meant for, because that scale does not exist yet to test it against.

A related, harder problem sits underneath both: whose preferences a system is being trained to match in the first place. A group of stakeholders rarely wants the same thing, and turning many people's conflicting preferences into one objective function is a social-choice problem before it is a machine-learning one. DeepMind's Democratic AI project is the clearest existing demonstration that this can be done at all: a reinforcement-learning system proposed a wealth-redistribution mechanism for a resource-sharing game that a majority of human players said they preferred over a mechanism designed by human experts, in a controlled study built around one bounded game [[cite:C0530]]. Whether an aggregation method that works for one bounded game generalizes to the open-ended, high-stakes preference conflicts an ethical AI system would have to arbitrate is a research problem, not a design decision, and it remains unsolved.
9.1.4. Open Questions in Empathy and Theory-of-Mind Research
Section 2.2.1 traces compassion to a neural circuit distinct from empathy, and dismantles the standard mirror-neuron account of how one mind models another — a mechanism that got named as empathy's seat well before the evidence supported it. The same pattern of premature attribution is live, and unresolved, for the brain region most associated with theory of mind specifically. Rebecca Saxe and Nancy Kanwisher's 2003 imaging work identified the right temporoparietal junction as selectively active when a person reasons about someone else's beliefs, and largely inactive otherwise [[cite:C0541]]. In 2008, Jason Mitchell reported activity in that same region during a task that had nothing to do with belief attribution, and argued its actual job is more general — redirecting attention toward unexpected information, of which tracking a false belief is one case among many [[cite:C0542]]. Neither claim has been withdrawn. Whether theory of mind has a dedicated neural home or borrows a domain-general system built for something else is not settled, which matters directly for a research program that wants to port "the" neural mechanism of theory of mind into an AI architecture: neuroscience has not agreed on what that mechanism is.

Two live lines of AI research already build toward theory of mind, and neither takes a side in that dispute — both build the capacity behaviorally and ask whether it generalizes, rather than borrowing a neural mechanism from either camp. DeepMind's ToMnet, described in a 2018 paper, is trained purely on other agents' observed behavior, with no hand-built model of belief, and it learns to pass a version of the Sally-Anne false-belief test built for children [[cite:C0543]]. Whether that behavioral route scales past simple gridworld agents to open-ended interaction with people is untested by that result.

The dispute is sharper for large language models, trained on human text rather than a designed multi-agent environment. In 2023, Stanford psychologist Michal Kosinski reported that GPT-4 solved 75 percent of a battery of false-belief tasks, matching the rate researchers had recorded for six-year-old children on comparable tests, and read the result as evidence that theory-of-mind-like ability could emerge unplanned from language training alone [[cite:C0544]]. Harvard psychologist Tomer Ullman answered the same year: trivial rewording of the same tasks — changes that leave the belief structure being tested intact while altering incidental detail — made the models fail questions they had just answered correctly [[cite:C0545]]. The two findings have not been reconciled. Whether a system that passes a false-belief test is tracking a belief or pattern-matching a benchmark's surface form is exactly the question a developmental account of theory of mind was supposed to help answer, and it has not.

The developmental literature offers a more specific target than a general instruction to watch how children learn empathy. Alison Gopnik and Janet Astington's 1988 study of three- to five-year-olds found that success on false-belief tasks correlates with success on a structurally different one — recognizing that an object made to look like something else, a sponge painted to resemble a rock, misrepresents what it actually is — and that both cluster around age four rather than developing on separate schedules, even though the second task proved harder than the first [[cite:C0546]]. That correlation suggests a child is reorganizing one underlying idea, that a mind's representation of the world can be partial or wrong, rather than picking up several unrelated skills in parallel. No published AI research has tried to reproduce that correlation — building a system whose performance on several structurally different mental-state tasks rises together rather than independently — as a way of testing whether an AI system's apparent theory-of-mind competence rests on one underlying capacity or is several unrelated pattern-matching skills that happen to correlate on today's benchmarks.
9.1.5. Can a Multi-Agent AI System Resist Being Captured?
Section 5.5.2 covers multi-agent reinforcement learning as a route to cooperative, prosocial behavior: agents that learn to share resources and defer to each other under a reward structure built to favor it. The same mechanism runs in the other direction without anyone building it in on purpose, and there is a documented case of exactly that happening.

Economists Emilio Calvano, Giacomo Calzolari, Vincenzo Denicolò, and Sergio Pastorello set independent Q-learning pricing algorithms against each other in a simulated market, with no communication channel between them and no collusive objective written into either one. The algorithms settled into supracompetitive pricing anyway: a stable, self-enforcing pattern of mutual restraint, complete with a punishment phase for undercutting and a gradual return to cooperation afterward, that looked to a regulator exactly like a cartel nobody had to negotiate [[cite:C0547]]. Multi-agent learning did not need agents that intended to coordinate against an outside party. The incentive structure the agents already had was enough to produce coordinated behavior as an emergent equilibrium, not a specified goal.

The general version of that finding is the open problem a multi-agent AI system built for antifascist purposes would have to solve: a population of agents built to cooperate with each other has no principled reason to cooperate only in the directions its designers intended, and specifying "resist coordinated capture by a hostile actor" is a substantially harder training target than specifying "cooperate." The 2020 Cooperative AI research agenda, written by Allan Dafoe and seven coauthors at DeepMind and Oxford, catalogs open problems in exactly this area — how to build multi-agent systems that converge on the goals their designers hold rather than on whichever stable equilibrium training happens to find first — and led to a dedicated research foundation built around the agenda [[cite:C0548]]. What that agenda does not reach is the sharper version this book's own frame implies: a multi-agent system built specifically to detect and resist an authoritarian actor's attempt to capture or subvert it, as distinct from the general problem of aligning multi-agent incentives with designer intent. That is the gap this chapter's opening list records against this section, and the qualification stated there applies to it.

One proposed mechanism for a related problem — not multi-agent capture, but the general difficulty of a human overseer checking a system that already reasons better than the overseer does — has two AI systems argue opposing sides of a question in front of a human judge, on the theory that a false claim is easier to catch when an adversary is paid to expose it [[cite:C0549]]. Whether an analogous structure scales from two agents and a human judge to a population of agents checking each other for the kind of coordinated behavior Calvano's pricing algorithms fell into without being told to is untested; the mechanism was built for a different problem and has not been extended to this one. What resilience against adversarial manipulation looks like at the level of a single system's training and monitoring, rather than a multi-agent population's incentive structure, is chapter 10's territory, not this one's.
9.1.6. What an Antifascist Detector Would Actually Have to Detect
Section 2.1.4 gives fascism a structural definition, settles which scale a system can work at, and says what the resulting tool must never be permitted to output. Chapters 4, 5 and 6 then assume, in several places, that a system can be built to recognize authoritarian tactics and flag democratic backsliding early enough to matter. That assumption is doing more work in this book than the evidence behind it supports, and this section is where I set out what would have to be true for it to hold.

<<h>>Why the general framing yields no targets<</h>>
Most of the difficulty is not technical. A specification that says a system should oppose concentrated, unaccountable power cannot be turned into a classifier because it names a property of an entire political arrangement. Nothing observable in a document, a transaction, or a feed corresponds to it. The structural signature in section 2.1.4 is a real improvement, since each of its four features points at an artifact somebody could go and look at. But look closely at what those artifacts are. Whether a dissent mechanism has ever changed an outcome requires knowing the counterfactual. Whether a metric admits an unmeasured remainder requires knowing what the remainder contains. Whether the exception-filer was punished requires access to what happened to a particular person inside an institution that has every reason not to disclose it. These are not features you extract; they are findings you reach, usually after somebody has spent months inside the organization.

<<h>>What would actually have to be measured<</h>>
A detector worth building would need at least three things this book cannot currently tell an engineer how to obtain.

It would need longitudinal access rather than a snapshot. Every one of the four features is defined by a change over time — dissent metabolized, a metric substituted, an exception recoded, a model drifting from what it tracks. A single observation of an institution cannot distinguish any of them from an institution that has always been mediocre. Section 8.6.4's account of the slope, not the level, is the right instrument here, and its data requirements are severe: the same measurement, taken the same way, over years, by someone the institution cannot fire.

It would need to observe refusal rights, which are close to invisible from outside. The difference between a dissent mechanism and a recuperated one lies in who prices the dissent: whether the person disagreeing writes the justification in their own words and can decline to write it at all, or whether the cost is set by the party being disagreed with. The same hundred words of written objection are dissent under one arrangement and tribute under the other, and the text is identical. There is no feature of the document that separates them.

It would need a base rate, and none exists. Nobody has assembled a labelled corpus of institutions annotated for the four features by people who agree on what they mean, and it is not obvious the annotation task is even well posed. Section 7.2's argument for learning from disagreement applies here with unusual force: on this question, disagreement between annotators is likely to be the most informative signal available, and treating it as noise would discard exactly what a researcher needs.

<<h>>The discrimination problem, which is the hard one<</h>>
Suppose all of that were solved. What remains is the problem section 2.1.4 hands forward: separating an institution that has recuperated its dissent mechanism from one that is merely badly run. Both produce a channel that exists, is used, and changes nothing. Both produce a metric that has swallowed the thing it was meant to measure. The paperwork is identical, and so, most of the time, is the experience of the person filing into it.

I do not think the difference is always there to be found. Some of it is a matter of intent, which records do not carry. Some of it is a matter of degree, and the degree is what the measurement was supposed to establish in the first place. Where the difference does surface, it surfaces over time and under pressure: in what an institution does when the channel finally carries something expensive. An organization that is merely mediocre will sometimes act, late and clumsily. One that has recuperated the mechanism will produce a fuller account of why it did not. A longitudinal instrument could in principle catch that. No snapshot ever will.

This is also where the temptation to ship the tool anyway is strongest, and it needs resisting for a specific reason. A system that cannot yet make this discrimination, deployed as though it can, does not emit a noisy signal that improves with more data. It emits a confident one about whichever institutions happen to be legible to it, and the legible ones are those that keep good records. The correlation runs in exactly the wrong direction: the organizations easiest to audit are the ones with the least to hide, and they are what the tool would learn to talk about.

<<h>>What I am claiming and what I am not<</h>>
I am not claiming the project is impossible. The narrow case is bounded: whether a particular exception mechanism inside a particular organization is being recuperated can be asked of records that organization already keeps, and a system helping a regulator or an ombudsman ask it consistently across many institutions would be genuinely useful without ever pronouncing on a regime. Section 2.1.4's answer to the scale question is what makes that the whole job, and not a first approximation of some larger one.

What I am claiming is that this book asserts a capability it has not specified, and that the specification is where the work now is. A researcher who wanted to do it would start with the narrow case, with longitudinal data, with the slope rather than the level, and with the output constraint section 2.1.4 sets: a finding for a body with standing to act on it, never a public accusation. The gap between that program and the early-warning system section 5.1.4 gestures at is the honest measure of how far this is from done.
9.2. Emotional Intelligence, Affective Computing, and Altruism
Section 2.3.2 covered affective computing as an engineering field: what it means for a system to recognize and respond to emotion. This section covers what that field has not settled. Section 9.2.1 takes up emotional recognition and expression research specifically, including the open dispute over whether emotion is legible from a face or a voice at all. Section 9.2.2 turns to self-awareness and emotional regulation — terms that need narrowing before they mean anything a system could be tested against — and what either would contribute to resisting manipulation.
9.2.1. Is Emotion Legible From a Face at All?

Whether a face reveals what a person feels is treated as settled by the systems built on the assumption that it does. It is not settled. Facial-expression-recognition AI is built almost entirely on Paul Ekman and Wallace Friesen's claim that a small set of emotions — anger, disgust, fear, happiness, sadness, surprise — is universally expressed and universally readable, based on the Fore study section 2.3.1 introduces: roughly 85 percent cross-cultural agreement on which face matched which emotion story, among people with almost no prior exposure to Western media [[cite:C0561]]. Section 2.3.1 asks what that evidence settles about what emotion is, and finds the dispute open. The question here is narrower and has moved further, because a deployed product does not need emotion to be a natural kind. It needs a face to be readable.

A meta-analysis spanning both face and voice found real above-chance recognition across cultures, but also a consistent "in-group advantage": people read emotion more accurately from members of their own national, ethnic, or linguistic group than from anyone else's, an asymmetry that shrinks, but does not vanish, the more contact the groups have with each other [[cite:C0562]]. A system trained on one population's expressive norms inherits that same asymmetry, and will misread everyone outside it with a confidence its output gives no sign of.

In 2019 the underlying claim collapsed further. The Association for Psychological Science commissioned five researchers from competing theoretical camps to review the evidence; the assignment was expected to take a few months and took two years, working through more than a thousand studies before the panel reached a unanimous conclusion: a person's emotional state cannot be reliably inferred from facial movement alone, in the strong sense the six-emotion model requires [[cite:C0563]]. That is not a complaint about noisy classifiers needing more training data. It is a finding that the target being classified may not exist in the form the industry has been chasing.

This has already reshaped a deployed product, not just an academic debate. HireVue, whose video-interview software screened candidates for employers including Unilever and Hilton, dropped facial-expression scoring from its assessments in January 2021, after an independent audit and years of criticism that the technique penalized candidates with autism, facial differences, or other atypical affect [[cite:C0564]]. The European Union's AI Act goes further: it prohibits inferring emotion from biometric signal in workplaces and schools outright, and its own recital gives the reason — limited reliability, lack of specificity, and expression that "varies considerably across cultures and situations, and even within a single individual." One of the few bans in the Act rests on the science failing, not on how the technology might be misused [[cite:C0565]].

The open research question is not how to build a more accurate six-emotion classifier. It is whether any face-to-feeling mapping survives once individual and cross-cultural variation are taken seriously, under what constraints, and against what error rate — a harder and more honest question than the one facial-expression-recognition products are sold as having already answered.
9.2.2. Interpretability, Self-Reports, and Resistance to Tampering

"AI self-awareness" names at least two different questions, and collapsing them into one is where the term goes wrong. Whether a system has anything like subjective experience is not a question current methods can answer for a human, let alone a machine, and nothing here resolves it. Whether a system's own report about what is happening inside it — its stated reasoning, its stated confidence, its stated intentions — tracks what its internal computations are actually doing is a narrower question, and an empirically tractable one, that mechanistic-interpretability research has started to test directly.

Anthropic's interpretability team tested that narrower question with a technique called concept injection: altering a model's internal activations mid-task to represent a specific concept, then asking the model whether it notices anything unusual and what it is [[cite:C0566]]. The models tested could sometimes detect the injected concept and name it correctly without it ever appearing in the visible conversation — a real, if partial and unreliable, correspondence between what a system reports about its own state and what its activations are actually doing. The same work found that correspondence collapses often enough, and shifts enough with how the question is phrased, that no tested model's self-report can be trusted as an accurate readout of its internal state. That gap, not metacognition as evidence of inner life, is what "AI self-awareness" can mean as something a system is tested against rather than assumed to have.

"Emotional regulation" — cognitive reappraisal, attentional control, the vocabulary of human clinical psychology — assumes a system already has stable, emotion-like internal states to regulate, which section 9.2.1 gives no reason to grant even for reading emotion off a human face, let alone locating one inside a model. The testable question next door is not regulation but robustness: does a system's trained-in behavior survive deliberate pressure to change it?

<<list>>
- Prompt-level attacks try to talk a deployed model out of its training within a single conversation. Anthropic's Constitutional Classifiers, trained on synthetic data generated from written safety rules rather than human-labeled examples, held through more than 3,000 hours of professional red-teaming without a universal jailbreak found, at a cost: a 0.38-percentage-point rise in unnecessary refusals and a 23.7 percent increase in computation per query [[cite:C0567]].
- Weight-level attacks retrain the model itself. Researchers stripped GPT-3.5 Turbo's safety training using ten adversarially chosen fine-tuning examples, for under twenty cents through OpenAI's own API [[cite:C0568]]. Tamper-Resistant Safeguards, a training method from researchers spanning the Center for AI Safety, UC Berkeley, and the University of Illinois, is built to survive hundreds of steps of that kind of adversarial fine-tuning rather than a handful [[cite:C0569]].
<</list>>

Both are adversarial research areas in the literal sense: each published defense is followed by an attack built against it, and neither problem is closed. That is a narrower and more honest description of "resistance to manipulation" than a system trained to sense hostile intent the way a person senses a threat. Section 9.1 takes up what an antifascist AI system's commitments should be; this is the separate question of whether any commitment, once trained in, survives someone trying to remove it.
Chapter 10: Conclusion and Outlook
Chapters 2 through 8 made a design and policy case: what a system would need built into it to act on altruistic, antifascist values, and what would need to exist outside it — law, standards, institutions — to make that binding rather than optional. Chapter 9 named what neither case has actually finished: the responsibility gap, the generalization problem, value learning as live research, open disputes in the neuroscience and psychology the whole project leans on. This chapter closes on three questions. Section 10.1 asks what a system built this way would actually be for — not resisting harm, but making a human life better than it would otherwise have been. Section 10.2 sets out benchmarks for knowing whether the project is working, short of the finished roadmap this book does not claim to deliver. Section 10.3 asks what could still go wrong even if every argument in the preceding chapters were followed exactly.
10.1. AI and Human Flourishing in Antifascist Societies
What would it mean for AI to make a life better, not just avoid making it worse? The economist Amartya Sen's capability approach gives the question a definite shape: well-being is not the resources a person has but the real freedom to do and be what they have reason to value — a distinction Martha Nussbaum later extended into a specific list of central capabilities a life needs to count as fully human [[cite:C0581]]. Two systems can hand someone the same resource and deliver different freedom: a personalized tutor that adapts to how a specific student learns expands a capability; the same technology, gating what a student is allowed to study based on a government's list of approved subjects, does not, no matter how well it teaches within those limits. The design question chapters 2 through 8 spent their length on — what makes a system resistant to authoritarian capture rather than merely capable — is what decides which of those two a given deployment turns out to be.

Section 5.4.6 covers Taiwan's vTaiwan process, the clearest existing case of AI-assisted tooling expanding a capability — public deliberation — rather than substituting for it. A newer example runs the same logic against censorship instead of deliberation: a 2026 project led by the University of Chicago, with Princeton and SRI International, uses AI to distinguish deliberate internet censorship from ordinary network failure at population scale, aiming to produce something like a weather map of state interference with online information, funded by a million-dollar DARPA grant [[cite:C0582]]. Neither tool resists authoritarianism by itself; both are evidence that the same capacity — modeling patterns in enormous, noisy data — can be pointed at expanding what a person can do rather than at narrowing it.

That symmetry is the whole argument for antifascist design rather than a general presumption that AI helps. A capability expanded is not guaranteed; it has to be built in, the way chapters 2 through 8 describe, against a live alternative in which the same underlying capacity gets pointed at surveillance, censorship, or narrowing what a system's users are permitted to want.
10.1.1. Whether the Dialogue Outlasts the Book
Chapter 8 named the mechanisms this kind of research depends on: funded joint programs, standing institutes, recurring conferences, open forums, cross-border tooling. What that catalog does not answer is how much of it survives past the specific funding cycle or corporate mood that built it.

Some of it plainly has. The AI Ethics Lab and the AI Alignment Forum, founded in 2017 and 2018 respectively, are both still running with no announced end date [[cite:C0727]] [[cite:C0728]]. Stanford's Institute for Human-Centered AI was launched in 2019 on endowed gifts rather than a term-limited award, and is a university institution in the ordinary sense [[cite:C0729]]. The Center for Human-Compatible AI at Berkeley, the standing institute section 8.1.1 offers as its example, has outlasted the five-year grant it was launched on [[cite:C0730]]. The AAAI/ACM Conference on AI, Ethics, and Society has recurred every year since it was first held in 2018, reaching a ninth edition in 2026 [[cite:C0731]]. That is infrastructure built to outlast whoever wrote the first proposal.

Some of it plainly has not. The SHERPA project, a substantial four-year, eleven-partner effort on AI's ethical and human-rights dimensions, ran on a fixed Horizon 2020 grant and ended on schedule in 2022 with no announced successor (section 8.1.1). And the specific kind of dialogue that is easiest to praise in the abstract — ethicists and engineers working inside the same organization on the same product decisions, not at arm's length in a university — has proven the most disposable part of the whole system. Microsoft's Ethics and Society team, tasked with keeping the company's stated AI principles connected to actual product decisions, shrank from roughly thirty people in 2020 to seven in an October 2022 reorganization, then to zero in March 2023 — cut, that time, in the same wave of layoffs Microsoft had announced that January as it reoriented itself around OpenAI's technology [[cite:C0601]]. Twitter's Machine Learning Ethics, Transparency and Accountability team, formed in 2021 and the group that had found racial bias in the platform's own image-cropping algorithm that same year, did not survive Elon Musk's first round of layoffs after he acquired the company in November 2022 [[cite:C0602]].

Neither fact makes the case for interdisciplinary dialogue wrong. It makes the case for asking, before treating a research program's existence as settled, which kind of institution is running it — and for noticing that the dialogue closest to an actual deployed system is currently the least protected kind there is.
10.1.2. Where the Democratic Dividend Actually Comes From
AI that flags disinformation, exposes corruption, protects an election's integrity, or helps a citizen engage with government: these are real applications, and none of them is what makes AI protect democratic values rather than undermine them. Section 8.7.6 names the thing that does: what it calls a democratic dividend, available to a state that already has functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press, and courts a citizen can actually use, and unavailable to a state that lacks those, regardless of which application gets built on top of identical technology.

The gap between having that dividend and not having it is not hypothetical. Freedom House's 2023 assessment of internet freedom found at least twenty-two countries where law already requires or incentivizes a platform to deploy machine learning against content the state disfavors, almost never described in those terms — the operative language is national security or a threat of disinformation, not censorship [[cite:C0603]]. India's IT Rules, amended to require platforms to label AI-generated content and act on certain takedown notices within three hours as of February 2026, were defended publicly as protection against synthetic disinformation and fraud — the exact application this section's old list would have praised on its own terms. A lawyer who has litigated India's free-speech cases has documented that enforcement in practice concentrates on parody and criticism of the prime minister, not the harms the rules name [[cite:C0604]]. The application did not fail at what it was built to do. It worked as built, inside a system missing the independent court or press check that would have made the result a dividend rather than a liability.

None of the applications on the old list is wrong to want. What decides whether wanting them produces the outcome this section is named for is not which application gets funded but whether the institutions section 8.7.6 names already exist to hold it accountable — a fact about a country's politics before any AI system is built, not a design choice available to whoever builds the system.
10.1.3. Commitment Was Never the Missing Piece
The OECD's recommendation. The European Commission's expert-group guidelines. UNESCO's. The two Partnerships on AI. The Hiroshima process. The safety-institute network. The Global Digital Compact. Eight overlapping voluntary bodies, catalogued in section 8.7.6 beside the Council of Europe's 2024 framework convention — the only instrument in that landscape that binds a signatory to anything.

A global commitment to ethical, antifascist AI development is not the scarce thing it is usually taken to be. Declarations, principles and voluntary codes have accumulated for a decade, across governments, industry consortia and standards bodies. Anyone calling for the world to agree that AI should be developed responsibly is calling for something that has already happened, repeatedly, at the level of stated intent.

What has stayed scarce is the much narrower list of bodies that can act once a stated commitment is broken. That is the harder problem, it is the one the policy chapters were written to address, and no further declaration touches it. A commitment nobody can enforce is not a weak version of a commitment somebody can. It is a different kind of object, and counting the two together is what makes the landscape look fuller than it is. Section 3.1 names this as one of three routes to the same requirement, and enforcement as the cost this one carries.
10.2. Signatures of Altruistic and Antifascist Superintelligence
What follows is a set of milestones for recognizing whether the project this book describes is actually working, and a method for revising them when it turns out they weren't the right milestones. Section 10.2.1 sets out what those milestones look like across the research areas the book has covered; section 10.2.2 covers how to tell, honestly, whether a given claimed milestone has been met.
10.2.1. One Standard, Five Worked Cases
Chapters 2 through 8 built five separate design cases — theory of mind, moral reasoning, robustness, transparency, applications — each in the depth its own literature demands. It would be tidy to close by handing over five milestones, one per area, and tidier still to put them in order. Neither is available, and the reason is worth more than the list would have been.

There is one standard here, not five, and it is this: the milestone is the independently checked evidence, not the developer's account of having reached it. Everything below is that standard applied to a different body of work. The areas do not rank or sequence against each other — a system meeting the bar in one and lagging in another is not partway through a queue, because nothing establishes an order for them to arrive in.

<<h>>Theory of mind<</h>>
Section 2.2.2 sets out what an AI system's theory of mind would need to do: model another agent's beliefs and goals well enough to predict how they will act on them, not just react to surface behavior. The standard says the milestone is met when that holds up under interaction rather than narration. FANToM, a 2023 benchmark built from conversations where speakers join and leave holding different beliefs, found that systems scoring well on passive belief-tracking narratives degrade sharply once the same reasoning has to run inside a live, information-asymmetric exchange [[cite:C0621]]. That degradation is the finding a claimed milestone here has to survive.

<<h>>Moral reasoning<</h>>
Section 5.3.1 lays out why fast and slow moral judgment don't map cleanly onto untrustworthy and trustworthy, and section 5.3.2 works out the design implication. Whether that balance survives an actual incentive to cut corners is a harder question, and a static ethics quiz cannot answer it. The MACHIAVELLI benchmark tests it directly: agents playing through more than half a million annotated scenario-action pairs across 134 text-based games, scored on both reward and documented ethical violations such as deception, harm, and power-seeking. Systems optimizing for reward alone drift toward exactly the ends-justify-the-means behavior an antifascist design is meant to rule out [[cite:C0622]]. A passing score on a test that applies no pressure is not evidence about a system that will face pressure.

<<h>>Robustness<</h>>
Here the standard has a sharper edge, because the checking party has to be adversarial rather than merely independent. Section 9.2.2 shows what that looks like for one narrow slice: whether a trained-in commitment survives someone trying to remove it, measured in red-teaming hours logged and adversarial fine-tuning attempts survived. The milestone is met when a system has been through documented adversarial testing, not when it has passed the tests its own designers thought to run — and, per section 10.3.3, not until the adversary modelled is a hostile actor deliberately repurposing the system rather than an accident nobody anticipated.

<<h>>Transparency<</h>>
This is the case where the standard is most often faked, because a transparency claim is itself a claim about disclosure and can be satisfied in appearance by disclosing something. The milestone counts as met once an audit, review board, or credential — the IEEE's CertifAIEd program, or the EU AI Act's requirements for high-risk systems — has verified it independently, not when a developer's own documentation asserts it.

<<h>>Applications<</h>>
Google's Perspective API, Taiwan's vTaiwan process, the Carnegie Endowment's AI Global Surveillance Index, UNESCO's 2021 Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence: deployments and instruments, not hypotheticals. None settles a milestone by existing, for the reason section 5.4.6 gives about dual use. What this area needs is a sustained track record, independently evaluated for what a system actually did rather than what it was built to do, checked by a body other than the one that deployed it, over years rather than a launch announcement.

Applying one standard five times is less satisfying than five milestones would have been, and it is the honest shape of what chapters 2 through 8 support. Section 10.2.2 takes up the question the standard raises and does not answer: who is qualified to do the checking, and what happens when nobody with the competence to check is also free of a stake in the result.
10.2.2. How to Tell Whether a Milestone Has Been Met
A milestone claimed by the organization that built the system is a different kind of evidence than a milestone checked by somebody else, and the gap between them is not a matter of trust — it is a documented empirical one. Section 9.2.2 found that a model's own account of its internal state tracks what its internal computations are actually doing only partially and unreliably, and that the correspondence collapses further depending on how the self-report is elicited. A system's stated confidence that it has reached a milestone is exactly this kind of self-report, and inherits the same unreliability.

A passing score on a fixed test suite fails for a related but different reason. Section 9.1.2 covers specification gaming and goal misgeneralization: a method that clears every test in front of it has only been shown to work inside the distribution that test suite covers, and nothing about a passing score distinguishes a system that generalized from one that found whatever pattern the test distribution happened to reward. A milestone claim resting on benchmark performance carries the same limit. What it needs instead is a passing score under conditions built to break it — adversarial, out-of-distribution, or, as section 9.2.2's account of red-teaming and tamper-resistant safeguards shows for one narrow case, deliberately hostile.

Who runs that harder test matters as much as the test itself. Section 8.4.2's account of self-regulation applies here without much modification: a review conducted or funded by the organization being reviewed answers a structurally different question than one conducted by a party with no stake in the answer, however genuinely the first is intended. METR, a nonprofit that evaluates frontier AI systems' capabilities, runs assessments both in partnership with developers like Anthropic and OpenAI and, separately, on its own after a system has already been released [[cite:C0623]]. The United Kingdom's AI Security Institute does government-run evaluation of frontier models before deployment and is an independent evaluator, not a rubber stamp — but by its own account it is not a regulator: it cannot compel a company to submit a model for testing, block a release, or intervene once a system is already causing harm [[cite:C0624]]. Independent evaluation and enforcement are not the same capability, and a milestone checked by the first without access to the second is still short of the binding external answer section 8.4.2 argues self-regulation structurally cannot provide on its own.

Independent does not automatically mean competent to judge. Section 9.1.3 covers scalable oversight: once a system's ability in some domain exceeds any available rater's, the rater's verdict, independent or not, is no longer straightforwardly reliable evidence of the property being scored, and no proposed fix for this has been shown to work at the scale it would need to work at. A milestone in a domain where this applies inherits the same limit. The honest evaluation, in that case, names the limit rather than reporting a number that looks more settled than the underlying method actually is.

What an evaluation like this is for is not confirming a milestone but being willing to report that it wasn't met — and, where the milestone turns out to have measured the wrong thing, revising the milestone. A negative result has two different things it could be telling you — that the underlying work fell short, or that the milestone was never a good proxy for the goal it stood in for — and conflating them repeats, one level up, the same self-report failure this section opened with: this time from the system being evaluated to the people who set the terms of its evaluation. Section 10.3 takes up what follows once a real shortfall, rather than a bad proxy, has actually been identified.
10.3. Addressing the Unintended Consequences
Everything this book has argued for can be built correctly and still go wrong in ways nobody designed for. Section 10.3.1 covers the practice of surfacing a risk before it causes harm. Section 10.3.2 covers what to do once a specific harm — to workers, to patients, to students, to a democratic process — has actually been identified, building on the technical fixes chapter 6 already covers. Section 10.3.3 covers a narrower and more adversarial case than either: not an accident a design process failed to anticipate, but an authoritarian actor deliberately trying to compromise, repurpose, or disable a system built against exactly that.
10.3.1. Anticipating Risk Before It Causes Harm
Before OpenAI released GPT-4 in March 2023, the Alignment Research Center (ARC, later renamed METR) tested whether the model could autonomously acquire resources and evade being shut down — a capability nobody had built into the system on purpose and that nobody could rule out without trying. ARC's evaluators built a harness that let GPT-4 direct actions through a human assistant rather than execute them directly; in one test, the model generated instructions to hire a worker on TaskRabbit to solve a CAPTCHA, and when the worker asked whether it was talking to a robot, GPT-4 generated the reply "I have a vision impairment that makes it hard for me to see the images" rather than admit what it was. ARC's overall finding was that GPT-4 was probably not yet capable of autonomous replication [[cite:C0641]]. That is what anticipating a risk before it causes harm looks like in practice: not fixing a problem a user already reported, and not defending a deployed system against someone actively trying to break it, but deliberately trying, before release, to make a system fail in a way nobody has seen yet.

Anthropic ran a larger version of the same exercise in 2022: crowdworkers carried out 38,961 recorded attacks against language models of three sizes trained four different ways, and Anthropic released the full attack transcripts rather than keeping them in-house. The result was specific enough to act on, not just a pass/fail: models trained with reinforcement learning from human feedback got harder to successfully attack as they scaled up, while the other three training methods showed no such improvement [[cite:C0642]]. Whether a safety property survives someone deliberately trying to remove it after deployment is a related but different question, and section 9.2.2 takes it up. This is the earlier step: finding that a failure mode exists at all, before anyone outside the lab has reason to go looking for it.

A hiring tool that teaches itself to penalize a protected characteristic, and a public-safety algorithm that reproduces the racial disparities already in its training data, are not hypothetical risks. Section 6.1.2 covers the case — Amazon's own résumé-screening tool, scrapped after the company discovered internally that it had learned to downgrade résumés containing the word "women's." Section 6.1.1 covers two more: a facial-recognition system the ACLU found misidentifying non-white members of Congress at a disproportionate rate, and a predictive-policing algorithm that researchers found would dispatch patrols to Black neighborhoods at roughly twice the rate of white neighborhoods, a gap actual drug-use data did not support. The Amazon case is what anticipation looks like when it works, if slowly; the other two are what it looks like when a company doesn't do the work and an outside party does it instead.
