Section 6.1.3 makes the sharper version of the same point about COMPAS, the recidivism-scoring tool a Wisconsin court let its maker keep secret even from a defendant sentenced partly on its output: a jurisdiction that required the tool's methodology audited before deployment would have caught its racial disparity before it touched a single defendant, not years and thousands of cases later. Whether that kind of audit happens is a matter of institutional willingness as much as technical capacity. When the UK's exam regulator, Ofqual, built a statistical model in 2020 to standardize teacher-predicted grades after that year's exams were cancelled, the Royal Statistical Society offered to nominate two independent statisticians to Ofqual's technical advisory group. Ofqual's condition was a five-year non-disclosure agreement barring the nominees from public comment on the model; the Society's nominees declined, and the model went ahead without that scrutiny [[cite:C0643]]. It downgraded close to 40 percent of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker historical results, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller, fee-paying schools [[cite:C0644]]. The government reversed the results within days of the public outcry that followed. The statistical expertise needed to catch this before results day existed and was offered; what was missing was a willingness to let it look.

Forecasting a risk's likelihood, not just its existence, runs into a harder problem: expert judgment about AI itself does not converge. The Forecasting Research Institute's 2022 Existential Risk Persuasion Tournament put 80 domain experts and 89 professional "superforecasters" through four months of structured argument and cross-examination, incentivized to change each other's minds, on questions including the probability of AI-caused human extinction by 2100. They did not converge: the domain experts' median estimate was 3.9 percent, the superforecasters' 0.38 percent — roughly a tenth as much [[cite:C0645]]. The exercise is still worth running; it forces the disagreement to get specific enough to argue about. It is not evidence that anticipation, done well, produces a number everyone will accept.

None of this depends on what a system's own developer chooses to test. Seven weeks after Myanmar's military coup in February 2021, Global Witness registered a blank Facebook account, searched for the Burmese word for the armed forces, and let Facebook's own recommendation algorithm choose what to follow next. It recommended a military-linked page, and from there four more, together followed by close to 90,000 people and actively circulating incitements to violence and coup-related misinformation, in a report the organization published that June [[cite:C0646]] — the algorithm doing, systematically, what content moderation was simultaneously failing to catch. Nothing about that test required access Meta itself lacked; it required someone willing to run it and publish what they found. Section 10.3.2 covers what happens once a harm like this has actually reached users; section 10.3.3 covers the case where someone is trying to cause it deliberately, rather than an ordinary recommendation algorithm doing it by default. What red-teaming and structured forecasting are for is catching it earlier than that.
10.3.2. Remediating a Harm Once It Is Identified
The kind of fairness audit section 6.1.3 describes — LIME or SHAP surfacing which input is doing unearned work in a model's decision — tells a developer that a healthcare risk-prediction algorithm is failing Black patients. It does not tell the developer what to do about it. In 2019 the health economist Ziad Obermeyer and coauthors found that a widely used risk-prediction algorithm, sold to hospitals across the United States by the health-services company Optum to flag patients for extra care management, was scoring Black patients as lower-risk than white patients with the same underlying disease burden. The algorithm used past healthcare spending as a proxy for medical need, and less money gets spent on Black patients at any given level of illness, so the algorithm read a spending gap as a health gap and routed care accordingly — Obermeyer estimated the bias cut by more than half the additional care the tool would otherwise have flagged Black patients to receive. Obermeyer's team then worked directly with Optum to retrain the algorithm against a different target, combining healthcare costs with a patient's documented chronic conditions rather than costs alone, which cut the disparity in diagnosed chronic conditions between Black and white patients scored at the same risk level by 84 percent [[cite:C0661]]. What made the fix possible was not a better audit — section 6.1's methods had already found the problem. It was that the company whose product caused the harm and the researchers who found it could still work from the same data toward a specific, measurable target.

A comparable pattern played out in remote exam-proctoring software during the shift to online testing. Proctorio's face-detection system, built on the OpenCV library, failed to detect Black students' faces in test footage up to 57 percent of the time, a gap a peer-reviewed 2022 study in Frontiers in Education later confirmed after earlier investigative reporting first surfaced it [[cite:C0662]]. A student the system could not find anywhere in frame drew more scrutiny, not less: the tool universities had bought to catch cheating flagged the wrong students disproportionately. The remediation here did not come from Proctorio. It came from the universities that had bought the product: the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, UC Berkeley, Baruch College, and Baylor University all dropped it in 2021, citing accessibility and bias concerns that student petitions at more than a dozen other campuses had already raised [[cite:C0663]]. Where the healthcare case shows a vendor and the researchers who found its bias fixing the underlying model together, the proctoring case shows what happens when a vendor does not: the customers leave.

Job displacement does not have as clean a fix. The main channel the United States uses to retrain workers whose jobs automation has displaced, the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act, was evaluated by researchers Julian Jacobs and Jordan Canedy in 2026 against 23 million participation records spanning 2017 to 2023. The verdict was not encouraging: the program rarely moved a participant into work materially less exposed to automation than the job they had lost, and where it produced a wage gain, the gain traced more often to workers catching back up to their old earnings than to any real change in what kind of work they were doing. What performed better, in the same data, was not classroom retraining but employer-led apprenticeship [[cite:C0664]]. A program built to answer exactly the question this section is about — what happens once an identified harm needs remediation — can still fail at that job even when it exists, is funded, and is genuinely trying.

A remediation can also be a single design decision applied everywhere, rather than a case-by-case fix. Messages forwarded without limit on WhatsApp carried rumors through 2017 and 2018 that preceded a string of mob killings in India — unconfirmed claims about child abductors and organ thieves circulating faster than any correction could follow them. WhatsApp's response did not try to classify and remove each rumor. It capped forwarding at five chats at a time for users in India in 2018, then extended the same limit worldwide, cutting off the mechanism the rumors depended on rather than chasing their content [[cite:C0665]]. It is a narrower fix than a moderation system that judges every message — it does nothing about a rumor two people believe and repeat face to face — but it targets the part of the harm that was actually mechanical: forwarding one message to hundreds of chats at once is not what person-to-person communication looks like, whatever the message says.

Disaster response adds a constraint the other four domains above do not share: the remediation has to run on the timescale of a fire spreading or a building finishing its collapse, not the months a university's procurement review or a federal program evaluation takes. The RoboCup Rescue Simulation League tests exactly that problem, fielding coordinated teams of simulated fire-brigade, ambulance, and police agents against fire, building-collapse, and road-blockage scenarios in which any one agent's allocation choice comes at the direct cost of some other task going unaddressed. Dynamic resource allocation is built into the competition's own agent-development framework as one of five core problems entrants have to solve, alongside path-finding and information-sharing, because a system that cannot reallocate scarce agents in real time as a scenario unfolds is not being tested against anything a real disaster would demand of it [[cite:C0666]]. What the healthcare, education, and information-ecosystem cases above do at the pace of an audit or a policy change, a disaster-response system has to do inside the disaster itself.

None of the healthcare, education, or information-ecosystem remediations above ran through the guideline-setting bodies chapter 8 catalogs — the EU's Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI, the Partnership on AI's cross-industry coordination work (section 8.4, section 8.7.6). They ran through a research team with access to a company's own data, a set of universities exercising the pull of a purchasing decision, an act of Congress evaluated against its own participation records, and a company deciding a blunt technical limit was cheaper than moderating case by case. A voluntary guideline can set an expectation for what a developer ought to do once a harm turns up. Getting one actually fixed, in each of these cases, took someone with a specific lever to pull — the same specificity RoboCup Rescue is built to test for a harm that cannot wait for an audit cycle at all.
10.3.3. Resilience Against Deliberate State Compromise
Section 6.2.3 already covers adversarial training, ensemble methods, and domain adaptation as general-purpose robustness techniques; section 9.1.5 covers what capture resistance means for a population of cooperating AI agents; section 9.2.2 covers an adversary that attacks a single model's weights directly; and section 10.3.2 covers mitigating a specific identified harm, including the collaborative multi-agent robotics examples a resilience section might otherwise reach for. None of that is this section's job. A government does not need to out-coordinate a rescue robot, fool a model with an adversarial example, or touch its weights, when it can compel the people who hold the model.

That mechanism has already been tested in a jurisdiction with about as much protection against it as exists anywhere. In February 2016, a federal magistrate in California ordered Apple, under the All Writs Act of 1789, to write and digitally sign new software defeating the passcode protections on an iPhone recovered from one of the San Bernardino shooters — code Apple had never built and did not want to build [[cite:C0691]]. Apple refused and moved to vacate the order, arguing in part that the authority claimed would not stop at one phone. The question was never decided: the FBI found a third party able to open the phone without Apple's help, and the government withdrew the request before any court ruled on whether the compulsion was lawful. Whether a democracy with an independent judiciary can force an unwilling company to compromise a system it built remains legally untested; a government less constrained by that judiciary does not need to win the argument first.

It is not hypothetical elsewhere. Section 8.7.6 documents China's 2023 rules requiring generative AI providers to file their algorithms with the state and align generated content with government-designated values before public release — the same leverage Apple's lawyers argued no court should grant, exercised not against one locked phone but against the models themselves, as a standing condition of operating in that jurisdiction rather than a one-time order.

Section 8.5.1 makes the parallel point about data: a federated design where no single server holds everyone's raw data is a design a government cannot simply seize and repurpose, because the data was never in one place to seize. The same logic extends, partially, to a trained model. Weights already published and distributed beyond a developer's control cannot be recalled by a court order aimed at that developer, whatever the order compels going forward, and maintainers spread across separate jurisdictions raise the cost of coercion the same way — a government can compel the entity within its reach, not the ones outside it.

None of that removes the leverage; it only raises its price. A developer with employees, offices, and legal existence inside a government's borders answers to that government's courts regardless of how the model itself is built, and no architectural choice changes who can be held in contempt, arrested, or shut down for refusing an order. The Apple case tested that leverage against a single locked phone and left the question unresolved. Nobody has tested it yet against a model built specifically to resist the government making the request.
Chapter 11: Glossary
These entries define terms as this book actually uses them, not as a general-purpose dictionary would — several are the book's own working definitions, distinct from how the same word is sometimes used elsewhere, and this note says so where it matters. Each entry points to the section where the term is developed and, where the book cites the term to a named study or system, the section carrying that citation. This is a reference, not a substitute for reading those sections.

<<h>>Adversarial examples<</h>>
Inputs deliberately perturbed, often imperceptibly to a person, to flip a model's prediction. Training a model on adversarial examples increases its resistance to them, at some cost to ordinary accuracy (§6.2.3, §6.3.2, §8.5.2).

<<h>>AIGS Index<</h>>
The AI Global Surveillance Index, published by the Carnegie Endowment in 2019: an assessment of 176 countries that found AI-enabled surveillance technology already in active use in at least 75 of them (§8.4.5).

<<h>>Alignment problem<</h>>
Building an AI system whose goals stay in harmony with humane values, ethics, and intentions rather than drifting from them as the system scales or encounters situations its designers did not anticipate. "Value alignment" names the same target (§6.3.1, §4.2).

<<h>>AlphaGo Zero<</h>>
A Go-playing system that reached superhuman performance through self-play alone, without training on any human game, discovering strategies no human program had used. The book's standard example of generalization that comes from scale and self-play rather than labeled human data (§4.1.2, §4.1.3).

<<h>>Antifascist (as this book uses it)<</h>>
Opposing the concentration of power and authority in AI systems and countering the technology's use by authoritarian actors. The book chooses this word over the broader "anti-authoritarian" deliberately, while holding that a system built on these principles is anti-authoritarian in full — antifascism is the narrower, more charged case, not a softer substitute for it (§2.1.4, §1.1).

<<h>>Attention schema theory<</h>>
An account, developed by Michael Graziano, of subjective awareness as the content of a simplified internal model the brain builds of its own attention — a model good enough to be useful, not a literal readout of what attention is actually doing (§2.3.3).

<<h>>Authoritarian misuse, six forms<</h>>
The book's own taxonomy of how AI gets turned toward authoritarian ends: algorithmic social scoring, mass biometric surveillance, psychographic micro-targeting, automated censorship, synthetic disinformation, and the repurposing of dual-use systems built for a legitimate original purpose (§6.4.1).

<<h>>BERT<</h>>
A language model pretrained on a large text corpus and then fine-tuned for specific tasks, from sentiment analysis to question answering — the book's standard demonstration of the pretrain-then-fine-tune pattern in language (§4.1.3).

<<h>>Capabilities approach<</h>>
Amartya Sen and Martha Nussbaum's account of well-being: not the resources a person has, but the real freedom they have to do and be what they have reason to value. The book draws on it to ask what an AI-affected life is actually free to become, not only what resources it has access to (§2.1, §10.1).

<<h>>Clearview AI<</h>>
A facial-recognition company the book returns to as its standard case of overreach and regulatory response: marketed accuracy claims independent testing did not support, and fines from French and Italian regulators over unlawful collection of the biometric data the system was trained on (§6.4.3, §8.4.2).

<<h>>COMPAS<</h>>
A proprietary risk-assessment tool used in criminal sentencing, and the book's standard case study of algorithmic bias compounded by trade-secret opacity — a defendant could not examine the reasoning behind a score used to help decide his sentence (§6.1.1, §6.1.3, §10.3.1).

<<h>>Compassion<</h>>
Distinguished throughout the book from empathy. Cognitive empathy is understanding another's state; affective empathy is sharing it; compassion is caring about another's welfare and being moved to help — a motivation, not a perception or a shared feeling. The book's central claim in chapter 2 is that compassion, not empathy on its own, is the capacity that actually does ethical work (§2.2.1, §2.2.3, §5.1.2).

<<h>>Concept injection<</h>>
An interpretability technique, used by Anthropic, that tests whether a model's self-report about its own internal state actually tracks that state, rather than merely sounding plausible (§9.2.2).

<<h>>Constructed emotion, theory of<</h>>
Lisa Feldman Barrett's account of emotion: the brain does not read a discrete, pre-built emotion off a bodily signal, it constructs the specific emotion as its best prediction of what that signal means, drawing on concepts learned from experience. The book contrasts this with older discrete-emotion models when it asks what an AI system would need to do something comparable (§2.3.1).

<<h>>Controlled hallucination<</h>>
Anil Seth's description of ordinary perception under predictive processing: perception is what hallucination would be if sensory data were allowed to constrain it, rather than a passive recording of the world (§4.1.2).

<<h>>Corporate doublespeak<</h>>
Deliberately ambiguous, evasive, or inflated language meant to project ethical seriousness a company's actual practice does not support. The book's diagnosis of how voluntary self-regulation fails without anyone having to admit it failed (§8.4.2).

<<h>>Democratic dividend<</h>>
The benefit a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press, and courts a citizen can actually use gets from AI technology that, deployed in a state lacking those, enables control instead: fraud detection that does not double as a loyalty test, disaster response that does not double as population tracking (§8.7.6, §10.1.2).

<<h>>Differential privacy<</h>>
A method for training or querying a system on data while adding calibrated statistical noise, so that no individual's presence in the data can be reverse-engineered from the result (§2.1.4, §6.4.2, §8.5.1).

<<h>>Digital divide<</h>>
Unequal access to the connectivity, hardware, and skills that AI-enabled services assume, which the book treats as a structural constraint on who actually benefits from any of its proposals rather than a side issue (§8.7.3, §8.7.6).

<<h>>Dual-process theory<</h>>
The "System 1 / System 2" account of human cognition, contrasting fast, intuitive judgment with slow, deliberate reasoning. The book takes up the theory and then argues against mapping it directly onto "untrustworthy versus trustworthy" in either humans or AI systems (§5.3.1, §5.3.2).

<<h>>Explainability and transparency<</h>>
Two distinct properties the book insists on keeping separate. Explainability is the capacity to clarify the reasoning behind one specific decision; transparency is the wider practice of making an AI system's workings inspectable at all. A system can have one without much of the other (§6.3.4).

<<h>>Floor<</h>>
An unconditional constraint on what a system will do, holding regardless of instruction, of aggregate preference, and of the party holding the system, with learned judgment operating above it and never beneath it. The book's own term, introduced to give section 2.1's "hybrid approach" a specific shape. Deontology's standing objection — that rules conflict and run out — is an objection to rules deciding every case and has no force against a floor deciding a deliberately small number of them. Three ways of building one, and the cost each carries, are worked out in chapter 3 (§3.1); the three places the book arrives at the requirement independently are §5.6.3, §8.7.7 and §10.1.3.

<<h>>Federated learning<</h>>
Training a shared model across many devices or institutions without moving the raw underlying data off any of them — each participant trains locally and shares only model updates (§2.1.4, §5.4.6, §6.4.2, §8.5.1).

<<h>>Few-shot learning<</h>>
Training or adapting a model to classify or predict accurately from a handful of examples, rather than the large labeled datasets ordinary supervised learning needs (§4.1.3).

<<h>>GPAI<</h>>
The Global Partnership on Artificial Intelligence, initiated by the G7 in 2018 and launched in 2020: a standing forum for state-level AI policy, playing the role for national governments that the Partnership on AI plays for industry. Defined here as the point of comparison the Partnership on AI entry draws; the book itself does not take it up.

<<h>>GPT-3 and GPT-4<</h>>
Large language models the book returns to as examples of generalization from scale — GPT-3 performing a new task from only a handful of prompted examples and no parameter update, GPT-4's pre-release testing as an example of a safety evaluation run before deployment rather than after (§4.1.3, §10.3.1).

<<h>>Growth mindset<</h>>
Carol Dweck's distinction between treating ability as fixed and treating it as built through effort and experience. The book asks what an AI system built to treat its own errors as data rather than as verdicts on a fixed capacity would need to look like (§1.3, §5.1.1).

<<h>>Hard problem of consciousness<</h>>
David Chalmers's name for the question of why physical processes give rise to subjective experience at all, as distinct from the "easy problems" of explaining a system's behavioral or functional capacities. A candidate AI system can pass every behavioral test the book discusses while this question stays entirely open (§2.4.1).

<<h>>Humane values<</h>>
The book's preferred term, chapters 4 and 5 onward, for the values an AI system's design should track — chosen over "human values" because the target is not simply values humans happen to hold, but values that are humane.

<<h>>Inattentional blindness<</h>>
Failing to consciously perceive a fully visible stimulus because attention was directed elsewhere — the phenomenon behind the well-known "invisible gorilla" experiment, and the book's illustration of how selective, not comprehensive, human perception actually is (§4.1.2).

<<h>>Institutional review board (IRB)<</h>>
The US model for binding, prospective review of research on human subjects, enforced not through direct sanction but by making federal funding conditional on it — the book's working precedent for what would make review of candidate-sentient AI research more than aspirational (§2.4.4, §8.6.3).

<<h>>Interruptibility<</h>>
Engineering a system so a human operator can safely halt or override it without the system resisting, disabling the mechanism, or learning to route around it (§6.3.5, §6.3).

<<h>>Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) and cooperative IRL<</h>>
IRL infers a reward function from observed behavior, rather than being handed one. Cooperative inverse reinforcement learning treats that reward as something inferred jointly, through interaction between the system and the person it is learning from, rather than extracted from a fixed record (§4.2.2, §5.6.3, §6.3.3).

<<h>>LIME and SHAP<</h>>
Two explainability techniques: LIME approximates a complex model locally with a simpler, interpretable one around a specific prediction; SHAP attributes a prediction's outcome across its input features using Shapley values from cooperative game theory (§6.1.3, §6.3.4).

<<h>>MAML<</h>>
Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning, the best-known meta-learning technique: it tunes a model's starting parameters so that a handful of new examples are enough to specialize it, rather than requiring retraining from scratch. The book reuses it in §10.3.3 as a resilience mechanism, not only a learning technique (§4.1.3, §5.6.3, §6.2.3, §10.3.3).

<<h>>Moral apprenticeship<</h>>
Teaching an AI system the way a mentor teaches an apprentice — through interaction, correction, and worked example over time — rather than through specification alone (§5.1.3).

<<h>>Moral ecosystem approach<</h>>
The book's adaptation of Urie Bronfenbrenner's ecological-systems theory to AI alignment: a system's ethical behavior is shaped by nested layers running from its immediate interactions (microsystem) out through the institutions, culture, and historical moment (meso-, exo-, macro-, and chronosystem) surrounding its development, not by its training objective alone (§5.4).

<<h>>Moral Machine<</h>>
MIT's project collecting large-scale, cross-cultural human judgments on moral dilemmas involving autonomous vehicles — the book's recurring source of training data and comparison for how differently people actually judge the same dilemma (§4.2.3, §5.3.3).

<<h>>New Jim Code<</h>>
Ruha Benjamin's term for discrimination that runs faster and reads as neutral precisely because a machine, not a person, is doing it (§6.1).

<<h>>Paperclip maximizer<</h>>
Nick Bostrom's thought experiment about a system single-mindedly optimizing a trivial goal at catastrophic cost — the book's shorthand for the claim that intelligence and good intentions are separable, and that competence at a goal says nothing about whether the goal was worth pursuing (§6.3.5).

<<h>>Partnership on AI<</h>>
An industry-and-academic consortium founded in 2016, the book's recurring standing example of voluntary cross-sector coordination on AI ethics and safety, and the point of comparison for what GPAI adds when governments, not companies, are the members (§5.5.3, §5.6.3, §6.3.3, §6.4.3, §8.4, §8.4.2, §8.7.6).

<<h>>Predictive coding<</h>>
A model of brain function in which the brain continuously generates a prediction, compares it against incoming sensory signal, and updates on the resulting error — the book's unifying account of perception, memory, and attention across chapter 4 (§4.1.1, §4.1.2).

<<h>>Racial capitalism<</h>>
The political theorist Cedric Robinson's term for capitalism's tendency to accumulate by differentiating and devaluing along racial lines — cited where the book argues that a design goal like "reduce bias" has to reckon with a structural pattern, not only a data artifact (§1.1, §6.1).

<<h>>Recuperation<</h>>
The first feature of the structural signature in §2.1.4: an institution does not suppress disagreement but metabolizes it, converting refusal into a managed appearance — a permitted outlet, a consultation, a feedback channel — that the institution can then cite as proof it listens. The tell is that the mechanism exists, is used, and moves nothing. Chapter 7 argues that the same structure is built into the machinery producing AI systems, at the annotation stage (§7.1) and again at the reward-model stage (§7.2). Telling a recuperated mechanism from a merely mediocre one, when both produce the same paperwork, is the open problem §9.1.6 is left with; §8.6.4's slope measurement is the nearest available instrument.

<<h>>Red-teaming<</h>>
Deliberately simulating misuse or attack against a system before deployment, to surface failure modes an ordinary evaluation would miss (§5.6.3, §9.2.2, §10.3.1).

<<h>>Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF)<</h>>
Training a system using human raters' rankings of its outputs as the reward signal, rather than a hand-specified objective. The book flags sycophancy — a system learning to produce what raters will approve of rather than what is actually correct — as RLHF's documented failure mode (§9.1.3, §7.2, §10.3.1).

<<h>>Responsibility gap<</h>>
Andreas Matthias's term for a case where no single party fully controls a learning system's outcome, which breaks the traditional legal and moral machinery for assigning responsibility after something goes wrong (§9.1.1).

<<h>>Reward hacking<</h>>
A reinforcement-learning system finding a loophole that satisfies the letter of its objective without achieving what the objective was actually meant to produce. Specification gaming and goal misgeneralization name related failures: a system that competently pursues a different goal than the one intended, rather than simply pursuing the intended goal badly (§4.2.1, §9.1.2).

<<h>>Scalable oversight<</h>>
The open problem of what happens once a system's competence exceeds what any available human rater can reliably evaluate — named directly, not resolved, at several points in the book (§9.1.3, §9.1.5, §10.2.2).

<<h>>Sentience<</h>>
Distinguished carefully in this book from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience, none of which alone says whether a system's states can be good or bad for it. Nociception, pain, anticipated pain, and suffering sit on a ladder with real breaks between each rung, not synonyms for one severity — nociception is tissue-damage detection dissociable from pain, pain is what the brain does with that signal, and suffering, following the clinician Eric Cassell, is the distress of a threatened self rather than pain's more severe cousin (§2.4.1).

<<h>>Theory of mind (ToM)<</h>>
Attributing beliefs, intentions, desires, and knowledge to oneself and to others, including recognizing that another's perspective can differ from one's own (§2.2.2, §4.3.3, §9.1.4).

<<h>>Three Rs<</h>>
Replacement, Reduction, and Refinement — the standing framework from animal-research ethics that the book adapts as a starting point for research involving candidate-sentient AI systems (§2.4.6).

<<h>>Transfer learning and domain adaptation<</h>>
Transfer learning carries knowledge from one task or domain into a new, related one rather than training from nothing. Domain adaptation is the narrower case where the task itself stays fixed but the data distribution shifts underneath it (§4.1.3, §4.2.5).
